#6850. Maple Bourbon Chicken With Brussels Sprouts

If you, like most people, hear the term “bourbon chicken” and have no idea what it is, let me call it by its more common name: toothpick chicken. Yes, the suspiciously delicious chicken shelled out at mall food courts on toothpicks is most likely bourbon chicken. Popular at American Chinese or Cajun-style Chinese restaurants, the sticky-sweet chicken is the utter definition of delicious, luring weary shoppers into food courts to find refuge from the bubblegum pink sweatpants and ripped denim. This version takes cues from the classic but swaps in some autumnal ingredients to make it fit for the season. Charred brussels sprouts leaves and pecans add bulk, while maple syrup and apple cider vinegar give it that delightful tart-sweet flavor we know and love. Serve it over rice or noodles or all by its lonesome for a hearty dinner that will take you back to the '90s.

More chicken dinner winners here: Sheet-Pan Hot Honey Chicken and Peaches; Basil Chicken Stir-Fry; and Ginger-Garlic Chicken and Vegetable Stir-Fry.

Whisk one 1½" piece ginger, peeled, finely grated, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, ⅓ cup bourbon, ⅓ cup pure maple syrup, ¼ cup apple cider vinegar, ¼ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes, 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, ¼ cup low-sodium soy sauce, and ½ cup water in a small bowl to combine. Set sauce aside.

Toss 1½ lb. skinless, boneless chicken breasts, halved crosswise, thinly sliced against the grain, remaining 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, and remaining 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce in a medium bowl until chicken is coated; set aside.

Heat 2 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large skillet over medium-high. Add 1 lb. brussels sprouts, halved through stems, leaves separated, and cook, stirring very occasionally, until just tender and blackened in spots, about 5 minutes. Transfer to another medium bowl.

Add more oil if pan looks dry and, working in batches if needed, arrange reserved chicken in a single layer and cook, turning occasionally, until golden brown and cooked through, about 5 minutes. Transfer to bowl with brussels sprouts.

Add reserved sauce to same pan and bring to a simmer. Cook, stirring constantly, until thickened, about 1 minute. Remove pan from heat and return brussels sprouts and chicken to pan; top with 1 cup salted roasted pecans, coarsely chopped. Toss to coat in sauce. Serve chicken and brussels sprouts with steamed white rice if desired.

#6849. Inside Well Beyond Breast Cancer’s Fundraiser to Give Survivors Access to Rehabilitation

Many don’t realize that breast cancer care doesn’t end with treatment.

Under violet lights, hanging florals, and rattan lanterns, guests at Well Beyond Breast Cancer’s third annual fundraising event mingled, cried happy tears, and came together in New York City to support survivors. Well Beyond Breast Cancer is a nonprofit organization that helps survivors access rehabilitation services after breast cancer treatment. Founder Amanda Grilli, an oncology physical therapist, decided to start the organization just three years ago after moving to the United States from Canada and recognizing a major gap in care.

“I was shocked by the differences in the health care system between the two countries,” Grilli told ELLE. “I quickly realized that the majority of individuals who are affected by breast cancer don’t have access to a therapist like myself because they’re experiencing so many barriers, whether that’s financial toxicity, insurance gaps, or a fragmented health care system. [Many] people aren’t able to access this care on their own.”

For breast cancer survivors, care continues even after surgery, chemotherapy, or radiation end. But rehabilitation is a major piece of breast cancer recovery that often isn’t considered. Grilli says, “So many people experience lingering pain, or they have difficulty lifting their arms. What these therapists are doing is helping people overcome these side effects so that they can get back to living well.”

Guests had the opportunity to hear from one of the organization’s beneficiaries, Allison Berg, an independent documentary filmmaker who was diagnosed with breast cancer in 2025. Even after treatment, her recovery continued. Berg described shooting pain through her arms and fingertips, tightness every time she reached for anything, and tender scars. “My body is part of my equipment,” she shared. “I was having trouble just sitting at a laptop and typing. How was I ever going to carry gear and hold a camera again?” Well Beyond Breast Cancer covered 12 one-hour rehabilitation sessions for Berg, and in September, she was able to shoot for a new film.

Prior to the event yesterday, Well Beyond Breast Cancer had funded 250 breast cancer rehab appointments and allocated more than $50,000 to direct patient care. Last night, at ILA Penthouse in Manhattan, guests were able to participate in a live auction, which is still ongoing and has already raised thousands of dollars. Before they left, guests were also invited to share a wish with the “Keeper of Wishes,” a figurehead with “hopes” pinned to her dress, creating a growing display and fountain of hope that continued throughout the night.

Katie Berohn is ELLE’s beauty editor. Previously, she held the same title at Who What Wear, where she was promoted from associate beauty editor. She’s written for publications like The Cut, Cosmopolitan, Good Housekeeping, and Mashable. Her interests include fragrance, vintage shopping, hot yoga, food, travel, music, books, and attempting to make every NYT Cooking recipe. She’s on the endless hunt to find the perfect shade of red lipstick.

#6844. Steak Bites With Spinach Couscous

A traditional steak dinner can feel like too much for a weeknight: too expensive, too likely to trip the smoke alarm, too fussy to cook to a perfect medium-rare. But cut a steak into bite-size pieces and suddenly it transforms into a quick-cooking protein that’s no more demanding than chicken breast or tofu. Rub the steak with the spices and make the quick no-recipe yogurt sauce a few hours ahead if it’s helpful with time management. To account for the extra liquid thrown off by the spinach as it cooks, the water-to-couscous ratio in this recipe differs from that often found on the back of the bag (or box) so measure accordingly.

More quick dinners this way: Spicy Chicken Lettuce Wraps; Gochujang Chicken Stir-Fry; and Easy Fish Tacos With Spicy Sauce.

Place 1–1¼ lb. boneless New York strip steak (1–1½" thick), cut into 1" pieces, 1 Tbsp. dried oregano, 2 tsp. onion powder, 2 tsp. sugar, and 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl; season generously with freshly ground pepper and toss to coat. Let sit at least 10 minutes or cover and chill up to 8 hours (no need to bring to room temperature before cooking if chilling).

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or medium Dutch oven with a tight-fitting lid over medium. Add 1 bunch scallions, thinly sliced, ½ cup finely chopped dill, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; cook, stirring occasionally, until scallions are softened, about 5 minutes. Add 10 oz. baby spinach in 2–3 handfuls, stirring and letting wilt slightly after each addition before adding more, 5–7 minutes total. Pour in 1 cup water and bring to a boil; season with more salt if needed. Add 1 cup Moroccan couscous and stir to break up any lumps. Remove from heat; quickly cover with lid and let sit 10 minutes.

Meanwhile, heat 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet, preferably cast iron, over high. Arrange steak in a single layer; cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, 6–8 minutes. Reduce heat to medium; continue to cook, stirring often, until meat is just cooked through, about 1 minute more. Remove from heat. Add remaining 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; toss to combine. (Residual heat from pan will cook out any raw garlic flavor.)

Uncover couscous and fluff with a fork to separate grains. Spoon into a large shallow bowl or onto a platter. Top with steak. Cut 1 lemon into quarters and squeeze juice from 1 quarter over steak. Scatter thinly sliced cucumber and some dill sprigs on top; drizzle with oil.

Combine 1 cup plain whole-milk yogurt and a generous pinch of kosher salt in a small bowl; squeeze juice from 2 lemon quarters into yogurt. Stir to combine. If sauce is thick, stir in water, a spoonful at a time, to loosen.

Serve steak and couscous with yogurt sauce and remaining lemon quarter and more lemon wedges for squeezing over.

#6837. Mussels With Garlicky Miso Broth

Mussels deserve your attention on weeknights. An affordable and accessible protein, they also happen to cook in less time than it takes to boil water. As the bivalves steam, they release a briny liquid, further perfuming the buttery, sake-fortified miso broth. Load up each bowl with a few good ladles, slurping every drop with an assist from crusty bread or warm rice. And why not enjoy the rest of the bottle of sake alongside your meal?

The seafood counter at your supermarket should sell rinsed and debarred mussels, but it’s easy to do at home. Discard any with cracked shells. Gently tap open raw mussels on a counter to see if they slowly close; if they do, those are okay to use. Swish them in a water-filled salad spinner or colander fitted inside a bowl, draining and refilling until water runs clear (give any especially dirty ones a scrub with a sponge). Use a towel to pull off the fibrous beard and set on a towel-lined tray in the fridge until you’re ready to cook.

More seafood dinners this way: Avgolemono Seafood Stew; Spaghetti alle Vongole; and Gorgeous Green Shrimp.

Place 2 Tbsp. scallions from 8 scallions, thinly sliced on a diagonal, in a small bowl and set aside for serving.

Melt 6 Tbsp. unsalted butter in a large pot over medium heat. Add 8 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, and remaining scallions to pot and cook, stirring often, until softened, about 4 minutes. Add 2 Tbsp. white miso paste and ½ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes (if using) and cook until miso is broken up and incorporated into butter, about 20 seconds. Pour in 1 cup dry sake or dry white wine and stir to combine, then add 3–3½ lb. mussels, scrubbed, debearded. Cover pot and increase heat to medium-high. Cook, shaking pot occasionally, until mussels open, 5–7 minutes (discard any that do not open). Remove pot from heat.

Squeeze 1 lemon half over mussels. Cut remaining 1 lemon half into wedges for serving. Taste broth and season with kosher salt if needed.

Ladle mussels and broth among shallow bowls, then scatter reserved scallions over. Serve with country-style bread or steamed white rice if desired and lemon wedges for squeezing over.

#6832. Butternut Pasta With Sausage and Dates

Squash and sausage are a heroic autumnal pairing, especially in a weeknight-friendly pasta. Butternut is the easiest squash to source—not to mention easily found peeled and chopped (a blessing for those with more timid knife skills). But other winter squash like acorn or red kuri can stand in as desired. Whichever squash you select, a sharp, heavy chef’s knife is best for breaking down the chunky veg. But to make your life easier, microwave the squash for 1 to 2 minutes. It will slightly soften, making it simpler—and safer—to slice through.

Rigatoni’s ribbed surface and large holes help chunky squash sauce cling and bites of seared sausage and date wedge—opting for a nutty whole wheat variety will only add to this meal’s autumnal earthiness.

More cozy pastas this way: Balsamic Mushroom and Sausage Pasta; Creamy Red Curry Pasta; and Baked Feta Pasta With Green Sauce.

Cook 1 medium butternut squash (1½–2 lb.), peeled, halved, seeds removed, cut into 1" pieces (about 6 cups), in a large pot of boiling salted water until very tender, 12–14 minutes. Using a slotted spoon, transfer squash to a medium bowl. Reduce heat to low, cover pot, and leave on burner to keep warm.

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or a medium Dutch oven over medium-high. Add 1 lb. hot or sweet Italian sausage, casings removed, and break up into small pieces with a wooden spoon. Cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, about 3 minutes. Continue to cook, stirring occasionally, until browned all over and just cooked through, 3–4 minutes more. Transfer to a plate with a slotted spoon.

Reduce heat to medium-low. Cook 3½ oz. Medjool dates (about 6), pitted, sliced lengthwise ¾" thick, in same pan, turning halfway though, until deeply browned, 3–4 minutes. Transfer to plate with sausage; season with salt.

Add remaining 3 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil to pan. Add 6 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper, ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and reserved squash. Cook, scraping up browned bits and smashing squash with spoon to break up, until lightly browned and broken up into a chunky paste (pieces do not need to be even), about 5 minutes.

Meanwhile, return cooking liquid in reserved pot to a boil over high heat. Cook 12 oz. short tube pasta (such as rigatoni) until very al dente, about 3 minutes less than package directions (it will finish cooking in sauce). Drain, reserving 3 cups pasta cooking liquid.

Add 1 cup pasta cooking liquid to squash and break up squash with spoon until a saucy consistency. Add pasta, another 1 cup pasta cooking liquid, and reserved sausage and dates. Increase heat to medium-high; cook, adding more pasta cooking liquid as needed, until pasta is al dente and coated in sauce, 2–3 minutes. Remove from heat and stir in 1 Tbsp. sherry vinegar or balsamic vinegar. Taste pasta and add more vinegar, salt and/or pepper if needed.

#6817. Denis Villeneuve on Finishing ‘Dune,’ Casting James Bond, and Why ‘Reality Is Unbeatable’

The last days of the making of the Dune trilogy take place on an unassuming Hollywood side street in September, in a dark, mostly silent room. “We were about to have a quick look at reel one because they did some adjustments, and it’s without sound,” Denis Villeneuve says, apologetically. “So it’s a way to discover the film that is a bit brutal for you.”

Villeneuve, the director and cowriter of Dune, Dune: Part Two, and Dune: Part Three, is tall, slender, and bearded, with deep-set eyes. He has a disarming effect. Many directors of Villeneuve’s stature—Christopher Nolan or James Cameron—lead their productions with a kind of remote but dazzling competency: They can do everything better than you and are used to the deference that comes with that fact. Villeneuve’s charisma is closer and more mischievous: He speaks quietly and playfully, in Quebecois-accented English, and commands loyalty via the protective instinct he inspires in nearly everyone he meets. His method is emotional; his instructions are conveyed with sincerity and praise. When presented with the results he is seeking, he will let you know with an almost childlike enthusiasm: I deeply love it. Or: Wow.

Today, Villeneuve and his cinematographer, Linus Sandgren, are at the postproduction facility Company 3, where they have been the past weeks and months, color-timing—the process of adjusting and balancing a film’s many colors and contrasts—the 150 minutes of Dune: Part Three. For my sake, and because there is still work to be done on it, they have started, once again, at the beginning of the film. “So here we could have fucked up the Warner Brothers logo if we wanted,” Villeneuve says, gesturing at the screen at the studio’s iconic WB, “but we are nice guys.”

Then he leans forward: “No, but seriously, without joking, are they too rich, the colors? A little bit? Or is it the original to your eyes?” Sandgren—a calm Swede in Birkenstocks—and Matt Wallach, the film’s colorist, chime in to agree and to tone down the blue. “Their logo is a little cartoonish,” Villeneuve says, still staring at it. “It’s not their best work, I will say. But that feels better. Thank you, Matt.”

Villeneuve and his wife, Tanya Lapointe, who is a producer and second-unit director on Dune: Part Three, disagree on how long they have been working on Dune: Villeneuve tends to say nine years, and Lapointe says 10. (It’s 10.) Frank Herbert’s Dune books, which are notoriously complex and hard to adapt, have stymied directors as talented as David Lynch and Alejandro Jodorowsky. But Villeneuve seems to have found a way. The first two Dune films—Part One came out in 2021—have grossed over a billion dollars, been nominated for 15 Academy Awards, and helped make stars of Timothée Chalamet (who plays Atreides, the film’s complicated messiah figure and main character) and Zendaya (who plays Chani, a native Fremen warrior and the lover of Atreides) while still capturing the uniquely strange and surreal texture of Herbert’s novels. (Multiple people in the Villeneuve films imbibe what one character describes as “worm piss.”)

Right now Villeneuve is feeling the last decade acutely. “I’m at that stage of the process where all my body hurts,” he says. On Dune: Part Three alone, the production shot for nearly a hundred days, in three countries: Hungary, where the film’s main stages were; Iceland; and the Liwa Desert, in the United Arab Emirates, which doubled for the sand planet of Arrakis. They wrapped last November, and Villeneuve went directly into the film’s edit and postproduction process. “It’s a feeling of some kind of deep melancholia to finally come to the conclusion of this long journey,” he says. “Because it has been nine years of my life dreaming about Arrakis, about sandworms, about the adventures of Paul Atreides. I’m pretty emotional about that.”

In the history of Hollywood, there have been only a handful of directors who have had the ability to work, again and again, at the highest level of the art form while also connecting with audiences at the widest possible scale. Villeneuve is one of them. His signature films—2015’s cartel thriller Sicario, or 2016’s science fiction film Arrival, which was nominated for eight Academy Awards—are knotty and severe and not always easy to follow; they are also beautiful and seem to play well anywhere, whether on an airplane, in an HBO rerun, or at an IMAX theater. Villeneuve’s reward for all his success is his next project: the 26th James Bond film. “It’s a blessing,” Villeneuve says. “Because to leave Arrakis, I need some kind of strong rocket, something that will grab me and pull me away from the orbit of that planet. I’m leaving a childhood dream. I need something so powerful to pull me away from it. And Bond is the one that will do it.”

In the dark screening room, Wallach lets the first minutes of the final Dune film play. We watch soldiers on a dimly lit warship prepare themselves for battle. Sandgren and Villeneuve discuss trying to bring out one of the actor’s features more concretely. “I guess what we’re fighting is we could have lit him more,” Sandgren says, “but also what Denis really wants here is the realism and the sort of incidental light—like he just happens to sit in a shitty light here, and gets what he gets, which adds to the realism, which is nice. But you still want to see him. So it’s a balance.”

“What Linus says, in a very polite way, is that it’s my fault,” Villeneuve says, smiling, while Wallach finds a little more detail in the actor’s face.

“I deeply love it,” Villeneuve pronounces, when he is done. “Thank you. The scene is at the beginning, and becomes claustrophobic. In French, we say they are chair à canon.”

“Cannon food, we call it in Sweden,” Sandgren says.

Villeneuve watches his own film as it plays on. “Wow,” he says. “Fantastic. Fantastic. That’s beautiful. Nice work on the highlights here. Thank you, guys. Wow. Great work. It looks real now. Wow.” His voice is full of emotion as he turns to Sandgren: “It’s beautiful, my dear friend.”

In the Dune movies, you can feel Villeneuve’s desire to go deeper into the desert, to shed all the big, loud machinery of his own films, and to get to the places out near the horizon. This is one of the reasons his trilogy works where others have failed: Despite Villeneuve’s talent for spectacle—his Dune is full of menacing, chromed-out oblong shapes and eerily familiar war machines—his true gift is finding the uncannily quiet spots in the worlds he creates, the lonely places where his characters are tested against themselves.

“That was the main condition when I agreed to make these movies,” Villeneuve says, about the way all three Dune films were shot in the desert, on location. “I said that it was deeply important for me to shoot it in the real environment.” The Liwa Desert was so mind-bendingly hot while shooting Dune: Part Three that the director had to incorporate a midday break into the crew’s daily schedule. Robert Pattinson, who joined the cast for the third film, has talked about the set being so intensely warm that he basically disassociated for most of shooting. But being there is one of the main things Villeneuve is after. “I love reality,” he says. “I love to be in the environment. It’s like a pleasure that I give to myself to be in the crew with a camera in the desert. I feel that there’s all these shots that we did, the realism, that I think you cannot achieve otherwise. And you cannot feel the power of the desert if you’re not there. There are some shots you can cheat sometimes. But in general, reality is unbeatable.”

Villeneuve on the set of Dune: Part Three with Timothée Chalamet.

Villeneuve and Zendaya on location in the Liwa Desert, Abu Dhabi.

Villeneuve, 59, grew up in Gentilly, Quebec, a small town right next to a river. From that place he emerged with “a relationship with horizon and silence,” he says. “I was in contact since my birth with incredible gigantic skies, very dramatic, operatic skies. And colors as well. I grew up in a world that has a black-and-white winter, which has a tremendous, beautiful, precise palette of black and white and blue and grays.”

Villeneuve likes to point out that the two main structures in his town were a church and a power plant. “I grew up melancholic, a child that was dreaming big alone in his corner and fascinated by the engineers that were working in that nuclear power plant,” he says. “I was raised as a Catholic, and I was raised going to the church, and those chants and the passion of religion [are] all that mixed together with incredibly powerful grandmothers. I think that’s the background of Dune right there.”

Villeneuve was a reader, a dreamer. It was only as an adult, when Villeneuve became a documentary filmmaker, that he began to discover a larger world outside his own mind. “I think that there’s something about Paul Atreides in the way he’s introduced at the beginning, the way he is like a kid that has been protected in a small bubble all his life and suddenly finds himself confronted to a new reality where he has to consolidate his identity in another culture,” Villeneuve says. “I think it’s something that I, at the time when I was a kid, I really connected with. And early on when I became a young adult, I traveled a lot, a lot. I went a lot to Asia and was spending a lot of time there doing small documentaries and being in contact with so many perspectives, so many points of view on reality.”

Villeneuve made his first feature, August 32nd on Earth, in Canada in 1998. He would go on to make three more films in French, before making his first English-language film, Enemy, in 2013. “I consolidated my identity as a filmmaker,” Villeneuve says about the time before he came to Hollywood “through documentaries and movies with less commercial pressure where I was free to express myself and find my own voice.” Partially, he learned by failing. “I will say that I don’t see my first feature films as...they had some clumsiness in them. I was a beginner. I learned my craft. I made mistakes and I learned from those mistakes, a lot more than from my successes.”

Villeneuve, as a young filmmaker, was wary of Hollywood, even as he was pointed at it like an arrow. “Hollywood has always had that kind of dichotomy, to be a paradise for a filmmaker and hell at the same time,” he says. “Since the birth of Hollywood there has been a struggle between the art of cinema, the idea that you want to express yourself—and for that you need a lot of money, and somebody will write the check, and you have a responsibility. Since the birth of cinema, it has been like that.”

In 2012, Villeneuve found himself facing a rare opportunity: He had two films greenlit at more or less the same time. One was Prisoners—an in-demand Hollywood script about the abduction of two children and the vigilante efforts of one father to get them back. The other was a coproduction between Spain and Canada called Enemy, about a college professor who becomes obsessed with another man with whom he appears to be physically identical. Both were English-language films, which Villeneuve had not yet done. Both films starred Jake Gyllenhaal. But to Villeneuve, they were very different propositions: Enemy was a deeply personal film about identity and the unknown nature of the self. And Prisoners, which also starred Hugh Jackman and Viola Davis and which would be largely distributed by Warner Bros. Pictures, represented Villeneuve’s first true chance to make a real Hollywood film.

Villeneuve decided to make both. But he made Enemy first. “I did convince the studio, Alcon”—the producer of Prisoners—“to wait a few months for Prisoners because first I wanted to make a first film in English in a smaller environment. And the real reason is that I wanted to make sure that I will shoot something that will be so close to me that if ever I was smashed and destroyed by Hollywood, there will be something. If people say, ‘What’s that? Oh, Denis’s there. He’s flying over there.’ Because I did feel that I was afraid.”

I remember watching Enemy in a small screening room the year it premiered, in 2013 (the movie was distributed in the United States by a then upstart studio called A24), and being increasingly unsettled and haunted by the film. Sometimes, in quiet moments, the final shot of Enemy still comes to me, unbidden. Who is this guy? I wondered then.

Enemy and Prisoners were both stylish, morally ambiguous films that showed the extremes of human behavior without ever venturing into sadism or pure spectacle, and this style of filmmaking—brutal, unresolved, lush, and heartbreaking—would become Villeneuve’s signature as he made progressively bigger, and bigger-budget, films in Hollywood: 2015’s Sicario, 2016’s Arrival, 2017’s Blade Runner 2049. “I always did projects I [felt] ready to do,” Villeneuve says. “I was ready to do Dune after Blade Runner. I would have not been ready to do Dune after Prisoners. I slowly learned my craft one step at a time—my skills with actors and then to work with more and more background action and increase the technical difficulty slowly. I just felt secure. And when I started Dune, I knew I could not go in front of people and ask them to invest $200 million [until I was ready]. I need to feel deep inside me that I can do that.”

Villeneuve says he only recently stopped feeling like a tourist in Hollywood. “I think that feeling of having the right to be here in some ways came from other filmmakers, colleagues that wanted to reach out and share thoughts about cinema with me or some friendships that began and that I accepted,” he says. He’s coy about who exactly he’s talking about. But part of it, surely, has also been the acceptance of the films, which despite their increasing size and scale and sometimes exotic source material are consistently deeply personal.

“My work is all about me,” Villeneuve says, helplessly. “If you don’t like Dune, the movies, I’m 100 percent responsible because I made every single decision. I brought them to the screen, and they are very personal films. Even for me, it’s like—scope is not something that is contradictory with the ownership of a movie. These movies have been brought to life by me, and I say that without arrogance. I mean, I’m the one who brought them to life. And yeah: It’s my Dune.”

A few weeks later, Villeneuve and Lapointe, who is shorter but equally warm and charismatic, are at the North Hollywood warehouse of Legendary, the production company behind the Dune films. The warehouse is full of costumes from past productions: Pacific Rim, Crimson Peak, Godzilla. In the back, transported from another continent entirely, is one of the ornithopters—a helicopter-like vehicle—from Dune: Part Three. Soon this prop, which is the size of an actual helicopter, will join a bunch of others in an upcoming exhibition of work from the Dune films. But right now it is here, in a parking lot under the glaring Los Angeles sun. Its paint—an homage to the specific color of a 1981 Porsche—glows in the light. “The last time I saw this vehicle, it was in a deep desert,” Villeneuve says, looking on. “We had brought it to Abu Dhabi, and it was, I remember vividly, at the top of a cliff of sand. This thing I’ve seen a lot. It’s like an old friend.”

Later in the day, Villeneuve tells me he is set to approve a final handful of effects shots. And then Dune: Part Three, and the director’s work on the trilogy, he says, will cease. “I do feel that I have to stop,” he reflects. “I don’t think I should spend another minute on it. Because I’m still curious, I’m still alive, and I do it for artistic reasons, not because I see that I’m comfortable or I see that it’s going to be good business-wise or for my career. I think it’s good for me to stop artistically.”

As Villeneuve’s star has risen, he has increasingly had to think through the question of how to remain an artist at the very high tier of the business that he’s in. “In the past 25 years, there’s a tendency to try to find the perfect formula to make a film, and to try to reproduce, reproduce, and make sequels,” Villeneuve says. “I’m responsible. I just made three Dune movies. I’m about to make James Bond 26. So I’m a sinner. But the idea is that as an artist, I feel that there’s an urgency, and you feel it in the young filmmakers, which I deeply love—the idea to bring new stories, original stories to the screen. It’s very important. And that we have to try to, at the end of the day, we need to take risks.… I want to say that we, as humans, as artists, if we want to be better than AI, we have to be unpredictable.” Only humans, he says, “can step into the unknown and go deeper into the dream. That is the strength of a human being. And I think that no computers can do that.”

It’s the end of September, and the Paramount–Warner Bros. Discovery merger is about to close. “I think that consolidation, I mean, less players in the business is never a good idea,” Villeneuve says. “Those studios are historical studios. And I hope, I truly hope, that the people in power will have the wisdom to protect the legacy of both studios. There’s a limit to profit. I mean, if the only goal is to make money, it’s really sad. Those things are historical gems of American culture and they have a duty to—I hope and I pray that they understand the responsibility that they have and that they will take care of both of these entities.”

Villeneuve, for his part, is already turning his attention toward Bond—another character, and another world, he grew up fascinated with. “I adore James Bond,” he says. “I adore Sean Connery, the Sean Connery years, the verve of James Bond, his panache, his drive, his charisma, his unapologetic way of living the strength and what it says, good and bad, about masculinity. There’s something about James Bond that deeply inspires me.” He says the previous producers of the franchise approached him with the idea of directing the film a few years ago, right as he was beginning Dune: Part Three. Villeneuve says he hung up the phone and actually screamed out loud when they offered the franchise to him. Then he told them they’d have to wait. “I said to them, ‘Yeah, I’m up for it, but I have something before called Dune: Part Three. I’m shooting in two weeks.’ And the only way I can do things in life is to fully commit to something. So I said to them, ‘If you want to work with me, you will have to wait many months because I will be entirely focused on Part Three.’ And so that’s why I’m sorry for everybody, but it took more time for the casting because I’m busy.”

Now we know: Robert Pattinson is James Bond. Villeneuve’s decision, albeit in consultation with his producers and the studio. He is incredulous that I am even asking. “I am the director!” he says. And then softens a bit: “I’m doing cinema because I love to share the creative process,” he says, about the conversations that led to Pattinson. “Otherwise, I will do ice sculpture in my basement. I love the idea to share creativity. But when it comes to casting, it’s something that—I’m doing the casting. But, of course, it will need to be accepted and approved by a ton of people, including you, the pope, my mother, my brother, who has a strong opinion about who should be James Bond.… I think Donald Trump wants to be James Bond. Everybody talks about James Bond. So yes, I will choose James Bond with my friends, producers, and hopefully everybody will.…” He pauses. “You know what? At the end of the day, the truth is, no matter who I choose, some people will be excited and others will be disappointed.”

Now Villeneuve has a gleam in his eye, talking about what he was looking for in the part. “The first quality is that I need to believe that this man is a killer, because more than a spy, Bond is an assassin,” he says. “I need that kind of anger deep in the eye. It’s not everybody who has that. I need someone who has that kind of unapologetic panache, someone that when he comes into the room, everything starts to gravitate around him, that kind of strong charisma and someone that has a lot of sensuality as well and that people are attracted to and that someone who expressed some kind of pleasure, someone who has width, sometime who has dark humor, sometimes that has pleasure into being in deep shit, someone who is not afraid to put his hand in the mud. I need someone that is strong, muscular, someone that is a force. And someone, of course, who has a deep intelligence in the eyes, someone that is always ahead of us.”

He is grinning. You can feel Lapointe, in the nicest possible way, willing him to say less. But Villeneuve, as is typical, is swept away. “Every time I’m thinking about James Bond,” he says, “there’s an electricity that goes into my vein that is the electricity of pleasure. It’s like: For me to do that is just a pure cinematic pleasure. Incredible pressure, stress, yes. But the pleasure is bigger.” ✦

#6812. Joaquin Phoenix Wasn’t Thinking About 'Her' When He Filmed This Intelligent Eyewear Campaign

It feels like a scene out of a sci-fi movie: Joaquin Phoenix is standing across from me, instructing me to speak into his eyeglasses in a foreign language.

“Let’s see what happens,” the Oscar winner says. “It could be any language.”

The idea we’re testing here is whether the glasses Phoenix is wearing—a dark-rimmed pair from the South Korean luxury eyewear brand Gentle Monster’s new Intelligent Eyewear collection—could live-translate these words into English. This is one of the glasses’ capabilities the actor was most delighted to discover while he was shooting their campaign film directed by another Oscar winner, Jonathan Glazer. The glasses were crafted in partnership with Google and Samsung, and feature AI-powered technology.

Joaquin Phoenix in the Jonathan Glazer-directed campaign spot.

The last time Phoenix appeared in a fashion campaign, it was 1997 and he was modeling moody suits for Prada. This time around, he stars in the Glazer-directed spot that Phoenix says was partially inspired by the 1969 documentary Salesman, in which vendors travel across New England selling Bibles. In the Gentle Monster ad, Phoenix’s character—who has an intense, quasi-manic energy—ventures into an unnamed hotel, where he falls into a deep sleep and meets colorful, large creatures at the end of a hallway. (One might even call them…Gentle Monsters.) Per a press release, the “intelligent eyewear becomes his way of understanding their language and communicating with them, opening up unexpected possibilities for connection.”

Phoenix had wanted to work with Glazer for a long time. “I’ve known him for years, and when we all approached him, we came up with this really interesting and offbeat idea that just [had so] much mood. He just gets super excited and inspired,” the actor says. During the shoot, the Zone of Interest filmmaker kept the camera rolling for prolonged periods of time, encouraging Phoenix to harness different emotions. A moment in the ad might last for “30 seconds, but we shot for 15 minutes,” he explains. “A whole range of things are happening in those 15 minutes, things that we were trying.”

In an email, Glazer reiterated that he was glad to have had a reason to work with Phoenix: “The first image I had was him asleep, maybe dreaming, with the glasses all wonky across his face. The rest kind of grew from there,” the director said.

When I meet the actor last Wednesday at 23 Wall Street in Manhattan—where Gentle Monster has staged an enormous activation for the Intelligent Eyewear launch, complete with fake horses with wagging tails and huge surreal sculptures—it’s hard not to think about his turn in the 2013 film Her, one of the most prophetic AI movies of our time. In the Spike Jonze near-future drama, Phoenix’s character, Theodore Twombly, chats with and subsequently falls for an AI-powered operating system voiced by Scarlett Johansson. If 2011’s Contagion foreshadowed the COVID-19 pandemic, Her’s depiction of chatbots feels eerily similar to the world of ChatGPT dots, Meta Muses, Claude Duets, and Gemini Sparks that we’re beginning to grow accustomed to.

Phoenix and his partner, actress Rooney Mara, wearing Intelligent Eyewear at the Gentle Monster event in Manhattan.

When I ask Phoenix if filming the campaign made him think of Her, he shrugs. “I wasn’t. It seems like I should have,” he says. “It’s funny because I think that sometimes films have more of, maybe, an impact on the people that view them than for me, but I hadn’t thought about that. Somebody just made a joke about it actually, and I was like, ‘Oh yeah, you’re right.’”

Phoenix, who most recently starred in 2025’s politically tinged neo-Western Eddington, leaves on his Intelligent Eyewear glasses—which are Gemini-embedded, sleek, and thick-rimmed—while we speak.

“The thing is,” he says, “I’m not even aware that I’m wearing them.”

#6807. The Governors Awards Marked the Official Start of the 'You Can See Everything' Oscars Campaign

This is an edition of the weekly newsletter Tap In, GQ senior associate editor Frazier Tharpe’s final word on the most heated online discourse about music, movies, and TV. Sign up here to get it free.

Start your engines: the 2027 Oscars race is officially underway. Awards Season always starts in earnest with the Governors Awards, a ceremony held in a smaller ballroom in the same Dolby Theater complex where the main event will take place early next spring. It’s where they give out honorary Academy Awards (and the esteemed Irving Thalberg Award) to the legends who, woefully, have yet have won a competitive Oscar—last year’s marquee honoree was Tom Cruise, this year’s were Glenn Close and Ridley Scott. But it’s also where Best Picture and the other awards narratives we will discuss endlessly for the next four months really begin to take shape.

Dune 3, Artificial, and Misty Green all had big presences in the room, which, duh. (Tim and Kylie in the cut, mostly sitting and not mingling, but it was interesting that he showed up this year but not last for the big Marty Supreme push.) But there were a few other films represented whose presence made their campaign intentions clear. Mahershala Ali presented the first award of the night to Floyd Norman (a 90-year-old trailblazing cartoonist who worked with Walt Disney and later Pete Docter) but the Your Mother 3x squad was deep, from director Bassam Tariq to John Cho. Jesse Eisenberg, Paul Giamatti and Julianne Moore put on for A24’s The Debut, Eisenberg's second directorial effort. (Watching Jesse and Andrew Garfield reconnect made for a beautiful Social Network reunion.) And Curry Barker and Inde Navarette being there would certainly imply a real push for Obsession. The duo with the most motion in the room, though, was unquestionably Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim. The only time they didn’t seem like the big dogs that everyone was gassed to chat with was when they were talking to King Big Dog himself, Christopher Nolan, front and center receiving will-wishers at The Odyssey table. Nolan and Fielder is a crossover that needs to be cemented on wax, a drum I will continue to beat. But it was cool to see Zendaya talking intently with Oppenheim; her collaborator curation has been pretty elite—imagine her in whatever Oppenheim chooses to follow Primetime with?

When I asked Fielder about embarking on the campaign trail and how far the doc can actually go, he sighed and said “one day at a time,” in that Fielder monotone where you can’t quite tell if he’s being earnest or not. But he laughed off the idea of being too invested in any nominations either way.

But most importantly: the Governors Awards is just a really fun room in which to be an interloper or fly on the wall. I spotted Shawn Levy and my brain immediately started spamming the word “sublime.” Daniel Kaluuya started cracking up at the mention of his great airport interview with Tyriq Withers. Olivia Wilde, Seth Rogen, Penelope Cruz and Ed Norton joined ranks for a brief Invite huddle. Everywhere I turned, there was either a person so famous or certified that it startled me to see them walk by (like Zendaya breezing in right as the show started, or turning around to find myself face-to-face with personal favorite Carla Gugino. (Karen Sisco mentioned!) Unlike some stuffier award ceremonies, the Governors Awards is kind of like the cocktail hour before the big sit-down dinner in March.

Ambling around the room with a stiff glass of Johnnie Walker in hand—“Did you just get carded”? an exec asked me incredulously; the bartender replied that he thought I was “like, 14"—I briefly caught up with David Corenswet, who, true to form, was unfazed by the forces beyond his control as ever. When I said “weird week huh?” he didn’t even realize I was referring to James Gunn’s DC exit, then laughed when it dawned on him and said “above my pay-grade” with a genuine shrug. We should all be so blissfully unbothered.

Later, Yahya Abdul-Mateen II told me he was excited for this week’s Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth red carpet premiere, noting that this film will be the first time he only read his scenes for a project and no other part of the story. Without wanting to give too much away, Yahya said Tarantino’s script is structured in a way where he could comfortably read his parts and get enough of the gist to stay in the dark about the rest, because the only thing better than starring in a new Tarantino joint is getting to still experience it like a fan.

Other notable sightings, interactions, and crossovers: Travis Scott doubled down on what he told me at our Men of the Year party: he wants that golden statue for his original Odyssey song, and he’s willing to play the campaign game for it, dutifully sitting through the whole night (and jetting off immediately after the ceremony ended to go catch Future’s 10 pm set at Camp Flog Gnaw up at Dodger Stadium.) Speaking of Odyssey collaborators, James Blake was also in attendance—I watched him meet H.E.R. and talk about potentially collaborating. Tom Cruise was the belle of the ball at last year’s ceremony but played it super lowkey this year, to the point where I didn’t actually realize he was there until I saw him sneak out of a secret side exit after briefly talking with Tessa Thompson. Digger’s divisive reviews and reactions have evidently not knocked Tom off of his Best Actor pivot.

As for the actual awards, after Floyd, John Malkovich charmed the room with a great toast to Glenn Close, and King Storyteller Matt Damon regaled everyone with a great impression of triple-OG crank Ridley Scott from their time together on The Martian. (Matt was hesitant to do another movie about a loner astronaut right after Interstellar, to which Ridley apparently replied “No one gives a shit.”) Both tributes and subsequent speeches were expectedly emotional; Damon pointedly said that "it’s about time we see Ridley Scott with an Oscar in his hand,” but Ridley’s remarks were all about how much the late great Tony Scott would’ve deserved one too, leading to misty eyes and standing ovations. Close addressed going 0 for 8 but said the work is the real reward as her daughter tearfully watched on. But there were great moments of levity, too, like Todd Haynes presenting Christine Vachon and Pamela Koffler with an award by revealing that he and Christine both slept with the same guy back in college.

Either way, a narrative of being long overdue seems to be emerging. An Awards Season where the Tom Cruises and Glenn Closes of the game finally get theirs, while upsets like You Can See Everything shake the table. What more could you ask for?

#6804. The Best Icebreaker Gifts for the Guy Whose Personality Is Cardio

You know the type. He’s run three half-marathons this year and will tell you about every single one. He thinks a “rest day” means a light 10-mile hike. When it’s 25 degrees and sleeting, he’s somehow outside doing hill sprints. He’s exhausting, honestly, but you love him, and he’s about to be very easy to shop for. Skip the gift card and head straight to Icebreaker. The New Zealand brand has made merino wool its whole thing, and it’s the fabric that does it all: warm when it’s freezing, breathable when he’s working hard, and resistant to that locker-room smell.

We tracked down the Icebreaker pieces worthy of his overachieving lifestyle, from jackets that look good at the bar after a ski day to base layers that’ll survive his toughest training block. Will he be up at dawn wearing them while you’re still asleep? Absolutely. But at least you’ll know he’s warm.

This is a puffer for guys who hate looking like they’re wearing one. Because, let's be real, most puffers trade style for warmth. The quilted bomber is insulated with merino rather than down, so it’s warm without the bulk, and it’s sleek enough to wear from the trailhead to drinks after.

Praise be, a base layer with some personality. The wavy blue pattern makes it the rare thermal he’ll actually want to show off, and the midweight merino keeps him warm on early-morning runs and ski days without getting swampy.

A slim, no-fuss beanie that fits easily under a hood or helmet. It’s warm enough for a winter run but breathable enough that he won’t rip it off halfway through.

Not the most glamorous gift, but they’re the one he’ll thank you for the most. These lightweight merino leggings are the secret to staying warm on the slopes, on the trail, or on the walk to a 6 a.m. workout class.

Good socks change everything, and every serious hiker knows it. These are cushioned where he needs it and made from merino that fights blisters and odor, plus the mountain graphic is a fun touch.

This hooded jacket is the do-it-all layer for cold-weather training. It’s light enough to pack and warm enough to wear on its own, and it pulls double duty as a mid-layer when the temperature really drops.

Once he wears a merino tee, his cotton ones don’t stand a chance. It’s light, breathable, and doesn’t hold on to smells, so he can go from a morning workout to a long flight without a second thought.

It’s a fleece with the cozy factor turned all the way up. The high-pile merino blend is soft and warm. It’s the kind of layer he’ll throw on after a long Friday and won’t take off until Monday morning.

This is the classic Icebreaker base layer, and with good reason. The rusty red color is a nice change from the usual black and navy, and it works just as well under a shell on the mountain as it does under a flannel in town.

A technical layer that doubles as a sharp turtleneck. It’s warm enough for a winter run and polished enough to wear under a blazer, which makes it one of the more versatile pieces on this list.

Trust us, a guy who’s always moving needs better underwear. These merino boxers stay breathable through long runs, hikes, and travel days, and the two-pack means he’s set for the week (or at least the weekend).

#6799. David Corenswet Wore a Cult-Favorite Seiko on the Red Carpet

Superman actor David Corenswet, who’s set to reprise his Man of Steel role in Man of Tomorrow next year, just wore a way-out-of-left-field watch with a tuxedo during the 17th Governors Awards last night. There was no mistaking the glint of its dial from across the step-and-repeat: a Seiko ref. 6139 "Pogue" automatic chronograph. The bulky sport watch looked totally out of place in the restrained, elegant atmosphere of the awards ceremony…and absolutely perfect.

The “Pogue,” as the yellow-dial variant is known, is a wildly cool and beloved watch, and Corenswet smartly took advantage of the way it pops against the black-and-white of a classic tux. Plus, it doesn’t hurt that among Seiko collectors, the Pogue is a cult classic, often ranking towards the top of best-Seiko-ever lists. Not only is it an aesthetic swerve on the red carpet, but it’s the type of relatively affordable oddball celebrities rarely reach for.

What’s so special about the 6139, and why is it called the “Pogue,” you might ask? The 6139 was among the very first batch of automatic chronographs. A group of brands—Seiko; a consortium of Hamilton-Büren, Heuer-Leonaids, Breitling, and Dubois Dépraz; and Zenith-Movado—raced to be the first to debut the mechanical innovation. Who won the race is still a matter of debate, but one thing is for certain: when the 6139 hit the Japanese market in 1969, it featured an integrated design, a column wheel, and a vertical clutch. Meshing hallmarks of classic chronograph construction with the convenience of automatic winding at a relatively affordable price, it was paired with a wide variety of colorful dials and funky cases and produced for roughly a decade.

One of these designs, the ref. 6139-6002, is particularly eye-catching thanks to its bright-yellow dial with a contrasting blue-and-red tachymeter bezel, a 30-minute chronograph register, and a day-date display. American astronaut Col. William R. famously wore the Pogue during the Skylab 4 mission from November 1973 to February 1974. Subsequently, the 6139-6002 took on Pogue’s name, and is now one of the most collectible vintage Seiko references.

The best part? Though there’s unfortunately no magic bullet to make you 6’4” and handsome as Superman, you can snag a pretty good “Pogue” for under $2,000. It may not take you into space, but as Corenswet has proven, it sure livens up your average black-tie outfit.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6798. Creamy Miso Mushroom Pasta

Mushroom fans: Bookmark this recipe immediately. It will give you everything you want when you crave a creamy mushroom pasta as soon as the crisp fall air sets in. The hardworking trio of miso, Parmesan, and touch of soy sauce delivers an umami bomb of flavor that doesn’t overwhelm, but magnifies, the best of earthy mushrooms. Coupled with luscious noodles enrobed in a creamy (but not heavy) sauce, what’s not to love?

A few helpful notes for pasta success: (1) While any pasta will work, egg-based noodles, like the fettuccine I recommend here, are the best complement to the sauce. The nests (Rummo and De Cecco are great accessible brands) cook far faster than standard straight pasta, almost as fast as fresh. (2) Don’t be afraid to season your mushrooms! Yes, I know this goes against all that we’ve learned about salting them too early in the cooking process, but just a bit of salt will get them going and releasing their water faster for eventual crisp-browned edges. Don’t be alarmed if you see a pool of water at the start of the mushroom cooking—that’s to be expected and just keep going until it evaporates. (3) Don’t forget to reserve plenty of pasta cooking liquid, which is critical to the eventual sauce’s success. I prefer transferring the al dente noodles from the pasta pot to the skillet, letting any residual water come with it (and to ensure you don’t accidentally drain away that liquid gold).

Find more fall favorite meals here: Weeknight Pumpkin Chili; Cream-ish of Mushroom Soup; Mushrooms au Poivre.

Cook 12 oz. egg-based pasta (such as fettuccine nests) in a large pot of boiling salted water, stirring occasionally, until al dente, about 1 minute less than package directions.

Meanwhile, heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet over high. Add 1 lb. crimini or mixed mushrooms (such as button, maitake, oyster, and/or shiitake), sliced ¼" thick or torn into bite-size pieces, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt and toss to coat evenly. Spread out into an even layer and cook, undisturbed, until mushrooms are starting to release their liquid, 2–3 minutes (do not be alarmed; there will be a lot). Continue to cook, tossing occasionally, until moisture is almost completely evaporated and edges of mushrooms are starting to brown, 6–8 minutes.

Reduce heat to medium-high; add 5 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, and remaining 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil. Cook, tossing often, until garlic is fragrant and golden, 1–2 minutes. Pour in ¾ cup dry white wine, scraping up any browned bits stuck to bottom of pan with a sturdy spatula or flat-edged wooden spoon, and cook until wine is mostly evaporated (you should have about 1 Tbsp. liquid left in pan), about 4 minutes. Reduce heat to medium-low. Add 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, cut into pieces, and 2 Tbsp. white miso and stir until melted and combined. Slowly pour in ½ cup heavy cream, scraping up any browned bits stuck to bottom of pan; simmer until thickened slightly, 2–3 minutes. Add 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce; season with freshly ground pepper. Taste and add more salt if needed.

Using tongs, transfer pasta to mushroom mixture and add 1 cup pasta cooking liquid. Increase heat to medium and bring to a simmer. Cook, tossing constantly and adding ½–1 cup more pasta cooking liquid to reach desired sauce consistency if needed, until pasta is al dente and liquid has thickened slightly, about 3 minutes.

Remove pan from heat. Add 1 oz. Parmesan, finely grated (about ½ cup) to pasta and toss to combine. Season generously with more pepper; taste and season with more salt if needed.

Divide pasta among shallow bowls and top with more Parmesan and pepper.

#6795. Nike Made an Air Jordan 1 for Guys Who Love Japanese Denim

Menswear guys love Japanese denim. Menswear guys also love sneakers. So why not make some kicks for the people who can tell you exactly which artisanal mill produced their selvedge? That seems to be the thinking behind the new Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko.’

Social Status is a North Carolina-based sneaker and streetwear boutique owned by the Whitaker Group, the same company behind A Ma Maniére. Over the years, it’s worked with Nike on everything from milk-inspired Dunks to Air Pennys. The shop’s last proper Jordan collab was an extremely hairy Air Jordan 6 from 2019, but now the pair are back with something a little easier to wear.

Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’

Social Status’s take on the AJ1 Low starts with the classic ‘Chicago’ color palette. The toebox is white, the overlays are red, and the Swoosh and collar are black. But rather than keeping everything box-fresh, Social Status has roughed up the leather so it arrives pre-scuffed, as though you’ve owned these shoes for years.

The big switch happens across the sidewalls. The usual white leather panels have been replaced with mismatched patches of indigo fabric covered in white sashiko-style stitching. Sashiko is a traditional Japanese embroidery technique used to reinforce, repair, and extend the life of clothing. It’s particularly associated with vintage workwear and denim, which is why these Jordans look like they’ve been mended using a very expensive pair of jeans.

There’s also an old-school nylon tongue, a slightly aged sole, and a custom shoebox wrapped in the same blue pattern.

Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’

While you might normally expect this sort of big Jordan release to land on the High, it’s nice to see the Low silhouette get some love. Some dudes prefer it, after all—it’s less bulky around the ankle and works better under wider trousers. You still get all the bits that make an Air Jordan 1 instantly recognizable, only without half the collar.

The Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’ is set to drop on December 18 for $155 through Social Status, with a wider release via Nike, the SNKRS app, and select stockists expected to arrive later on. Japanese denim guys, get your credit cards ready.

A version of this story originally appeared on British GQ.

#6791. Joseph Zada of The Hunger Games Is Still Getting Used to Being a Movie Star

Joseph Zada is doing “very, very, very good,” he tells me, with a heavy emphasis on the third very and an audible exclamation mark. And why shouldn’t he be?

His much-anticipated film, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, finally comes out this week. Zada and I spoke the morning of his New York City premiere, while he was being shuttled from one press obligation to the next, already go-go-go before actually hitting the red carpet Monday night in custom Louis Vuitton, punctuated with a pair of gold-toed cowboy boots from Pharrell’s first collection for the brand.

In the film, Zada plays a young Haymitch Abernathy, who we follow through his own Hunger Games, some twenty-five years ahead of the events of the original trilogy. When asked about the movie, the 21-year-old Australian demonstrates palpable care and excitement, even a bit of sadness that it’s over. “I just think that it was the most I’ve had to prepare and the most support that I’ve had and the most that I’ve ever stepped into a character’s shoes,” Zada says. “And it was just such a joy to act in this and to feel what it was like to really be this character.”

Since being cast, he’s barely had a second to stop and reflect on joining one of the biggest franchises in the world—and this comes right on the heels of East of Eden, too, in which Zada played another haunted young man. He laughs when I note the comparison: “I guess I just pride myself on my ability to show my vulnerability.”

Below, read our full conversation with the emerging star as he reflects on his Hunger Games journey, talks red carpet style, and shares his thoughts on Robert Pattinson playing James Bond.

Joseph Zada: Yeah, I wonder if I’ll be nervous.

Maybe towards the start. It doesn’t get old… but it gets familiar.

Well, I did SiriusXM this morning, and then, I mean, it’s basically just been like, "Hey, do press all day and then go to your premiere." So there’s not much time for a ritual, so I guess I go a little bit nonverbal and half-dissociate.

No, it’s so much fun. I have a lot of fun at these premieres. It’s always like a crazy, crazy crowd. The way they’ve set it up has just been otherworldly, and I’m not sure if I’ll ever have it again, but I distinctly remember each stop and the feelings that I’ve had thus far, and it doesn’t get old. It’s always crazy to see this many fans and the passion they have for this project. I mean, it’s not every day that you get to step into something with a built-in fan base where people are already coming out and supporting it before they’ve even seen it. So it’s a really special experience, and I don’t know when I’m going to be able to get that again.

Where did I have the most fun? I had a lot of fun in London because my little sister was there, my little sister and my brother’s girlfriend, so I got to hang out with them and I haven’t seen them in a while. And Mexico. Mexico was the craziest event that we’ve had. There were literally thousands, several thousands of people there. The reception was incredible.

Am I allowed to drop this? Oh, yes, that is what this [interview] is for. I’m wearing a Louis Vuitton suit—custom, custom—that Jason [Bolden] and I have worked together on, and I’m a big fan of the boots. The boots are pretty dope.

I feel a lot more comfortable in these clothes nowadays, and it’s actually become a lot more enjoyable to put them on. And it feels like more of an armor and less of a scary thing, and it feels like I build a lot of confidence being able to do this.

My red carpet style, I think... I don’t know. Just whatever Jason puts me in for the night.

Yeah, I think just doing it so often and finding different looks and just being able to wear all the different brands and find what I’m comfortable in.

I pride myself on my ability to have vulnerability. I think as a young man, that’s what I find interesting in these performances of these young men coming of age, and I think that was really what I was just looking for in my early career. I modeled my career on people like Timothée Chalamet and Lucas Hedges, and I love the way that they kept their heart on their sleeve and how they feel like an open nerve. I love being able to portray that in my performances.

I just think that it was the most I’ve had to prepare and the most support that I’ve had and the most that I’ve ever stepped into a character’s shoes and really, really felt like I was there every single day and just had the most incredible support in terms of my cast mates and my directors and my producers. And it was just such a joy to act in this and to feel what it was like to really be this character.

I guess everything just kind of changed just like that. Everything just changed in one quick second, and it feels like... I guess I haven’t really thought about what it was like before. I’ve just kind of been trying to ride the wave. I’m sure being able to go back home for Christmas, I’ll have some time to reflect, but right now it’s just—what’s the day ahead of me? And just making sure I’m ready for all of that.

I’m just excited to be at home every single day and watch movies with my mom and my siblings and see all my friends. That’s what I’m really excited for. I’m just excited to also just turn off my phone for a little bit, maybe go on a little road trip down to South Australia and get a little beach house and not bring my phone and have six days where I’m just off the grid.

That’s a great question. I’ve got Apocalypse Now on 4K Ultra HD on my projector that I bought at home, and I cannot wait. I feel like people ask me what was the best part of my year, and I just start naming all the movies that I’ve watched. And genuinely the happiest that I ever am is when I’m watching a film, which is kind of sad but also kind of cool.

I think... I don’t know. I loved Wild Horse Nine. That’s a favorite I’ve watched in cinemas. I loved Primetime too, because I love Rob Pattinson. I love Skyler Gisondo.

I did just see the Bond casting news! I am chuffed. That is so exciting. That is beyond exciting. He is just the most phenomenal actor, and he really just makes the most interesting choices. And I think him in that role, the stoicism that he’ll bring along with those really interesting choices, it’s going to make that character so interesting. He’s a really special actor. It feels like every young male actor that I speak to feels like that is a person to look to right now and a person’s career to emulate in terms of the risks and the commitment to his craft and all these roles that he’s churning out.

I don’t know. It’s so ridiculously helpful to have a book with you because it’s just so much information, and I love preparing for roles and having a book because it’s just an internal monologue, which is what you should be doing for every character. You read a scene, and you should be writing what is going through your character’s head. And when you have a book, it’s all done for you, or at least you have a good foundation.

I hope I get to do as many book adaptations as possible, but I think what I’m really looking for is just a great director and a great script. I’m not sure what’s [next]. I’ve got a fresh slate right now, but there’s a couple of things floating around.

#6787. Robert Pattinson, I’m Begging You For a Quirked-Up James Bond

Our long international nightmare is finally over: two-time Meme of the Year nominee Robert Pattinson has been chosen as Daniel Craig’s James Bond successor, bringing an end to an exhausting series of “reports” and “leaks” about Amazon’s search for a new 007. Finally we can stop the debates and fan-casting and let Patts and Denis Villeneuve cook until they’re ready to present their new vision. But that would require a level of restraint that the internet doesn’t typically exercise. Immediately after the protracted process of casting Bond concluded, the internet moved on to naming its dream picks for Bond Girls and Bond Villains (sometimes, to brilliant effect.) I can’t say I have a dog in either fight, at least not right now. My Bond WishList is of a different magnitude altogether: can we make James Bond silly again? Just a little, even, 15 percent goofball?

In the hands of another (read: more limited) actor, I’d never suggest such a risky swerve. But the best thing about Pattinson is his range. Sure, if Villeneuve and co. want to render Bond as the stoic and taciturn gentleman he’s generally been this century, Rob has proven he can do that, as every Tenet truther has been gleefully preaching for the last 24 hours. But more often than not, Pattinson is keen to get weird, whether that’s going Full Freak, as he does in Primetime, or approaching certain archetypal parts with a silly, goofy mood (like his conniving suitor in The Odyssey). He can do a “voice” (Mickey 17), he can do scumbag grime (Good Time), he can do hapless screwball (The Drama). Why not make what’ll be his biggest role to date a little cocktail of all his best elements? (Okay, he can leave the Mickey 17 voice behind.)

20 years ago, Casino Royale was a refreshing reset and a crucial corrective to the overflowing ridiculousness of the Piece Brosnan era. But in the past two decades, the pendulum has swung back—the gritty reboot has become studios' stock-in-trade. I feel the same way about Batman; the actual risky choice would be to make something less like Nolan’s series that, instead, hewed a little closer to, say, Batman Forever, or even the gothic camp of Tim Burton’s two films.

Of course, Robert Pattinson was at the center of a Batman reboot as well, and the result was a movie that felt more like a spiritual cousin to David Fincher’s Se7en; audiences ate it up so much that the hunger for a sequel persists even after endless delays. When it comes to franchises as high-stakes as Batman or Bond, you can’t trust these increasingly sauceless execs to make anything but the basic choice. There’s no reason to expect Jeff Bezos isn’t texting Denis screenshots from Skyfall for reference from his megayacht at this very moment.

But if there’s anybody capable of putting a little English on the handle while still giving the mainstream what it wants, it’s Big Bob Pattinson. He could easily make his James Bond a little off, or he could go full seductive cad who smiles like Neil Tenet, has a ki with Q, and charms women with some playful banter instead of simply brooding them into submission. I’m not looking for invisible cars and sharks with lasers, or a Lothario like Roger Moore who speaks in puns. All I’m asking is that we give camp a chance. Because ultimately, the most exciting thing Villeneuve and Pattinson can do to set their Bond apart would be to loosen him up a little.
