#6747. Are You a Super Ager? This Competition Will Be the Judge of That

Alan Krepack stands with his hands on his hips and takes a deep breath as he sizes up the Over Under: an obstacle with a high wooden box and low wooden beam spread 10 meters apart. Music booms over the speakers and other competitors shuffle around the cavernous room of the Armory Track in Washington Heights, New York, but Krepack stays laser-focused, thanks to years of meditation practice.

As soon as the volunteer says “go” he sprints to the box, plants his hands, and hoists his feet up and over. As he lands on the other side, he sprints to the beam, dropping down into an army crawl. “Take your time, take your time,” his son yells from the sidelines. Krepack reaches the far end, rushes back, and repeats the sequence again before crossing the line in 51 seconds. To cool down, he paces back and forth with his hands on his head. “I think I’m a sprinter, I’m a jumper, but then it’s like, fuck, no, I’m 73 years old,” he says, catching his breath.

The challenge is just the first of eight trials Krepeck will tackle throughout the day alongside his sons, Harrison and Aaron, at the Super Age Games, a fitness and mental competition designed to assess abilities associated with healthy aging. On November 7th, the inaugural day-long event drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries, and about 70% of participants were between the ages of 40 and 64. Krepeck agreed to let me shadow him to understand this new event through the lens of someone who is, in fact, aging, and would like to do so gracefully.

The name of the Games comes from a buzzword that originated in the longevity world: super ager. The term was originally coined by Northwestern University researchers to describe adults over 80 whose memories rival those of people 30 years younger. These days it’s thrown around on podcasts and in books to describe anyone who defies both the typical trajectory of both cognitive and physical aging.

The inaugural Super Age Games drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries.

From crawling across a cargo net to reading microexpressions, the challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill. At the end, athletes see where they rank among their age group and receive their “Super Age,” learning how their body’s physical fitness stacks up against the general U.S. population’s.

In an era where your average middle-aged midwestern dad is training for Hyrox, and a new, outlandishly extreme challenge is being invented every few months, the Super Age games are a low-key counterpoint to raw, stunty, athleticism, born from the metric-obsessed fitness subcultures who prize longevity above all. And now that fitness trackers like Whoop or Apple Watch can track VO2max, heart rate variability, and resting heart rates, that group continues to grow. Along the way, these stats have become status symbols on par with owning a Lamborghini. And now that overall health is quantifiable, fitness values have become less about just how fast you run or how much you lift, but how slowly aging.

It follows that, despite its name, Super Age Games isn’t just for aging athletes. The crowd there leaned middle-aged, and the youngest person at the competition was 21. As we stood by the dead hang station, Krepak pointed across the room at a brolic young man who looked like he could be an NFL prospect: “That dude’s a serious athlete,” he says, as we watched him run around the track cradling a sandbag on his shoulder.

Unlike competitions like Hyrox, where athletes race through a sequence of workouts against the clock, the Super Age Games allows competitors to rest as long as they need between trials. In fact, Junior Kennedy, the fitness program director for the Games, encourages it. “There's no rush,” he says. “The goal is to get through each event as intentionally as you can. We need to understand what it means to recover.”

Each trial was developed with a certain longevity marker in mind, and developed with input from researchers at the Buck Institute for Research on Aging, a leading nonprofit research center based in Novato, California dedicated to understanding aging. The Over Under, for example, tests agility by judging how fast you can change levels and switch directions. Meanwhile, the Synapse, a challenge where participants track moving balls on a screen and identify them after they've been shuffled around, is meant to measure cognitive processing.

Challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill.

Krepack, a psychologist and lifelong athlete from Mill Valley, California, first heard about the event through its founder, David Stewart. Stewart is a media founder behind two niche longevity platforms, AGEIST and Super Age, where he writes about longevity, wellness, and redefining aging (one of his website testimonial’s says he has made aging “cool and aspirational.”). Krepack’s generally a skeptic of the profit-driven modern wellness industry—“I've always had a jaded view of all of that stuff,” he says—but he has followed both Stewart and the Buck Institutes’ work for years.

The competition appealed to Krepack, in part because he’s spent decades cultivating a consistent exercise routine he’s proud of. He played baseball in college and has spent much of his life skiing, cycling, and playing other sports. These days, his schedule revolves around a handful of rituals: bike rides on Mondays and Fridays, a Saturday hike with a friend, gardening, yoga, and Zen meditation most days. When he signed up for the Games in June, he altered his exercise routine, lifted more weights, practiced hanging from a bar, and experimented with the Neurotracker cognitive test online.

Yet all that training didn’t prepare Krepack for the biggest surprise of his day. After scoring well on both the Inferno (the assault bike VO2max test) and Synapse, next came the fourth trial: the “Tightrope,” a balance challenge that requires competitors to walk along a low beam while carrying a water-filled bag, taking as many consecutive steps as they can in two minutes without falling. He had expected to excel, thanks to years of skiing and yoga. Instead, he struggled to keep steady as the water shifted inside the bag. As his foot slipped off the bar, his eyes got wide. “I can’t believe my balance,” he says. “That’s my one disappointment in life now.”

Alan Krepack's sons Harrison and Aaron during the “tightrope” challenge.

Krepack was still hard on himself as we walked over to the dead hang trial. But after beating both his sons’ times, he seemed to get his competitive spirit back. He pushed through the remaining challenge determined to finish strong. And he did: By the end of the Games, he had placed second overall, with scores adjusted for age.

A few days after the competition, I checked in with Krepack to ask him how he’d processed the event. He responded with screenshots of his scores. His highest score came from the cargo net, which he credited to sheer determination rather than any particular finesse: “due to my will and overcoming an unconventional and desperate style!” he texted me, “Plus my sons were great support and focus partners.”

But the Games were also a reality check about aging: “I left there feeling both young and old,” he tells me on a phone call. As he watched his sons rocket through the obstacles, he was forced to confront how much of his athleticism he’d lost. “They just flew over it. They were so fluid, and so fast,” he says. “I’m not 30 or 40 or 50 or 60 anymore.”

Despite all the longevity industry's promises of maintaining youth, Krepack sees little value in denying the inevitable. Aging well, he says, is about “accepting, improving, and doing your best. It’s not a denial of who you are at this point in life.” The goal, he says, is to make the most of what you’re still capable of.

#6746. Soothing Rice Porridge

This is a Japanese-style rice porridge known as okayu. I ate this every morning in the early weeks of postpartum after my first daughter was born. It’s what our family reaches for whenever we need a simple, nourishing dish that warms from the inside out. For the smoothest porridge, let the rice soak in cold water before cooking.

If you have time, soak the rice, which makes the texture softer and can aid in digestion. Place washed rice in a bowl and pour in water to cover by 1". Soak at least 30 minutes and up to 4 hours. Drain off excess water before starting the recipe. The wakame seaweed, which you’ve probably had in miso soup, can typically be found dried anywhere Asian groceries are available.

In The Fourth Trimester, we ask parents: What meal nourished you after welcoming your baby? This month it’s a cozy rice porridge by Eating at Home cookbook author Trinity Mouzon Wofford. Read more: Breakfast at Home, After Birth at Home.

Combine 1 cup short-grain rice, washed, soaked if desired, ¼ cup dried wakame (seaweed), 1 Tbsp. dried red shiso, and 6 cups low-sodium broth (any kind) or water in a large donabe, medium Dutch oven, or other heavy pot. Cover and bring to a boil over medium-high heat (you’ll hear bubbling). Uncover and stir, making sure nothing is stuck to bottom of pot. Cover and reduce heat to low. Continue to cook porridge 30 minutes more (resist the urge to peek).

Remove pot from heat and let porridge sit (still covered) 10 minutes. Uncover and stir. Rice grains should be cooked through, broken open, and creamy, but porridge should still be fairly loose. Season porridge with fine sea salt.

Ladle porridge into bowls and serve, topped with toasted sesame seeds, toasted sesame oil, and/or thinly sliced scallions.

#6745. 29 Vintage Photos of a Young Meg Ryan, Fall Style Icon

#6744. The Horse Girl Trend Is Here for Your Jewelry Box

#6743. Pride and Prejudice’s Rufus Sewell Is the Most Dashing Man on Television

It’s possible that Rufus Sewell is having too much fun right now. Between playing morally ambiguous Vice President (and husband to the U.S. Ambassador to the Court of St. James’s) Hal Wyler in Netflix’s frothy, prestige-y series The Diplomat and the beloved but deeply flawed Mr. Bennet in the streamer’s upcoming adaptation of Pride and Prejudice, he’s cornered the market on lovable rascals. He thrives on “exercises in gray” and relishes “when it goes wrong” for a character. “It’s the opposite of what you want in life,” he tells me. “You want drama, you want mistakes.” These roles offer plenty of both.

Wyler, of The Diplomat, is navigating how to work with the increasingly autocratic and bloody-minded President Grace Penn (Allison Janney) thousands of miles away from his wife Kate (Keri Russell), who is starting to exhibit some of his own manipulative behaviors she claims to abhor. Two centuries prior, Mr. Bennet is reckoning not with geopolitical crises and egomaniacal heads of state, but with the challenges his wife faces in marrying off five eligible, if dowry-poor, daughters.

Sewell sat down for a long chat with GQ about the two series, the value of blending high silliness with high stakes in the same scene, and how bodily functions are an under-appreciated key to unlocking a character’s specificity.

Rufus Sewell: The interesting question for me, for the second half of the season, was how much is Hal truly aware of everything that Kate and everyone else are noticing and concerned about with Grace's behavior. How much does he see? How much is he choosing to pretend not to see in order to stay in the room? He's always believed that access is all, but how much do you compromise in order to stay in the room? Where’s the point when you realize you stayed in the room too long without saying anything, and the moment’s passed, and you are just an accomplice? I found it very difficult to play his seeming unctuousness and obsequiousness with Grace this season, and to not be trying to protect my character in the wrong way, because I felt he was failing.

He’s overestimated his ability to effect change with Grace. Hal was prepared to appear to be supporting her, when he believed in his ability to steer her in a better direction, and I think he goes past that point. When I read the final episode script and saw where he ends up walking out, it gave me the courage to really lean into how much he goes wrong, by his own standards earlier in the season. He begins to lose his way in a way that he never has done before. But I embraced it, knowing that the real gift of the part was getting to play him going wrong and then seemingly correcting himself. We actually shot a speech that he made, just to give us a sense of where the scene was headed and for me to know how to play it. They might end up using some of it in the next season, or they could end up completely changing what was written. Hal coming out of the summit is a momentous decision in terms of what that does for the future and I've got no idea what’s next—I can't wait to start filming season five to find out.

I was always shocked when this show was described as a thriller because I always thought it was a kind of romantic sex comedy with extremely high stakes, because what it reminded me of firstly was some of the comedy of divorce screwball films of the 1940s. The characters may be divorced after a very tempestuous relationship, but they’re interlinked in a way that can never be undone. There's a romance to all of that history.

The other thing that it reminded me of was the kind of films that were made in the '70s—French films where halfway through an argument one of the characters would sit down on the toilet, you know what I mean? The people inside these films remind you of your own relationships. Your long-term relationship might not go to the same extremes, it might not be in the same setting, but I think people are people. What I love about their relationship is their shared wonkiness, and the getting into the nitty-gritty of the politics and taking someone down and hatching a plot whilst fighting over pastries and getting crumbs on the bed—all of that is part of their foreplay.

That is what the show is about, as far as I'm concerned. These are people who wear suits that might stand at a lectern and be on TV, but the fact is, how do you scratch an itch? Do you smell? Do you need to go to the toilet? These are the things that the script leans into a lot, and it might put some people off, but I don't care about that. That's the thing that makes it feel real, and it's so refreshing to play.

He'll flirt with anyone. We all know people like this. It's not even necessarily sexual, he just engages with people on that level and it's a natural thing for him.

I would say Hal’s flirtatiousness being a problem is open for debate, because a lot of what we understand of him is via Kate’s descriptions of him and his behavior. I can and often do see it in a different way. Part of the story of seasons three and four is Kate’s realization that a lot of stuff she thought of as his personality was in fact the mantle of the job that he'd taken on, because we see her doing the same kinds of things now that she is an ambassador. They both have a lot of the same qualities. They're both killers in a sense, and it's been very convenient for her and great for the show for her to introduce the public to Hal through her descriptions. And yet, every time it seems like he's changed character, in fact, it is revealing something from a different perspective.

We learned pretty early on that we were going to be doing some very, very long scenes, and we were given the advantage of seeing the scenes very early so we could slowly gestate the performances. We needed that because Windsor Castle in the show was comprised of several different buildings and locations, some of them with oceans between them, so we’d be shooting some parts the next day, and other bits in four months’ time.

Especially with a two-hander, with such long takes, you can go in and out of being relaxed, unrelaxed, free, slightly bound, and actually you get to the point where you stop worrying about it. It's like being on stage, because you end up too tired to be bad all the time and end up inhabiting the moment and things just happen. I love that all of it was a surprise to us.

I think what is most important in this particular iteration of the relationship between Mr. and Mrs. Bennet is that he comes to realize which ways he's failed his daughters. When Mrs. Bennet says to him in this version, it's easy for you to say that I’m silly or frivolous, but you try being a woman at this age with no means—this is the fight of their lives that he is making these jokes about. And yes, of course he's funny and right in certain ways, but he's also guilty of negligence of his family for the sake of an easy life for himself. The reason they have no money is that so much money has been spent on bonnets. Money could have been saved, but in order to have a quiet life, he's ducked out of fights in order to avoid responsibility.

Exactly. It was a gift in this role to have that moment when he feels that, admits that, and gets to be slightly humbled in this idea of himself that he's been cultivating. He didn’t actively or consciously avoid having this revelation, but that’s the effect of staying in his study, and now he’s facing the consequences. To use very modern terms, he has a revelation of what his pattern has been. It made sense!

One could be forgiven for thinking that Mr. and Mrs. Bennet are two people that used to be attracted to each other and now have nothing in common and don't like each other. I think it's born out in the book, though, the idea that there is still a love there, and especially a shared humor between them, and a bit of a spark remains.

I love the fact that we get to get drunk together and stuff like that. Olivia Colman and I worked on The Father together a few years ago with Anthony Hopkins, which was a very different kind of gig, but as is often the case with things that are very heavy and intense, we had a hoot doing it. I loved working with her, and was very excited about the chance to work with her again. I love the scenes between Mr. Bennet and Mrs. Bennet. They were such a treat for us.

#6735. How to Grow a Beard

So you want to learn how to grow a beard. First off, you should know that it helps to have the right genetics. Second, you have to stay committed to the endeavor. Too often, beard-growing attempts end before they’ve even had a chance to establish itself.

“I’d say give it at least three to four weeks of solid growth before you even think about touching it with clippers,” Victor Mendoza, co-founder of beard care brand Viking Revolution, says. “Your beard needs time to actually show you what it’s going to look like, and that growth period lets you see the natural direction everything’s growing in. Plus, some hairs take longer to come in than others, so patience is key.”

There’s a sweet spot between letting your facial hair grow out and knowing when it’s time to take the trimmers for the best results possible, even if you can’t go full lumberjack. These are the best tips to maximize your hair follicles’ potential whether this is your first beard or your fiftieth.

There are many different beard styles to choose from; you don’t need to stick to neatly manicured, uniform look. Let your growth be influenced by the style you want to achieve.

“Your genes determine hair thickness, growth rate, density, and color,” Mendoza says. “If your dad or granddad had a thin beard, you’re probably dealing with the same hand, and that’s just life.”

If your beard is patchy, or if it has a unique growth pattern (sparse on the cheeks, full on the goatee, for example), then you should set your expectations properly. This goes for beards big, small, and medium alike. And while nutrition is important for hair growth, you can’t cheat genetics with your diet.

Patience is a virtue. Not only can a little time help fill in some patchiness, but it’s also requisite for almost any style. A beard transforms into something new every week. So, if it’s seeming like you’ll never reach your goal—in particular if it’s a long beard—then you honestly might wake up one morning and realize that you’ve arrived at the destination.

Mendoza says to wait at least two weeks before you start to do a cleanup, but only around the ends, i.e. the hairs that aren’t within the perimeter in which you want your beard to grow. That’ll help you figure out your beard’s natural shape.

One of the tenets of beard maintenance is to keep it defined. In short: shave the neckline and clean up the cheek lines. These keep it more beard-like and less neckbeard-like—unless, for some reason, a wooly stoner beard is your aim. In that case, let it grow wild and free.

The second way to keep things looking intentional is to sculpt and trim your beard as it grows. It might feel counterproductive—and counter to #2—but it’s the same way you need to get haircuts while growing out your hair. As Mendoza says, this helps direct the hair exactly where it needs to be: Fuller in some spots, less so in others. The styling products in the next tip can of course help influence things, but you foremost want to keep hairs in check as needed: This can mean spot-checking strays, snipping away split ends, or simply taking out some bulk. You’ll want a beard trimmer or a sharp pair of scissors.

There are a handful of beard products you should consider picking up if you’re serious about proper care and maintenance. A few drops of beard oil will absorb quickly and give your hair the right nutrients (plus a pinch of shine), while also making it less wild and frizzy. You can also swap in a beard balm or cream, something slightly heftier than an oil, to help style the beard and keep it in place all day. What’s important is that you clean your beard every day with an actual beard wash and not just a regular shampoo, which Mendoza says will dry out your beard and your face.

Weekly maintenance will consist of a deep condition, which you can do in the shower. “If you’re doing those things consistently, your beard is going to look way healthier and fuller than most guys out there,” he says.

Beard brushes help exfoliate the skin beneath the beard, for starters, which lifts dead skin cells and prevents future flaking. Moreover, brushing your beard at the end of each day will ensure its health and tame-ability in the long haul. This distributes the natural oils produced in the skin, which otherwise collect at the base of the shaft and only nourish the bottom of each hair. This oil distribution prevents breakage, frizzing, and split ends. (This is a great time to apply a few drops of beard oil as a booster, especially after a shower.)

Once your base has grown out—this can take months—it’s time to really make it yours. One of our favorites is to snip the bridge between the mustache and the chin, thus marooning the mustache. Or, pare back the beard itself, leaving a fuller mustache. You have lots of options, and it’s your privilege as a beard grower to flaunt it.

#6734. DoorDash Reveals The Thanksgiving Desserts & Sides Each State Loves

Somebody might want to check on the pumpkin pie loyalists.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

During a Thanksgiving food debate, everyone at the table likely has a strong opinion. DoorDash just added some numbers to the argument, and they might surprise you—especially if you assumed pumpkin pie was untouchable.

Ahead of Thanksgiving 2026, the delivery company went ahead and analyzed what Americans ordered during last year's holiday, and apparently, we've got some explaining to do. From an unexpected dessert taking the national crown to some questionable side-dish allegiances, the results make a pretty convincing case that no two Thanksgiving tables are exactly alike.

According to DoorDash's 2025 ordering data, chocolate cake was the most popular store-bought pie or cake purchased through the platform during Thanksgiving week. Apple pie came in second, followed by cheesecake. Pumpkin pie, arguably the holiday's most recognizable dessert, had to settle for fourth place, with pound cake rounding out the top five.

Things get even more interesting when you look at individual states. DoorDash also identified which desserts each state ordered at a higher rate than the national average, revealing some pretty distinct regional preferences.

Pumpkin pie still had plenty of supporters, with strong support in 14 states, including California, Washington, and Arizona. Hawaii was especially devoted, ordering it at nearly three times the national share. Meanwhile, New York, Connecticut, and Massachusetts leaned toward apple pie, while New Jersey and Pennsylvania showed a particular fondness for pound cake.

Cheesecake stood out in eight states, including Illinois, Michigan, and Ohio. Texas, Louisiana, and Tennessee favored pecan pie, while Maine and New Hampshire were among the states where chocolate pie stood out.

And then there's Georgia, where key lime pie was the surprising choice.

Mashed potatoes topped DoorDash's national Thanksgiving-week ranking, followed by corn, sweet potatoes, green beans, and gravy. Mac and cheese, stuffing, cornbread, carrots, and cranberries also made the top 10.

Regionally, though, things get a little more interesting. Cornbread stood out in Texas, Louisiana, and Mississippi, while North Carolina and South Carolina showed a preference for mac and cheese. Tennessee's standout was biscuits, and Georgia, Alabama, and Florida leaned toward corn.

New York and New Jersey, two states that can barely agree on pizza, apparently found some common ground in carrots. Meanwhile, Vermont was the only state where stuffing stood out most, and neighboring New Hampshire went with squash.

It's worth remembering that these state rankings reflect what people ordered more frequently relative to the national average on DoorDash. They aren't necessarily each state's most popular dish overall, and they don't account for whatever Grandma was making from scratch.

Still, if someone tries to tell you there's only one acceptable Thanksgiving menu, you now have some pretty compelling evidence to the contrary.

#6731. Apple Juice Bread

A simple swap from water to apple juice transforms this basic bread recipe into a subtly sweet loaf that is perfect to tear into at breakfast time with a smear of good butter on top, but it also takes a PB&J to the next level. The subtle, but unmistakable, undercurrent of apple sweetness struck me as a bit of a magic trick in a bread that, otherwise, to the eye and hand was just a soft white loaf.

I first encountered the idea at the small four-seat teppanyaki restaurant in the back of the Suiran Hotel in Kyoto, Japan, where chef Tsubasa Yamaguchi served it before my first course; it was a perfect way to start the meal. When I asked him what he did differently to prepare it, he said he just took the same rustic bread recipe he baked elsewhere and used apple juice in place of water. I was incredulous. That was it? The same amount? The same technique? Yes and yes, he said. I was hooked. It turns out the water-to-juice switcheroo gives an added taste of fall without taking anything away from the shape and texture. It’s become an instant favorite at my house both breakfast and lunch, and I expect it will be at yours.

Take care when you’re lowering the bread into the Dutch oven; it is a slightly nerve-wracking moment but a little attention is all that’s needed. You can do it!

More apple recipes here: 37 Apple Desserts That Make Us Want to Pick the Whole Orchard

Mix 4 cups (500 g) all-purpose flour, 1¾ cups apple juice, 1 Tbsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 Tbsp. sugar, and one ¼-oz. envelope instant yeast (about 2¼ tsp.) in a large bowl just until flour is hydrated and a very shaggy dough forms, about 1 minute. Cover bowl with plastic wrap and let dough sit at room temperature 15 minutes.

Set a small bowl of room-temperature water on work surface for easy reaching. Uncover dough, dip your hand in water and stretch one side of dough up and over to meet opposite side; turn bowl 90°. Repeat 3 more times, turning bowl 90° after each fold. (This constitutes 1 full fold.) If dough doesn’t always stretch to meet opposite side, just aim for the middle. Wet your hands and shake off excess water any time dough begins to feel sticky. Cover dough and let sit 30 minutes (set a timer). Repeat folding process to make 1 full fold. Re-cover and let dough sit another 30 minutes. Repeat folding process and resting one more time. By the last fold, dough should be significantly smoother and tighter.

Transfer dough to a generously floured surface, dust with flour, and shape into a tight ball. Dust dough with more flour. Dust a kitchen towel with flour and place inside a high-sided bowl to line. Arrange dough, seam side up, in bowl on top of towel. Tightly cover bowl with plastic wrap and let dough sit in a warm spot until puffy, about 45 minutes.

Place a rack in middle of oven, then set a large ovenproof Dutch oven with a lid on it; preheat oven to 400°. Place a 12" square of parchment paper on a flat surface. Cut a 2"-long slit in each corner toward the center. Very carefully, tip dough out onto center of parchment, trying your best not to deflate it or ruin its shape. (It’s easiest to place parchment on a flat plate so you can easily turn dough out of bowl.) Working very quickly and using a pair of kitchen shears lightly coated in vegetable oil, cut an X in center of bread. Remove pot from oven and very carefully lower dough into pot. Cover pot and return to oven.

Bake bread 35 minutes. Uncover pot, reduce oven temperature to 375°, and continue to bake bread until crust is deeply browned, 35–40 minutes more. Carefully tip loaf out onto a wire rack and let cool at least 1 hour before slicing.

Do Ahead: Bread can be baked 3 days ahead. Let cool completely; store airtight at room temperature.

#6730. How to Play GTA 6 All Weekend Without Destroying Your Body

The day has finally arrived. Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and you’ve taken that “sick day” to cause chaos on the streets of Vice City. After more than 10 years of waiting, you’re finally back in one of the franchise’s most classic locations, this time with an open world to explore. It’s not hyperbole to call this the most highly anticipated game in history. With more than 17 million preorders, that’s a lot of people who will be running from the police while trying to avoid (or rack up) six stars.

If you’re one of the many fans who’ve cleared out their schedule to binge the game this weekend, you’ll want to make sure you emerge from your haze with extremities that still function.

“The metaphor we like to share with pro players or casual gamers is the concept of the health bar,” says Dr. Matthew Hwu, owner of physical therapy clinic 1 Health Point in Monrovia, California. “The muscles and tendons of your wrist, hand, thumb, as you move the analog stick, only have so much capacity or ‘max HP’ that they can handle throughout the day.”

Below, we spoke with Dr. Hwu and other veteran gamers about how to keep your “HP” bar full for your whole weekend without developing carpal tunnel.

Playing a game for long hours puts the soft tissues in your hands and arms into overdrive. When you ramp things up, you’ll feel sore and fatigued. The most common version of this is “tendon-based problems of the wrist and hand,” says Dr. Hwu, who has spent more than 11 years working with e-sports players to help them recover from injuries. As GTA VI will be available on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series S and X, most people will be using a controller, which puts the thumbs under a lot of stress as people “control the analog stick to flip left, up, right, down; press A, B, X, Y.” The most common gaming injuries are gamer’s thumb and tennis elbow, according to the Cleveland Clinic.

So, don’t forget to take breaks. Dr. Hwu recommends at least a five-minute break for every 90 minutes played, though younger players are more resilient. If you want to push it, the key is taking longer breaks afterwards. “Two hours, 10-minute break, three hours, 15-minute break,” Dr. Hwu says. “If you want to be degenerate, you can be a smart degen.” You can also be strategic about scheduling more downtime after sequences with higher APM (actions per minute), that are more intensive on hand muscles. In essence, go touch grass after a high-stakes bank robbery.

That said, after more than a decade of waiting for what our reviewer described as “the most technically and artistically impressive game world ever made,” your instinct may be to lock in. So it’s wise to plan for breaks ahead of time. Set alarms on your phone or watch. If one goes off in the middle of a mission, commit to taking a break after crashing that helicopter. Make sure you eat and stay hydrated, and move your body, whether that’s a neighborhood walk or just a short twirl around your apartment before your next mission.

“It’s basically just your muscle and tendon tissue recovery, it’ll restore a little bit of HP over time,” Dr. Hwu says. “When you go out and walk outdoors, that can help with your overall circulation, nervous system as well. So you don’t feel like that meme where you come out of the cave and you’re like a ghost.”

Just like in other forms of training, stretching is a key part of your self-care arsenal for long hours of gaming. To loosen up for a long stretch of car-hijacking, do a few stretches before turning on your console, and during cut scenes. Your body will tell you what areas feel tight and could use some attention. But don’t forget those thumb muscles. “Bring your thumb away from the next finger, stretching it down,” Dr. Hwu says. “Every time you’re flicking back, left, or right, you might be using these muscles.”

You’ll also want to stretch your index fingers after long hours of beating ass with a crowbar while riding your motorcycle. “Every time you press your trigger,” Dr. Hwu says, “you’re using the extensors, the top side of your fingers, but also the flexors to push down.” You can also work on relaxing your forearm muscles, by extending your arm out and pushing your palm downward.

After 13 years, and a budget rumored to rival that of Dubai’s Burj Khalifa skyscraper, the new Grand Theft Auto is finally here. Our critic's verdict? It's a tour de force that reimagines what a video game can be.

Don’t forget your neck and upper back, which can feel stiff from holding onto stress after high speed chases and parachute combat. It’s so easy to load tension onto those upper traps as you shrimp further into your recliner while bird watching, or scoring a pair of black half-rim sunglasses and Eris sneakers (definitely not Nikes) for Jason (no judgment here).

“I will do small things: roll my neck from side to side, stretch my traps by sitting on one hand and pulling my head [in the] opposite direction,” Calliope Ryder, platform director at GYLD, previously a producer on Tales of the Shire, says. “[I] tense my calves in spurts to help with blood flow, or stand up and let my body fold at the hips while swaying side to side with my arms crossed. Again, alerts to remind me to do it are necessary or I'll go hours without noticing.”

Meanwhile, using a heating pad or an ice pack on a sore area (like your lower back) can help with any nagging dull pain you’re feeling. Heat loosens up stiff muscles, while ice can help dull the achy sensation. They won’t build your long-term strength, but they’ll make you feel better in the moment.

If you’re old enough for back pain, then you’re old enough to have to consider video game ergonomics. Wherever you’re gaming, Dr. Hwu’s recommendation is simple: Sit with your legs at a 90-degree angle, feet planted on the ground, back appropriately supported. Then put a pillow on your lap and rest your arms on it. This helps prevent forearm strain and hunching over. You can even haul your expensive Aeron from your office into the living room, rather than rotting on the sofa and ruining your back.

There are also a number of helpful gaming peripherals. Maddy Myers, editor in chief and co-founder of video game publication Mothership, uses a specific gaming pillow from a brand called Mechanism. “The pillow allows you to have the handheld device suspended in the air in a comfortable, adjustable spot so that you don’t tire out your arms from holding it up,” she says. “You can also put a controller on there.”

Using the device helps her get through play stretches of eight hours a day on the weekends, “while taking breaks to eat, do chores, stretch.” It’s been especially invaluable during high-intensity work periods. “I have to play GTA 6 for my job, so I am really going to be relying on these tools to keep my arms and body feeling as close to normal as possible,” she says.

Don’t let a terribly designed controller be the reason you develop carpal tunnel. Julia Lee, section lead for video game guides at Polygon, plays at least two hours of games a day, and will sometimes fit in 10-hour sessions. Her wrists started hurting and “those flat [Nintendo Switch] Joy-Cons were directly to blame,” she says. “I ended up getting a Nintendo Switch Pro Controller (which has a much more ergonomic shape and grip), and that helped alleviate pain a lot.” She also reminds herself not to hold the controller so tight, as it causes pain.

As Dr. Hwu explains, some controllers have staggered sticks, while others have an inline design. It all comes down to individual comfort and muscular endurance. He’s helped pro Call of Duty players modify their analog sticks, and has coached people to avoid death-gripping the controller. “If you use a claw grip position, an awkward grip, or a modded controller, that might lead to more stress for certain muscles in your wrist and hand,” he says.

If your hands are really killing you, there’s also the option of switching to another controller style altogether. “Moving two to four of my most awkward hand-based actions from my hands to my feet allows me to play longer,” John Warren, chief marketing officer and cofounder of Glydr, a foot-based video game controller, says. Warren has minicore disease, which causes muscle weakness. “Anytime you have an opportunity to stop overloading your hands while you play, you give your hands a little reprieve,” he says, “it makes your high-precision hand actions more accurate.”

Lastly, if you have existing injuries, and your medical provider has prescribed a brace, actually follow their guidance for how and when to use it. For my part, I use thumb braces called the Push Metagrips, to help support my floppy thumb joints anytime I plan on playing video games for long stretches.

So you’ve done all this work to binge GTA 6, and you’re feeling solid but a little sore. But you want to play for longer periods, snipe with greater accuracy, and fuck around with less fatigue. Consider going to the gym.

Doing exercises for those small muscles and tendons can help you build up that tolerance, like any other form of weight and endurance training. “When you rest, ice, massage, kinesio tape, you know, passive interventions, bracing, all of these things can restore your HP,” Dr. Hwu says. “It does nothing to change the size of your health bar, which represents the tissue’s capacity or your endurance. The way we do that is you can do specific exercises to build up or create a buffer for how much you’re going to handle.”

For any chronic pain, injuries, or conditions, it’s important to work with a physical therapist. I’m currently working with one for repetitive stress injury recovery, and doing exercises specific to playing video games. I do slow wrist curls with a two-pound weight, rotator cuff exercises with a resistance band, and squeeze an egg-shaped stress ball for thumb and forearm endurance. I also train my shoulders and core.

For those who aren’t managing any injuries, training those finger and arm muscles make it easier to game for longer periods. Consistency is key, and so is having reasonable expectations. Dr. Hwu recommends starting once a day, three to four times a week, “on a day where you’re not doing a lot, and then you’ll learn how your body responds to doing exercises within the context of your specific life, and you can make modifications to scale up the aggressiveness or scale down,” he says. As for seeing progress, “it takes four to six weeks for muscles, eight weeks for tendons.”

And don’t forget the stabilizer muscles in your shoulders, which hold up your upper body and support your arms. “I focus on ‘chest opening’ exercises that strengthen your back,” Ryder says. “I tend towards curving forward so I consciously strengthen the 'opposite' muscles so they pull my shoulders back a bit.” She also does chin tucks, to strengthen her deep neck flexors.

Strength training is also a great way to tackle back pain, another common complaint after gaming for hours. “A few years ago I started having extreme back pain when I sat in my office chair,” Gita Jackson, cofounder of video game publication Aftermath, says. They play one to two hours of games a day, but often play marathon sessions when new games come out. “The pain was excruciating, and sometimes my legs would get so stiff I couldn’t walk. Recovery started with physical therapy, and then a new ergonomic chair,” and now they regularly attend pilates.

“I really had no idea that if I wanted to continue playing noodley strategy games without pain that I would have to be a person who works out multiple times a week,” they say.

Maybe you’ve done a little too much, and your arms start to feel heavy as bricks, with thumbs that ache for hours. Even with the best precautions in place, it can happen to any of us.

“If it gets to a point where cumulatively, over days, symptoms are getting worse, maybe reflect and identify what you could change,” Dr. Hwu says. “If things are consistently sharp, or you start to feel some weakness, where when you go and try to pick up something, you’ll want to drop it. That’s a sign that it may be time to make a change in some of the variables that you can control.”

In 2002, weeks before the release of Grand Theft Auto: Vice City, the journalist David Kushner met Sam and Dan Houser, plus Rockstar co-founder Terry Donovan, at their SoHo loft. It would be their last and only in-depth sit-down before they decided that the world’s most successful video game required little explanation.

This is when paying attention to your daily habits is key. Instead of freaking out over hand pain, and deciding you need to quit playing cold turkey, you can instead think about what you did during the day that might have contributed to your discomfort. “How much you’re playing, what intensity you’re playing, are you taking breaks, are you exercising, how are you responding to the pain?” Dr. Hwu says. These are all factors in why you might be having pain, and all things to consider modifying. If it truly is tendon fatigue, then the key is finding the right volume of use, rather than quitting altogether.

There are also some signs to look out for that may require medical intervention. Dr. Hwu pointed me to 1 Health Point’s website, which suggests seeking a doctor’s opinion if your wrist looks swollen or bruised, if you can’t move or bear any weight, if your symptoms worsen or spread with rest, or if you experience constant hand numbness, especially if it comes with weakness.

Gaming for long hours ultimately comes down to using your common sense alongside your precious free will. Pay attention to your habits. Commit to taking breaks and stretching between bank robberies and doomscrolling through Snapmatic (in-game social media). Do some preventative exercises while planning out your next mission—with a soundtrack that includes Bad Bunny and Dua Lipa, as well as some ’80s bangers, you’ve already got the music for it. Your hands and arms will thank you, even if the regular people of Leonida probably won’t.

#6728. Patrick Schwarzenegger Says This Small Detail Makes All The Difference In A Great Martini (Exclusive)

Three years with one of vodka's oldest families will apparently teach you a few things.

Patrick Schwarzenegger knows a thing or two about a good martini. After three years working with Ketel One and spending time with the family behind the vodka, the White Lotus actor has learned that some people take their cocktails very seriously.

"I'm not that severe," Schwarzenegger told me, although he's developed a few preferences of his own. His biggest? "A frosted glass. A cold glass I think makes all the difference."

I caught up with Schwarzenegger in Schiedam, Netherlands, during a Ketel One-hosted visit to the historic Nolet Distillery, where the family has been making spirits since 1691. The trip was actually his idea. After hearing so much about the distillery from the Nolet family, Schwarzenegger wanted to see everything firsthand, even getting his hands dirty by shoveling coal into the distilling equipment.

According to Schwarzenegger, family members have gotten so particular about their drinks that they'll check where a bar stores its vermouth, olives, and white wine before ordering. If certain ingredients have been sitting out rather than refrigerated, they might opt for a drier martini.

Bob Nolet sees that same attention to detail in the way people order cocktails today. "Today's martini drinkers know what they want," he told Delish, whether that's a classic dry martini, something dirty, or an espresso version.

Of course, that hasn't stopped bartenders from seeing just how far they can stretch the definition of a martini. Schwarzenegger told me he tried The Corner Store’s viral sour cream-and-onion version largely because he'd heard so much about it, something he deemed “really good.”

The Tomatini was another story. He appreciated how convincingly it captured tomato flavor, but the novelty only went so far. "I think that I could have that for a few sips, maybe one max, but then I'm tapped out of that," he said.

His more dependable order is an espresso martini, something the Nolets know particularly well. Schwarzenegger revealed that the family even arranged for espresso machines to be sent to his wedding in Idaho, giving guests a way to enjoy his favorite cocktail during the celebration.

For Carl Nolet Jr., the connection goes deeper than a shared appreciation for espresso martinis. "Patrick comes from a family that is equally committed to their craft in the entertainment space," he told Delish. "You can see that commitment through his career and how he brings his legacy into a new generation."

Carl also credits Schwarzenegger with offering a younger perspective on cocktail culture, particularly how drinking has become part of the way people socialize, host, and celebrate.

For someone who knows his way around a cocktail, Schwarzenegger doesn't actually drink all that often. Between filming, fitness, and work, he's made a rule to keep alcohol to weekends. He's also taken an interest in the growing demand for better-for-you options, pointing to canned cocktails with less sugar and real fruit juice as examples of how people's drinking habits are changing.

That curiosity extends well beyond the bar. With his involvement in the protein snack company MOSH, Schwarzenegger has become the kind of person who considers wandering supermarket aisles a perfectly good Friday night activity. One of his first stops after arriving in the Netherlands was a grocery store, and he loves comparing what's available in different places, from Los Angeles to wife Abby Champion's home state of Alabama.

His Instagram algorithm isn't helping matters, either. Food, drinks, random syrups—if an ad catches his attention, there's a decent chance it'll end up on his doorstep. "I buy everything," he admitted.

Interestingly, all that enthusiasm for trying new things doesn't necessarily translate to elaborate dinners. He and Champion love exploring restaurants together, but they're equally happy cooking at home or keeping things simple. She's a fan of Chick-fil-A and In-N-Out, while he's perfectly content with a plate of chicken and rice.

After talking martinis with Schwarzenegger and the Nolets, I might start paying a little more attention to what happens behind the bar. I'll leave the vermouth inspections to the professionals, but keeping the rest of my martini on ice until I'm ready for a fresh, frosty glass? That's a level of service I could get used to.

#6727. Tom Ford Resort 2027

“I’ve been criticized for objectifying women,” Tom Ford ventured to The Guardian in 2015: “But I’m an equal opportunity objectifier—I’m just as happy to objectify men. The thing is, you can’t show male nudity in our culture in the way you can show female nudity. We’re very comfortable as a culture exploiting women, but not men. But I don’t think of it as exploitation [either way].”

Two years later, Ford returned to the point in New York Magazine, observing: “I’ve objectified men just as much in my career, but you just cannot run those images. I put that perfume bottle between a woman’s breasts, but I also put it between a guy’s butt cheeks, but [few] would accept that.”

A decade later, Ford’s successor, Haider Ackermann, is still flicking away at the same, still-sensitive cultural double standard, apparently to advocate for equal-opportunity objectification. Following a runway show featuring what the designer termed “ass out” menswear looks, Nicole Phelps observed in her Vogue Runway review: “Ackermann just about out-Forded Tom Ford with this one.”

In this pre-spring lookbook-delivered collection for women—and its simultaneously released brother lookbook for men—Ackermann went all in on symmetry. Through both pose and clothes he placed a mirror between the genders that broadly reflected similarity, while playing with the nuance of difference. As he observed in one of his prepared statements about these collections: “The deeper I delve into the signs, the more I find freedom in interpreting them.”

That interpretation began with Fordian tailoring that appeared especially “correct” in its severity. Double-breasted jackets were cut with strong shoulders, wiry waists, and generous pants, sometimes in pinstripes or dogtooth. Suiting was regularly broken, as with a checked jacket layered over a striped shirt and black leather pants. Elsewhere, the tailoring’s structure was tempered by the liquid flow of silk and the occasional jolt of brilliant color. Tailoring was similarly commanding in menswear, although there its expression was more consistently rooted in the traditions of masculine dress: Ackermann seemed to be toying delicately with the tensions of tailoring’s masculine convention to redistribute its power.

Away from tailoring Ackermann returned to American sportswear and louche, casually-precise dressing. Cropped leather jackets, suede bombers, denim, and sharply fitted leather pencil skirts all had an inherent ruggedness that was made highly refined through fabrication and fit. The collection expanded into languid, bias-cut dresses, fluid evening gowns, and body-skimming silhouettes in silk and sheer fabrics. The discipline of tailoring gave way to a more liquid glamour—very Fordian—sometimes punctuated by the in-between punctuation of underwear.

In the accompanying men’s collection, similar ingredients were rearranged to related effect: the two closely adjacent wardrobes were presented as variations of two closely adjacent personalities whose greatest point of difference was gender. In both lookbooks, tailored jackets were also worn over boxer shorts, with the models shot in parallel poses. The between-knee perspective of multiple, house-specialty crotch shots of similar looks in the two lookbooks made this founder-established, equal-opportunity approach to objectification eminently clear. Ackermann wrote that this season’s woman was “self-possessed, confident in her sensuality, unwilling to be defined by a single attitude.”

#6726. Toasted Orzo Chicken Soup

Chicken noodle soup just got way more interesting. Inspired by Egyptian bird tongue soup, this warming spice-tinged chicken broth is as comforting as it is flavorful. The real star here is the orzo. Toasted in butter until it’s deeply golden and nutty-fragrant, the pasta takes on a new personality. Unlike plain noodles, which quickly go limp in hot soup, well-toasted orzo stays al dente for longer, holding its shape and texture until you’ve doled out every bowl.

Any store-bought chicken broth, stock, or bouillon paste offers an extra boost of flavor to the base that chicken thighs and water alone can’t compete with—for an even more robust option, go for chicken bone broth.

More nourishing soup recipes this way: Curried Yellow Squash Soup; Broken Lasagna Noodle Soup With Spinach; Spicy Cod and Potato Chowder.

Season 1¼ lb. skinless, boneless chicken thighs (4–5) all over with 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt.

Heat 3 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large pot over medium. Add 1 tsp. crushed fennel seeds, ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper, ½ tsp. ground cardamom, and ¼ tsp. ground cloves and cook, stirring, until fragrant, about 20 seconds. Add chicken, one 1" piece ginger, peeled, finely grated, 4 cups low-sodium chicken broth or chicken stock, and 3 cups water. Bring to a simmer over medium-high heat. Partially cover pot, reduce heat to medium-low, and simmer until an instant-read thermometer inserted into thickest part of a thigh registers at least 165°, 15–18 minutes. Transfer chicken to a cutting board and let cool slightly. Reduce heat to low and cover pot. Using 2 forks, shred chicken.

Melt 4 Tbsp. (½ stick) unsalted butter in a large skillet over medium heat. Add 1 cup orzo and cook, stirring often, until golden brown and fragrant, about 5 minutes. Remove from heat and stir in 1 Tbsp. finely grated lemon zest, ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and remaining ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper.

Scrape orzo mixture into pot with broth. Increase heat to medium and simmer broth, uncovered, until orzo is just al dente, 8–10 minutes. Remove soup from heat and add shredded chicken, 5 oz. baby kale or spinach, and 3 Tbsp. fresh lemon juice. Stir until greens are just wilted. Taste and season with more salt if needed.

#6725. I Just Doubled My Blush Collection With a Single Product

SHEGLAM’s new gray blush makes me feel like a pro makeup artist.

Personally, I’m thrilled that cool-toned makeup is making a comeback. I lived through the warm-toned trend, when it seemed like every palette featured varying shades of orange, orange, and more orange—none of which complement my fair complexion with pink undertones. Now, I’m taking advantage of the latest, cool-toned trend by enhancing my eyes with silver shadow, swiping on milky pink lip glosses, and even transforming my warm, summer blushes into flattering winter-ready shades with the help of the new SHEGLAM Squeeze & Dot Matte Paint Blush in Gray-tiful.

Yes, the blush is actually gray in the tube, but that isn’t as scary as you may think. In fact, it’s quickly becoming the MVP of my makeup bag. Not meant to be worn alone, it’s designed to blend with other blush shades to neutralize bright colors, cool off warm tones, and help you customize the intensity of your blush. I can combine it with any of my cream or liquid blushes, including the rest of the shades in the new SHEGLAM Squeeze & Dot Paint Blush collection in matte and shimmer. Suddenly, I’m getting so much more mileage out of blushes that I used to only reach for during the warmer months, when my complexion is naturally sun-kissed and I feel more pulled toward more vibrant looks.

Gray-tiful is one of eight Squeeze & Dot Matte Paint Blush collection, which also includes shades of peach, mauve, pink, and red. The formulas are infused with hyaluronic acid and glycerin to keep cheeks soft and hydrated. The squeeze-tube dotter makes it easy to pinpoint application exactly where I want it, whether that’s high up on my cheekbones, on the apples of my cheeks, or on my chin and nose for an all-over flush.

Here are my three favorite ways to use Gray-tiful, below.

SHEGLAM Squeeze & Dot Matte Paint Blushes such as Chili Rouge, a candy apple red, and Love Cake, a bright pink, are impressively pigmented. For me, this makes them perfect for special occasions, but for running errands or casual hangs with friends, I prefer a softer look. When I mix them with a dot of Gray-tiful using a dense bristle brush, these bold shades become more subtle, which matches my low-key, everyday vibe.

There’s something so pretty and whimsical about a frosty cheek, especially in the winter. I like to apply two dots of the SHEGLAM Shimmer Paint Blush in the peachy Pink About You on my cheekbones, then add one dot of Gray-tiful, and blend. The result is a luminous, cool-toned flush that catches the light beautifully.

This has to be my favorite way to use Gray-tiful; I feel like an artist mixing up my own custom hues. This is a time for creativity and experimentation, so there’s no one right way to mix colors. I like to squeeze a dab of Gray-tiful on the back of my hand, then add one, two, or even three blush colors on top and mix them all together to see what I can create. Recently, I transformed the Matte Paint Blush in Heart Wish, a warm mauve with two dabs of Gray-tiful and the tiniest hint of Pink About You. The result? My dream winter blush shade.

#6722. Tom Ford Resort 2027 Menswear

“I’ve been criticized for objectifying women,” Tom Ford ventured to The Guardian in 2015: “But I’m an equal opportunity objectifier—I’m just as happy to objectify men. The thing is, you can’t show male nudity in our culture in the way you can show female nudity. We’re very comfortable as a culture exploiting women, but not men. But I don’t think of it as exploitation [either way].”

Two years later, Ford returned to the point in New York Magazine, observing: “I’ve objectified men just as much in my career, but you just cannot run those images. I put that perfume bottle between a woman’s breasts, but I also put it between a guy’s butt cheeks, but [few] would accept that.”

A decade later, Ford’s successor, Haider Ackermann, is still flicking away at the same, still-sensitive cultural double standard, apparently also to advocate for equal-opportunity objectification. Following a runway show featuring what the designer termed “ass out” menswear looks, Nicole Phelps observed in her Vogue Runway review: “Ackermann just about out-Forded Tom Ford with this one.”

In this pre-spring lookbook-delivered collection for men—and its simultaneously released sister lookbook for women—Ackermann went all in on symmetry. Through both pose and clothes he placed a mirror between the genders that broadly reflected similarity, while playing with the nuance of difference. As he observed in one of his prepared statements about these collections: “The deeper I delve into the signs, the more I find freedom in interpreting them.”

That interpretation began with Fordian tailoring that appeared especially “correct” in its severity. Double-breasted jackets were cut with strong shoulders, wiry waists, and slimmish sometimes slightly-kicked pants. Skinny scarves fronded around necklines. There were tuxedos and jacquard cocktail jackets, sometimes with eveningwear sparkle. An amazing tufted scarlet rococo jacquard jacket was layered over a monochrome silk snakeprint shirt. In another look a dogtooth jacket was worn over puppytooth pants. Tailoring was similarly commanding in womenswear, punctuated by a more overtly fluid sensibility. Ackermann seemed to be toying delicately with the tensions of tailoring’s masculine convention to redistribute its power.

Away from tailoring Ackermann returned to American sportswear and louche, casually-precise dressing. Cropped leather jackets in green and turquoise suede, checked Western shirts, and very straight-cut denim all came with an inherent ruggedness that was highly refined through fabrication and fit. There was top-to-toe leather and all-corduroy prep. In the accompanying women’s collection, similar ingredients were rearranged to related effect: the two closely adjacent wardrobes were presented as variations of two closely adjacent personalities whose greatest point of difference was gender.

In both lookbooks, tailored jackets were also worn over boxer shorts, with the models shot in parallel poses. The between-knee perspective of multiple, house-specialty crotch shots of similar looks across the two lookbooks made Ackermann’s fealty to Ford’s equal-opportunity approach eminently clear. In his menswear statement, Ackermann said: “I like the idea that a man does not need to choose between different expressions of himself. I keep following this intuition, wherever it may lead.”

#6721. Upside-Down Apple Cider Cheesecake

If you feel there’s never enough graham cracker crust with your cheesecake, then this version is for you. The entire no-bake cheesecake is surrounded by crust, so there are buttery graham crackers and creamy cheesecake in every single bite. The exterior isn’t just hiding a silky-smooth cheesecake filling, but also a surprise syrupy apple filling. If you want to win the holidays this year, set this unassuming dessert on the table and watch everyone ooh and ahh as they cut in and discover the sweet, spiced, and completely delicious treat hiding inside.

How to make it upside-down:To achieve a double layer of crust, you need to freeze one layer so that it’s easy to place on top of the cheesecake. Before serving, you’ll flip the whole thing onto a plate, which puts the seam on the bottom and gives you a beautiful display of a solid crust. It’s easier than you think to flip it, and the parchment will ensure nothing sticks to the springform pan.

Make ahead & storage:I love to make this dessert during the holidays because you can prep and make it all ahead of time. The apple filling can be made up to 3 days ahead, and the assembled cheesecake can be made up to 2 days ahead of when you want to serve.

#6720. Chocolate Strawberry Nails Are the Moody-Sweet Manicure Trend to Try Next

Think mocha, pinky brown, and deep berry tones with a just-dipped shine.

We’ve had espresso nails, almond milk nails, and now we’re savoring chocolate strawberry nails. So what exactly are they? Think of anything in the family of mocha shades, pinky browns, and deep berry tones. It’s the color you get when a ripe strawberry takes a dip in melted chocolate.

The look works because it balances warm and cool. The rich brown base keeps things grounded and neutral, while the berry undertones add a soft flush of color that feels fresh rather than flat. It’s a shade that flatters nearly every skin tone, and it’s subtle enough for the office yet moody enough for a night out. For an extra touch, try a glossy top coat to mimic that just-dipped chocolate shine, or add a thin chrome or sheer pink accent nail for a little dimension.

Below, we’ve rounded up some fun designs to get you inspired for your next chocolate strawberry manicure.

From dreamy cotton-candy pink to a deep, jammy berry, these rosy shades capture every stage of a juicy strawberry, from first blush to fully ripe.

This nail set looks like creamy milk chocolate glazed under a frosty chrome finish.

#6719. Charles Melton Is Still the King of Gigantic Pants

Charles Melton has long loved big, baggy pants. Back in 2024, the actor landed on GQ’s annual list of best pants thanks to one extremely voluminous pair from the Japanese label Sillage. And if his fits this fall are any indication, Melton won’t be stepping away from the assertively wide bottoms anytime soon. In fact, he keeps wearing one fantastically huge pair after another.

The Her Private Hell star’s latest giant-trouser run kicked off in mid-October at the star-studded Academy Gala in Los Angeles. While A-list dressers like A$AP Rocky and Jacob Elordi showed up in respectfully roomy pants, Melton upped the ante in an ERL tuxedo that left no doubt about his dedication to the slouch. His cropped-front tailcoat was neat as a pin and fit like a glove, but his slacks were dramatically roomy and artfully rumpled—parachute pants gone black tie.

Later that same month, he popped up in Texas for the F1 Grand Prix in a very Fashion Dude outfit: Louis Vuitton workwear jacket, sporty sunglasses, bucket hat, and some choice Wales Bonner x Adidas Sambas. And on his legs? A twisted-seam silhouette billowy enough to make a scrum of downtown stylists and creative directors blush. ​

But Melton saved his biggest look of all for last week in New York. At a VIP dinner for the South Korean eyewear label Gentle Monster, he donned a matching set from Viviano, the Tokyo-based label from designer Viviano Sue. Cut from gabardine as dark as a moonless night, both his Harrington jacket and balloon trousers looked as though they’d been blown up to the size of a weather balloon and then deflated, leaving behind all the crinkles and creases. He contrasted the dramatic ensemble with a pair of tiny oval sunglasses, which capped the whole thing off just right.

Even as menswear’s ideal pant fit moves towards a relaxed straight leg, some guys who dove deep into the baggy waters aren’t quite ready to give their roomy fits up just yet. Melton clearly loves the look and wears it well. And when your gigantic trousers help you stand out in a crowd as effortlessly and stylishly as his do, why bother changing?

#6718. An Azzedine Alaïa Exhibition in San Francisco Makes a Case for the Human Touch

It’s possible to view “Sculpting Fashion”—the new Azzedine Alaïa exhibition at San Francisco’s Legion of Honor museum—as a kind of benevolent Trojan Horse that has infiltrated big tech’s ground zero with a dazzling demonstration of human capability and physicality. “There’s no one anywhere in the world that can touch him in terms of construction. And no one else makes your body look better,” says model Veronica Webb in a vintage clip included in a film made for the show.

To be clear, the exhibition isn’t “anti” anything. “Azzedine for sure would’ve been fascinated [by AI] because he was fascinated by the telephone,” said Carla Sozzani, president of the Fondation Azzedine Alaïa. The designer, who once said couture necessitated a “state-of-the-art spirit,” did indeed use technology as a tool, working with Italian knitwear specialists on technical fabrics and the like, but what is breathtaking in this show is his manual savoir-faire.

The first American retrospective of the designer’s work, “Sculpting Fashion,” was curated by the Legion of Honor’s Laura L. Camerlengo with Olivier Saillard, director of the designer’s foundation, and it anticipates the tenth anniversary of the designer’s passing in 2017. Alaïa had a relationship with the museum, having visited in 2005 when he received an honorary doctorate from the Academy of Art University.

Before his death, Alaïa established a protocol for exhibiting his work that Saillard and the foundation stick to. The designer had clear, headless forms based on Naomi Campbell’s body created for displaying clothes. They stand on raised plinths, taking on a monumental quality and fixing attention on Alaïa’s construction and fit and materials. In “Sculpting Fashion” this focus is uninterrupted by any of the ephemera—photographs, magazine clippings, etc.—that has become common in fashion displays. What’s interesting here is how, as with “Art and Fashion” at the Louvre last year, garments have taken up residence in the art galleries. A baroque beaded dress in black and red has been placed among religious paintings and under a 15th-century decorative ceiling that was once part of a Spanish palace, and two coquettish looks in celadon green and white keep company with a sofa once owned by Marie Antoinette (and recently acquired by the museum) in a room devoted to the 18th century. Visitors who haven’t come to the Legion of Honor for “Sculpting Fashion” will encounter his work in the first floor galleries; the main exhibition is displayed downstairs.

Grace Jones in Alaïa’s sprigng 1986 couture show.

Azzedine Alaïa and Grace Jones at the Fashion Oscars, 1985.

Grace Jones plays hostess to the show in absentia. The first, spotlit, piece on display is the lilac, hooded jersey bandage dress the performer and model wore when Alaïa picked up two awards at the Oscars de la Mode in Paris in 1985. Behind and surrounding that wonder are other designs arranged in a manner that feels almost Grecian. What’s really on display is the divine feminine as unleashed by an autodidact designer whose work seems to defy time—the result, in part, of his evolutionary, rather than trend-driven, approach to design. “The clothes I make can not be dated,” Alaïa once said. “They are made to last.”

Born in Tunisia in 1935, Alaïa enrolled at the École des Beaux Arts school in Tunis to study sculpture at 15. “When I realized I could not become a great sculptor, I turned myself to fashion,” says Sozzani, quoting the designer in the exhibition film. Alaïa’s twin sister Hafida taught him to sew and over time he became a dressmaker, a practice he’d continue in Paris, where he moved in 1956. Alaïa worked briefly with some of the French maisons, but his real education came from living breathing women, the performers he costumed at the Crazy Horse, friends, models, and clients. He was also tutored by the greats of fashion history. The designer amassed a collection of 15,000 historical garments over his lifetime, some of which he took apart, but most of which he lovingly preserved. Some of these are on loan to the Legion of Honor and are displayed in a separate room from Alaïa’s designs. “He believed true innovation required an understanding of the past,” Camerlengo explains.

Azzedine Alaïa’s Archetypes: Red knit dress, spring 1992 ready-to-wear

The classicism of Alaïa’s work, the sense that it exists in continuity with what’s come before, is especially resonant in this destabilizing era of technological expansion. In the museum’s Rodin gallery where two column dresses are on view, “you can see a sculpture, which is a huge hand, and you see the clothes. It reminds me that Azzedine spent his life doing something with his hands,” says Saillard.

The second room of the exhibition speaks to Alaïa’s relationship with America. In 1982 the designer, then still relatively unknown, was invited by Dawn Mello to do an in-store show at Bergdorf Goodman; it was a raging success. Camerlengo stresses here how Alaïa’s designs spoke to more assertive and sexualized expressions of feminism in the ’80s, following the fitting-in approach women entering the workforce had adopted the previous decade. “After a decade of concealing their bodies in androgynous clothing in male-dominated corporate spaces,” the curator writes, “professional women flocked to Alaïa’s formfitting clothes, made in supple leathers and stretch knits that celebrated the female form.”

Azzedine Alaïa and the United States: Zippered denum dress, spring 1986 ready-to-wear

The two arteries of couture, tailoring and flou, are addressed in the third room. Here, Alaïa’s rather dandy suiting shares space with romantic pieces inspired by the 18th century. Saillard recounted that the designer had moved into a building where Madame du Pompadour (the mistress of Louis XV) had lived, and she was an inspiration for his spring 1992 collection.

All sorts of couture techniques are on display in the next room. While the Legion of Honor’s collection is mainly European, in “Sculpting Fashion” the influence of Egypt and Africa are visible. Camerlengo makes a connection between the designer’s cut leathers and mashirabya, the latticework screens of North Africa, for example.

An Enduring Legacy: Dress worn by Tina Turner, spring 1989 couture

Azzedine Alaïa and Tina Turner, Paris, 1989. Photograph by Peter Lindbergh.

Alaïa was also greatly influenced by the movies, to which his grandparents frequently took him as a child, and the final room is dedicated to all-out glamour. It’s here that the gold bead dress he made for Tina Turner holds court among other stunners, some that pay homage to the ’30s and others that demonstrate why Alaïa was dubbed “The King of Cling.”

“Sculpting Fashion” is on view at the Legion of Honor from November 21, 2026 through April 11, 2027.

#6717. French Onion Croissant Strata

A breakfast strata is one of our go-tos when we want a hearty, shareable brunch dish, but it’s hard to put into words how good this French onion croissant strata is. It takes a few extra minutes to deeply caramelize the onions, but the extra flavor and richness make a HUGE difference.

Making it French Onion:Caramelizing the onions over medium heat helps them brown evenly and consistently. If the onions begin to stick or burn, add a splash of water to bring them back! Garlic, thyme, and black pepper are the aromatic unsung heroes in the onions, so you don’t want to skip them. Deglazing with white wine is essential in any French onion recipe, so use the one you’d sip! Once the wine has reduced, add some beef stock to impart meaty French onion soup flavor. The caramelized onions then get layered with torn croissants and Gruyère cheese, and topped with dollops of cream cheese.

Delish Official:Eggland's Best Eggs are the Official Eggs of Delish. That means they're the only eggs we're using in our recipe content.

Storage:The beautiful thing about a breakfast strata is that you can make it ahead. Store in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 1 day and reheat until the internal temperature reaches 165°. This is also great to portion and store for breakfast at the office.
