#6716. Lafayette 148’s Emily Smith on 30 Years in Fashion and Finding Her Uniform

In ELLE’s Office Hours series, we ask people in powerful positions about their first jobs, worst jobs, and everything in between. This month, we sat down with Lafayette 148 creative director Emily Smith as the New York-based fashion house celebrates its 30th anniversary. Smith has spent more than two decades at the helm of the brand, working her way up from a part-time freelancer to creative lead. But her fashion ambitions began long before she arrived in New York. Growing up in Atlanta, she ran her own neighborhood art camp before discovering designers like Donna Karan and realizing fashion could be a career. Now, Smith is celebrating the women who have shaped Lafayette 148 through its “Uniform” campaign, which highlights accomplished New Yorkers and their individual approaches to personal style. Below, Smith reflects on her unconventional start, why she’s stayed at the company for over 20 years, and the career advice she wishes she’d followed sooner.

I ran a camp in my neighborhood because, at the time, I thought I wanted to be an art teacher. It was basically babysitting all the neighborhood kids, but I created a flyer, got paid for it, and did the whole thing. I started in second grade, and we did it every year for about eight years. I even started hiring people so we could have two classes going at once. We had swimming lessons. The local news even came and did a segment on it. I loved decorating my classroom and setting everything up. My parents were so nice to give me the garage and basement to do it all. Not necessarily an official job, but my first money-making moment.

I was a bagger at Kroger. It just was not for me.

I was always creative growing up. At a certain point, I discovered Donna Karan. It’s kind of crazy when you see somebody doing something and suddenly realize, Oh, that’s a career. I can do that. I became obsessed with everything in the ’90s, all the New York fashion at the time. That was when I decided I wanted to pursue it. When college applications came around, my mom really encouraged me to go to art school. I started in fashion, and that became my focus. Growing up in Atlanta, I wasn’t necessarily surrounded by a big fashion community. But my dad’s office was across the street from the art museum at one point, and I remember going there and having that exposure. My mom was always crafty and creative, too. She really encouraged us to be who we were.

There’s a language creative people have that’s a little different from the way business people communicate. When I went to art school, I felt like I’d found my people. It wasn’t just fashion people. It was photographers, fine artists, and all these different kinds of creatives. Being around people who thought about things differently was really inspiring. Even today, I thrive around people who have creative insight. There’s always a little more depth to the way they see things.

I interned at Bill Blass for about six months. I moved to New York in July 2001, and we had our fashion show on September 10. I remember Marc Jacobs had his show that day, too, and everybody was celebrating. People were saying, “Just come a little late to work tomorrow.” And then, obviously, September 11 happened. A week later, the entire team was let go, from the top down. What I learned from that experience was the fragility of everything. Don’t take anything for granted. Anything can come and go in a moment. But I also worked with Lars Nilsson and Hervé Pierre Braillard, who were just the kindest people. This industry has a little bit of everything, but I loved that they led with kindness, even when they were under so much pressure. As an intern, I was literally carrying ironing boards down the street and doing a sketch here or there. It was very much about doing whatever needed to be done.

After my internship, I had a brief stint of freelancing. Those were some long months. I remember coming home from one job and my roommates asking how my day was. I said, “Well, I was putting pigs on socks.” I knew I wouldn’t be going back. Freelancing is hard. It’s a grind, but you meet a lot of great people. I actually made some really good contacts during that time. I eventually met Deirdre [Quinn], Lafayette 148’s founder. She met with me on a Saturday, which I thought was incredible. I was really just looking for advice. I wasn’t looking for a job, and I didn’t even know much about the brand. It was only five years old. She was so kind, full of knowledge, and passionate. After I spent a year freelancing and meeting people who seemed so over the industry, her enthusiasm reminded me why I wanted to do this in the first place. She offered me a part-time freelance position, and the rest is history.

In the beginning, security was really important to me. The idea of losing a job and having to pay rent was scary, so that was definitely part of it initially. But really, it was the growth. Within a couple of years, they were sending me to the factory. It was a small, tight-knit company, so I was able to wear a lot of different hats. I had a mentor named Barbara who was traveling back and forth from Italy, and I was learning so much from her. I’d compare notes with friends in the industry and realize how much hands-on experience I was getting. I understood the value of that and really loved being so close to the product. It never got boring. And whenever things did get slightly boring, I’d ask, “Okay, what’s next?” And there was always something. It’s such an entrepreneurial place that you can almost create your next opportunity. That’s what made it so exciting.

I think if you asked Deirdre, she’d have a different answer because she has her own experience of building the company. For me, it’s about having started at a company that was really a factory and being part of the journey of building its identity and what it stands for. I was the kid running up and down the factory floors collecting samples. That hustle and bustle was so innate to who the brand was. To me, it was really exciting to build more of a brand story around that. Thirty years later, we’re still celebrating those roots and making sure we haven’t strayed too far from them, while continuing to evolve. Being part of that journey has been really exciting.

The constant has always been this idea of an accomplished woman. She’s always been at the forefront of who we’re designing for. At the time, it was the career woman. But now, what does that even mean anymore? What does that look like? There are so many different versions of that woman and so many different ways people dress. At the heart of it, she’s a woman who knows what she wants. She’s busy and has a really full life, whether she’s starting her career or ending it. They can all find something in the clothes.

For us, it was about choosing people who inspire us. Number one, they had to be New York women. That was the base. We wanted women who make us want to be better ourselves. From someone like Deborah Berke, the architect, to women who have built incredible careers in other fields, it’s important to shine a light on them. I think back to when I was a kid and how much you need that kind of inspiration. Even at a certain stage in your career, when you’re wondering what the point is, it’s nice to have people who inspire you. These are women who have really built something for themselves, and I think that’s worth celebrating.

Sophie de Rakoff said something during the project that I really liked, and I’m going to steal it: “Dressing with intention.” When you know yourself, you know what works on you. It’s no longer about dressing to impress. There’s a confidence in knowing what works for you and making it work. We’re all so busy. Who has time to overthink getting dressed? You know what works, and you build a system around that. You’re able to create a wardrobe that really works for you. Everybody has a different definition of a uniform, and that was the whole point of the project.

When I first started in the industry, I was a total hippie. I was making my own clothes, and they were terrible. Absolutely falling apart. It was what it was. I think part of the change comes from not having as much time as you did when you were younger. But it’s also about simplicity and comfort. I know that if I’m going to be on the floor pinning something but also have to give a presentation, there are certain pieces that work for both. I can be comfortable and still feel appropriately dressed. I’ve found a handful of things that work for me, and I don’t have to think too much about them anymore. A crisp, oversize white cotton button-down is half my closet. I also love a soft skirt and a big chunky sweater. Those are the pieces I always go back to and interchange depending on the day. I even wear sweaters in the summer. It’s cold in the Navy Yard!

Our pants fit incredibly well. The moment somebody gets into our pants, they’ll love us forever. Then it’s the jacket and the white shirt. Those three things are our gateway pieces, and everything else follows. You need a good blazer and a good leather piece, too. Once you find those foundational pieces that really work for you, everything else becomes easier.

I really want to see more women in our clothes. It’s not just about growing the business. It makes me happy when I see people put on our clothes and feel really good. We work so hard to make sure things fit the way a woman wants them to fit. I just want more people to enjoy that.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6715. 16 Best Cashmere Sweaters for Men in 2025, According to GQ

The best men's cashmere sweaters are a lot like that guy Bryce from high school. Remember him? The one who was valedictorian and the star quarterback and the lead in the musical, and still always really nice to you. Hell, he probably also volunteered on weekends. Well, the best men's cashmere sweaters are the Bryce of autumnal staples.

Cashmere is the strongest and warmest and softest and swankiest form of wool there is, and it’s here to take your dreary cold weather wardrobe to bold new heights of handsomeness and coziness. The very best cashmere sweaters instantly make whatever you happen to have on that day—a T-shirt and denim, say, or a button-down and blazer—look miles more considered, sophisticated, and polished.

Make sense? Good, because now comes the fun part: settling on the right cashmere sweater for you. Are you after a basic cashmere pullover? A svelte cashmere turtleneck, perhaps? How about a currently-trending cashmere cardigan? To help make that decision as simple and streamlined as possible, we found 16 of the very best men's cashmere sweaters on the planet—from the gigantic flexes worth dropping a paycheck on to the budget-friendly crewnecks you should cop in every color. You'll look even better than Bryce did the night he was crowned prom king. Take that, Bryce.

In 2025, Banana Republic hawks no shortage of discreet office-ready staples, but tucked in amongst them is one of the best cashmere sweater propositions you'll find today. Essentially, this is the platonic ideal of an affordable cashmere sweater: Soft as anything, snug at the wrists and hem, and still roomy enough in the body and shoulders.

But the folks at Banana Republic didn't down tools once they'd nailed the fit and fabric. (And that's exactly why they've earned the top spot in our rankings.) Instead, they paid a ton of attention to their color choices, turning out a range of dignified, unexpected, and expensive-feeling shades, from the dusty blue above to a spotted brown, reddish pink, and classy ecru—alongside your typical black, gray, and navy. No wonder 205 happy customers took the time to give it a five-star online review.

After its supreme softness, cashmere’s best-known attribute is its high price. But thanks to the forces that be (globalization, capitalism, etc.) cashmere is shedding its high-falutin’ price tags. The quality of more affordable cashmere sweaters aren’t up to par with the truly nice stuff, of course, but some options are better than others.

For us, the best of the budget bunch is Uniqlo. For just a hundred bucks, you get a satisfyingly soft pure cashmere sweater in a classic, relaxed fit, available in an impressively wide range of colors. If you want an upgrade from merino wool without the massive price jump, Uniqlo’s your best bet. The style is timeless and the cashmere is softer than you’d expect for the pricepoint.

We’ve said it time and time again: long-sleeve polos make you hot. And in winter, the best long-sleeve polos are made from luscious cashmere. And have the right fit. And a really good drape. And also a collar that’s not too small nor too thin.

All of that is usually one spinning plate too many for most brands, but Buck Mason is the Cirque du Soleil of knitwear and its California Cashmere Polo sweater is its harrowing act. The fabric is a 12-gauge jersey knit made from long-staple cashmere that has a pillowy hand and an elegant drape that, unlike many flimsy cashmeres, has plenty of structure. The neck hits the bullseye and is deep enough for a tee to peek out, but not so deep to reveal the insane graphic on said tee. It also hits that middle-tier price point oh so well, and even out-classes some higher-priced options.

You don’t have to do much to a cashmere sweater for it to make a big impression. The fabric itself speaks volumes, so even the most basic of knitwear options can instantly make an outfit feel a bit luxer, more considered, and altogether elevated. (Just think about how put together guys like David Beckham and Daniel Day-Lewis look wearing plain ol' pullovers.)

At the same time, though, a little visual flourish never hurt anybody, which is why we love these cashmere crewneck sweaters from the new J.Crew. They still let the cashmere do the talking, but the classic cable knit gives it a bit of visual interest and keeps it from falling flat. The quality cashmere is more than solid for the price and looks great solo with jeans and sneakers, but cleans up real good with a button-up peaking out and a blazer over top.

There isn't too much to say here: You're either a cashmere hoodie person or you aren't. The ones who are, though, would tell you they make flights (and movie nights on the couch) a cosy, incognito dream, and allow you to get away with wearing a hoodie all kinds of other places that they wouldn't typically be welcome. And when it comes to where to find one, Todd Snyder is here, as always, with a handsome, clean silhouette that makes the cashmere hoodie's refined nature even more so. It also comes in three nice colours. Thanks Todd.

Most cashmere stuff feels very East Coast prep—all straight-laced and solid-colored—which makes sense, because the East Coast is where it’s cold. Back in 2007, though, LA designer Greg Chait flipped that on its head: under the moniker The Elder Statesman, he started making cashmere blankets, then sweaters and ponchos and socks and stuffed animals, with a distinctly Californian vibe.

The stuff is often tie-dyed and dip-dyed, sometimes emblazoned with palm trees or magic mushrooms or the checkerboard pattern on your trusty Vans for a jolt of Justin-Bieber-exiting-Sushi-Park swagger. But here, Chait and co. are playing the hits, rendering their cult-favorite sweaters in six different straight-forward, appealing colors.

Working on an Irish isle, Inis Meáin has woven traditional Aran craftsmanship with modern techniques for decades. But, crucially, it still only produces its knits in small batches. Which means this is a rare, rare waffle.

Quince makes one of the best affordable cashmere sweaters on the market. It’s hard to disagree with the idea that a good piece of knitwear shouldn’t equate to a down payment on a home. It might not be as luxe as some of our other favorite options, but it’s still ultra soft, which means you can swaddle yourself comfortably without worrying about the damage this sweater has done to your bank account.

Whoever this supposed “Jordan” guy is, we’re guessing that Alex Mill named its cashmere sweater after the famed basketball legend because it also has it all: thick-gauge yarns, a fluffy texture, elegant drape, classic fit, and a perfect crew neck collar.

While some DTC cashmere brands focus solely on finding the luxest fibers, Everlane remains focused on its pricing also. The cashmere crewnecks use a blend of new cashmere and recycled cashmere for a reduced environmental impact, and still feel incredibly soft in the hand.

Mr P.—Mr Porter's in-house line—normally makes excellent, quiet basics. We're proud of the fictional man's decision to go off a little here.

Ghiaia Cashmere’s has expanded over the last few years but the brands initial focus is quite literally in the name. If there’s one place you can turn to when you’re in search of beautiful cashmere knits, then it’s Ghiaia. It’s knitted in Italy, and it has raglan sleeves with subtle ribbing in the sleeves and neck.

The cardigan du jour is not the tight-fitting knit of trends gone by, but rather a thicker, looser variety. In fact, it's sort of looks just like this.

Extreme Cashmere's designs come in only one, inclusive size, so if you're a small person who likes a close fit—move on. But if you're anything else, swaddle thyself.

A deep cut from our Italian friends across the Atlantic, Fedeli is the go-to cashmere brand for many exacting Europeans.

Another polo sweater here, but this time from the luxury gods at Connolly.

Cashmere is a kind of wool that’s made from the undercoats of goats. While goat’s outercoats are thick and coarse to protect them from the harsher elements, the undercoat remains ultra soft and fine. The fibers are actually hollow which makes them great for insulation which is why cashmere is known for its super smooth hand and high warmth to weight ratio. What separates cashmere from other types of wool is its superior silky hand and fine fibers which can vary between 14 to 19 microns in thickness—thinner than even the finest sheep’s wool. For reference, human hairs are only as fine as about 50 microns.

Here's the catch: high-quality cashmere will usually cost you a pretty penny. For the price, you should be getting a form of cashmere made from long staple fibers spun into multi-ply yarns and knit into a tightly-tensioned fabric. Those factors all contribute to cashmere's pedigreed reputation as a material that's much less prone to pilling and stretching than its woolier counterparts. (That reputation, rightful or not, also informs its notoriously high price tag.) Cashmere is graded by its thickness and staple length with Grade A being the highest quality, followed by grades B and C. The higher the grade, the softer the yarns, the warmer the fabric, the better the durability.

Over the last few years, demand for good quality affordable cashmere sweaters has transformed the erstwhile rich-guy signifier into a bona fide cold weather staple you don't have to drain a 401(k) to afford. With one small caveat: supply chain transparency is infamously hard to suss out in the fashion industry, but don't expect a $100 cashmere joint—no matter how tony its source of origin—to hold up as well as a $500 one.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6713. Homemade Mac & Cheese

The perfect mac & cheese will have you dreaming about its cheesy goodness for days after you eat it. Obviously, the cheese has to be top-notch, but the real secret to the best version is the perfect crunchy topping. My recipe delivers on all of that and more, so you won't even think about reaching for another mac & cheese recipe for the rest of your gatherings. From weeknight dinners to potlucks and holidays, this is the classic side dish everyone will be reaching for.

What People Are Saying:"Always a huge hit with friends and family! This is my go-to if I'm bringing a dish to a holiday dinner with friends. I've rarely had leftovers to take home!" - Zafire_Shadows

"This is exactly what mac and cheese dreams are made of. Love the gruyere funk and the mustard powder (so clever!)." - Pastagofer

First, you’ll boil the pasta. Start with a big pot of salted water (and I mean super salty, it should taste like the ocean), then add your pasta and cook it until before al dente—I’m talking still-has-a-ring-of-hard-white-pasta-in-the-middle-when-you-bite-in uncooked. You want it to meld and absorb with your cheese sauce in the oven, not turn into mush. Undercooking now guarantees the perfect bite when you pull it out of the oven.

Then it’s cheese sauce time, and that means making a béchamel. You’ll start by melting your butter in a pan over medium heat, then sprinkling the flour over the top and stirring everything together until it starts pulling away from the pan slightly. You’re looking for what’s called a “blonde roux,” which has a slightly yellow tint and should smell a bit like baked goods. You don’t want it super dark (so keep an eye on the heat!), but you definitely don’t want it as light as it started.

Next, add the milk in slowly, stirring constantly so you don’t get thick lumps. Want to shortcut this process a bit? Heat your milk ahead of time to room temperature or even slightly warm. You’re going to cook it until it’s thick and bubbly (about 2 minutes), so having your milk warm will help you get there faster. It’s going to get a LOT thicker when you add the cheese, so if it’s still a little thin but you want to get on with it, that’s okay too.

Once you’re ready to add the cheese, it’s very important to take it off the heat before so that the sauce doesn’t start to separate. That said, if you’ve been at it for a minute and are still having trouble getting your cheese to melt, pop your pot back on the heat for just a few seconds to bring the temperature up and help the process along.

Once it comes together, you can stir in the macaroni until every noodle is coated.

At this point, you could eat this mac & cheese right now, and it would taste wonderful. But! If you want exceptional mac & cheese, you’ve gotta bake it. So pour it into a pan, then get ready for the best part—the topping. To make it, you’ll combine some breadcrumbs and the remaining half cup of Parmesan in a bowl, then add the olive oil and mix it all up. Sprinkle it on top of the mac and cheese in the casserole dish.

All you have to do is pop it into your preheated 375° oven and bake it until it's golden brown on top. Go ahead, snag a bite before it's fully cooled—you'll really be able to taste all your extra effort.

The full list of ingredients and directions can be found in the recipe below.

If you’re looking to make this dish ahead of time, prepare the mac & cheese up until step 4, then cover it in your baking dish and refrigerate it up to 3 days (or freeze it up to 2 months). When ready to serve, add the Parmesan and panko mixture, bake, and serve. If baking from frozen, allow to thaw in the refrigerator overnight before baking.

If you have any leftovers, store them in an airtight container in the fridge for around 3 days.

#6712. The GTA Files: My Long-Lost Interview With the Brothers Who Founded Rockstar

“You can’t fuck with Keith Richards.” That’s what Sam Houser told me when I asked the inspiration for his company name, Rockstar Games. “I’m a huge fan of the classic golden era of rock stars—both the music, but also the life,” he went on. “You can't argue with Keith Moon riding in a Rolls Royce with a blowup doll hanging out the back and a woman in there screaming, ‘I'm being raped.’ The guy is the original punk rocker. And if we can bring a fraction of that to games, then we’re doing something.”

It was October 2002, just a few weeks before the release of their latest blockbuster, Grand Theft Auto: Vice City. I’d come to their glossy loft in Soho, Manhattan to interview the elusive 30-year-old Houser and Rockstar co-founders, his younger brother Dan Houser and managing director Terry Donovan, for Rolling Stone. It would be one of their first, last and only in-depth sit-downs together before they decided that the world’s most successful and controversial video game required little explanation from them at all.

I continued reporting on them and their games during the raucous and turbulent years to follow: hitting over $10 billion in sales, getting compared to Balzac and Dickens in Time magazine, being sued (unsuccessfully) for causing real-life violence. As I investigated in my book Jacked: The Outlaw Story of Grand Theft Auto, they also triggered what remains the most outrageous and cinematic scandal ever to hit the industry: the discovery of a hidden sex scene in 2005’s GTA San Andreas that would inspire attempts to legislate and ban the game. As Hilary Clinton said at the time, “The disturbing material in Grand Theft Auto and other games like it is stealing the innocence of our children and it’s making the difficult job of being a parent even harder.”

With the gamer generation now grown up and in power, the Reefer Madness panic around GTA has finally given way to something more important: this little game out of Scotland has become the most successful and influential entertainment product of all time. Today, Rockstar returns to Vice City with the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI. More than a decade in the making, with a budget Rockstar has never disclosed but analysts put between $1 billion and $2 billion, the game follows Jason Duval, an ex-soldier, and Lucia Caminos, who phones him from prison, as they’re pulled into a criminal conspiracy across a send-up of Miami. Its first trailer has been viewed more than 300 million times on YouTube, and footage premiering on Netflix nearly crashed the streamer’s servers. Take-Two Interactive, Rockstar’s parent company, expects record revenue of $8.1 billion this fiscal year.

For longtime fans, GTA 6 marks something more personal: the first game in the franchise since the original band behind it broke up. Following the departure of Donovan and other central figures, Dan Houser exited Rockstar in 2020, leaving his big brother Sam as the last founder still there. With Sam remaining notoriously quiet about the new game, I went digging through my old tapes and found my long-lost interview with gaming’s original punk rockers. During our two-hour talk, the young co-founders were brash, unrepentant, and championing a lifetime of obsessions, as Sam put it. “I wasn’t just obsessed about games,” he told me, “we were obsessed about everything—obsessive about music, obsessive about film, obsessive about fashion.”

GTA 6 shows that the obsession never left him. Here in this exclusive excerpt from my interview, the Rockstars share how it all started.

Sam Houser: We nearly had a fist fight last week. We have one argument a year.

Sam Houser: That was an old arcade game called Mr. Do! It used to be in like, a bodega, a corner store. You go in there and buy a cigarette for seven P. And you get a couple of games of Mr. Do!. But the games that really got me hooked was when I got into the Sinclair ZX Spectrum. It was very British: a weird little computer with rubber keys, and it was the games that came out then. I was obsessive about everything, really. We had every significant game on every game system going.

Dan Houser: They thought it was interesting. Sam was probably more into games throughout the time. I would come in and out from being interested in them, or being forced to play them. There was physical violence.

Sam Houser: I would beat him into playing.

Terry Donovan: I think for me, there was a period of arcade games: Defender, Track and Field, Tron, Pole Position. So that was that was like the golden age. You would you stand there and if a bloke lasted two hours on 10p on Defender, you thought he was it. If you grew up in the '70s, you probably wanted to be a guitarist. If you were in the '80s, you probably wanted to be a DJ. The things you grow up wanting now, you probably want to be a video game designer.

Sam Houser: Absolutely. That’s one of the essential things, for me, in games. Look at the games we do now. The game that really coined that in the early days, particularly for me, was this game Elite. You were a space smuggler, and it was just very simple graphics—black screen with just white lights on it. And you flew around space, interacting with other spaceships and other colonies and other planets and so on.

Dan Houser: It's like reading any kind of book when you're a kid or anything that makes you feel like you're not in the real world.

Sam Houser: What was interesting about GTA 1 was that graphically it was top down. It wasn't as sharp or appealing as things that were popular at the time, like Tomb Raider and all these other games that were coming out. You were put into the world, and you were given choices. It was a very sort of non-linear experience. Because it showed all these people out there that wanted this kind of experience that were prepared to forego the graphical gratification you got at the Tomb Raider—you know, that girl with the nice breasts. Suddenly you were playing in 2D and it was top down. It was utterly compelling.

I thought the subject matter was a really appealing hook. We felt that the sort of the fantasy sci-fi, fantasy, you know, Dungeons & Dragons, and here's my sword and there's that orc. We didn't feel that the mass market was that into that stuff. We wanted to make things that felt a little bit more relevant to people like ourselves. The reason it sold is it was incredibly fun to play. It was originally called Race n' Chase. Kind of catchy name. In the UK, the game was in the House of Parliament, and it got a massive share of media attention. It was on BBC News five times in one day. Like, oh my God!

Terry Donovan: It was one first piece of entertainment that was sort of unashamed antihero. There's no twist at the end where he turns out to be a good guy.

Dan Houser: Freedom was the thing that appealed. Because as free as games like Defender and Scramble were for us, 10 years on, they felt restrictive. I don't consider myself a master gamer. I play every game. I'm good at them. But I think that we try and make games that can be played by normal folk as well as hardcore too. And the problem with some of those other games is when you hit a point that's frustrating, you can't get past it. But in Grand Theft Auto, any of them, when you hit a point that's tough, you just go and do something else. And then while you're doing the other thing, you're thinking about this other thing that you've been stuck on, and then you come back to it and crack it. I think that's fucking great.

Sam Houser: I came out here and did a very energetic pitch where I must have sweated through three layers of clothing in my kind of insanely sweaty way, and everyone in the office was like, "Who the fuck is that guy?"

Sam Houser: They just didn't want it. Business is so like, “What's that person doing? We must copy that.” Everybody else was doing 3D platform games and 3D sports games, all these shooters.

Sam Houser: I first came here in my 18th birthday. It my 18th birthday present or whatever to just go and buy leather hip-hop puffers and Air Jordan Mark V's. From that moment I was chronically in love with the place. The idea that I would end up here making games where games is traditionally a West Coast thing, to me it's just like… in some ways I feel it was meant to be. We were kind of enlisted to sort of build that.

Sam Houser: The mission was basically to bring our attitude, to try and make games that felt more relevant to the audience that was playing.

Dan Houser: It’s more modern, more accessible, gameplay over graphics, but with a slightly anarchic, edgy fantasy—a fantasy you would have walking down the street rather than from reading Dungeons & Dragons. It’s a fantasy where you're a street racer rather than a wizard.

Terry Donovan: [to Sam] How much did you want a Def Jam baseball jacket?

Sam Houser: More than anything in the world. They had this attitude: “Here's where we from. Here's what we do. If you don't like us, fuck you. Everything we touch is somehow made more magical.” A guy like Rick Rubin is such a fucking hero, because you go from doing the hip-hop that he pioneered to doing Slayer, to doing The Cult.

Terry Donovan: It was a first-floor apartment on Water Street, in the South Street Seaport, and it had glass on the front. There was no fucking light in it.

Sam Houser: There was about 20 people. By the time I was in there, there was about seven people living in there. That whole teenager-in-the-bedroom-with-the-bottle-top-specs thing comes around the games industry like a fucking albatross. This wasn't the kind of lonely nine-year-old or 12-year-old kid, or the one who's going to end up exposing himself to schoolgirl doing this.

Terry Donovan: The lines are our decision of what's funny and what's tasteful. What isn't relevant to what a lot of what other people think. If you think it's funny, you put it in your machine, if you don't think it's funny, you don't. By the time you've reduced Grand Theft Auto on national television to a 30-second soundbite, it's a game with a hooker in it, and that's offensive to the very, very maximum. That's the thing—you realize that [gamers] aren't all 10 years old. There isn't some overriding social responsibility to have a redeeming social value. Our responsibility is to provide a toolkit with which young adults can entertain themselves.

Sam Houser: When you do something wrong in the game, the police come and get you. You don't just run around on a rampage and carry on. You commit crimes, the police are on you. You commit more, they're on you more. You commit more, the FBI will turn up, the SWAT will turn up, and then the Army turns up. When you play the missions, you're only you're only doing bad things to bad people. You're whacking other gangsters.

Sam Houser: When I play it for fun, I tend to just drive around and look at cool things and drive in as cinematic a way as possible— sort of like I’m a stifled film director. And I’m carrying out my own little stories in my own head, listening the music. I mean, gosh, I am 30 years old. It's sort of tragic, isn't it? I just sort of go off in my own world.

Sam Houser: Oh, well, Scarface, Miami Vice. At the end of GTA III, we took our whole team out to Miami. We're all out there. This is we live and breathe this place, learn this place. For research, we were staying in a very average hotel on Ocean Drive. You know, it was 30 Scottish guys walking around with cameras in the day. The second half of the week, everyone got insanely burned.

Dan Houser: Vice City is not quite Miami. It's Miami through the Grand Theft Auto lens. On the other hand, you don't want to offend the nerds because you've not put the right number of bridges in, or things are a little bit different. You know, we're so anal that we don't want to offend other anal people. For example, with the radio commercials, me and the other guy, one of the other guys who just then with me would sit for hours and listen to '80s radio commercials. We got we ring up advertising agencies and say, ‘send us all your best best '80s radio and TV commercials.’

Sam Houser: We're trying to do a satire about the 80s.

Sam Houser: We cast them ourselves. Contact their agents ourselves. Tommy Vercetti is played by Ray Liotta, interacting with Lance Vance, Philip Michael Thomas, [Ricardo] Tubbs from Miami Vice. The two of them together, they have these really entertaining moments.

Dan Houser: Her character is a porn star. She was really sweet, though. She was really lovely. She turned up with her husband and her mother and father, and I'm particularly very English in these situations, so I'm feeling slightly… I'm going, "Oh, hello, Jenna. Could you sound like you're excited?" And she's looking at me more and more obliquely and says "Well, how would it be if I sound like I was having sex?" And I'm like, “Well, that'd be actually perfect.”

Sam Houser: My favorite thing is probably the overall look. I think the thing that we set up so much stuff on was, get me neon. We've got to have neon!

Dan Houser: You can do everything you can do in a film and more. It's all the bits that go in between the equivalent plots of about five films. But it's a different experience compared to a film.

Sam Houser: Almost by definition, no. Apart from choose-your-own-adventure books. You feel like, “Hey, [I have] my little piece of digital real estate, and there's my car and everything. I own this little space.” It's just some numbers and ones and all this code. When the machine's on and it's going, it's there, it's real.

Terry Donovan: There's no point being edgy if your game's a shit.

Sam Houser: At the end of the day, there's enough people in the country that would like to see us thrown out and/or locked up for doing what we do. Look at the trouble we got in for the prostitute thing. You'd be amazed how conservative people are.

#6710. Creamy Grape Salad

Creamy grape salad is a Southern side dish staple. To make it, toss fresh grapes (use both green and red grapes for a colorful dish) in a sweetened mixture of cream cheese and sour cream until they're evenly coated. Then, top with chopped pecans for the perfect crunch to balance out the creamy dressing. Trust me—if you haven’t tried this nostalgic dish yet, add it to your holiday list for the ultimate sweet side.

In a large bowl, using a whisk or spoon, mix cream cheese, sour cream, granulated sugar, and vanilla.

Using a spatula, gently fold in grapes. Transfer to a serving bowl.

In a small bowl, stir pecans and brown sugar. Top salad with pecan mixture. Enjoy!

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Store leftover grape salad in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 3 days. Give it a gentle stir before serving.

#6708. Zohran Mamdani’s Mustache Is Singlehandedly Bringing Movember Back

When New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani passed out water bottles to marathoners on Nov. 1, few clocked the newly mustachioned man as the city’s head honcho. After all, he’s the first bearded NYC mayor in over a century after, and his constituents had grown familiar with his signature look. When images of his new facial hair circulated, people wondered what inspired the change. Had the mayor been influenced by his hip Brooklyn constituents enough to grow his own on-trend mustache? As we learned the day after the marathon in an online video, the mustache was in support of a somewhat-ancient tradition: Movember.

If it feels like no one’s talked about Movember for a while, that’s because the non-profit charity’s heyday online peaked in 2012. That year, according to the Australian-based foundation, over 1.1 million people participated in Movember—a campaign that encourages men to grow mustaches to raise awareness and funds for men’s mental health, prostate cancer, and testicular cancer—collecting more than $126 million in charity. But like planking, “Gangnam Style,” and flash mobs, Movember eventually faded from the cultural conversation. Google Trends recorded a 10% drop in searches for the cause the following year, and by 2026, search interest had fallen 90% from its 2012 peak. Now, after Mamdani, alongside Council Member Rita Joseph, announced a $4 million investment in a new prostate cancer center at Brooklyn’s Kings County Hospital in honor of the hairy month-long social movement, interest in Movember has since climbed to its highest level since 2019, according to Google Trends.

While the mayor’s stubble has come and gone throughout the month, the heavy mustache has remained constant.

The stubble around the mustache has come and gone during the month, but that thick mustache has remained a staple. He had it at a Veterans Day reception, during a plethora of press conferences, and when he announced the disbanding of the New York Police Department’s controversial Strategic Response Group.

Politicians have long been defined, at least in part, by their hair: Donald Trump’s comb-over, Bernie Sanders’ horseshoe-shaped hairline, Gavin Newsom’s slicked-back locks. But facial hair is increasingly becoming part of the political image, too, as more men embrace letting their beards grow. Pete Buttigieg, once clean-shaven, grew a beard after leaving his post as Transportation Secretary; JD Vance became the first bearded vice president in decades; and Mamdani ended more than a century’s worth of clean-shaven NYC mayors.

And hey, what better time to revive an early 2010s tradition than following the Democrats’ historic victories in the midterm elections. Millennial woke optimism is back, baby, and so is growing facial hair for a charitable cause. If the mayor’s facial hair can turn a bygone internet fad into renewed attention for men’s health, it may be his most consequential grooming decision yet.

#6707. Japanese Sweet Potato Chaat

Every cuisine has a sauce that’s an absolute essential—a ride or die, if you will. For South Asian food, this is a cilantro mint green chutney. It brings a punchy, herb-packed burst of flavor that goes with pretty much everything. This recipe combines cilantro, mint, and just the right amount of heat from green chiles, creating a balance of spice and freshness. Lime juice adds brightness, while cumin, sugar, and chaat masala add complexity. Use this as a zesty topping for your favorite dish or as a base for pani puri water (just add 1½ cups [360 ml] of water and strain), or make it creamy with yogurt or sour cream to use for spreading and dipping.

Slow-roasting Japanese sweet potatoes flesh-side down releases their natural sugars, caramelizing and deepening their sweetness. The flesh becomes incredibly tender, almost custard-like in texture, while the skin turns a rich golden brown and becomes slightly crispy. So don’t bother peeling them! Top them with tamarind concentrate (I like Tamicon, Cock Brand, Pure Indian Foods, or Dragonfly—anything without citric acid), green chutney, fresh onions, tomatoes, and cilantro for a chaat-inspired appetizer.

To a blender or food processor, add 2 bunches cilantro (about 6 oz. [170 g]), leaves and stems, 1 bunch mint (about 2 oz. [55 g]), leaves and tender stems, 6 green Thai chiles or 2 serranos, stemmed (more if you want it spicier), ½ cup (120 ml) lime juice (from about 5 limes), 2 tsp. cumin seeds, 1½ tsp. fine sea salt, 1 tsp. sugar, and 1 tsp. chaat masala. Pulse for a few seconds in intervals until the ingredients are pulverized into a smooth paste. Transfer to an airtight container and store until ready to use, up to 1 week in the refrigerator or 1 month in the freezer.

Preheat the oven to 375°F (190°C) and line a baking sheet with parchment paper.

Drizzle the sheet with extra-virgin olive oil and place 1½ lb. (680 g) Japanese or Korean sweet potatoes, ends trimmed, halved lengthwise, cut-side down. Bake for 60 to 70 minutes, until golden and caramelized on the cut side and cooked through and tender; a knife inserted into the center of the potato should slide through easily. Let rest for 5 to 8 minutes. Once cool enough to handle, cut the sweet potatoes into 1" to 2" [2.5 to 5 cm] pieces.

To a small bowl, add ½ cup (120 g) plain whole-milk yogurt or sour cream and ½ tsp. fine sea salt and whisk to combine. If you’re using sour cream, thin it out with a little water.

Place the sweet potatoes on a serving platter and drizzle the yogurt, ½ cup Evergreen Chutney, 3 Tbsp. tamarind concentrate or paste, and 2 Tbsp. lime juice evenly all over. Sprinkle with 1 medium red tomato (5 oz. [140 g]), finely chopped, 1 small red onion (7 oz. [200 g]), finely chopped, 2 Tbsp. chopped cilantro leaves and tender stems, 1 tsp. chaat masala, 3 green Thai chiles or 1 serrano, stemmed, thinly sliced, and crispy sev and serve immediately.

Do Ahead: You can make the individual components a day ahead of time and store them in airtight containers in the refrigerator, then assemble to serve.

#6706. We Found The 6 Strongest Drinks At Starbucks, Ranked By Caffeine

Each of these bevvies delivers a serious buzz.

I would love nothing more than to be the type of person who jumps out of bed with the sunrise—without the lure of a steaming hot and very caffeinated cup of coffee waiting for me. But I need to smell the freshly brewed espresso before I can muster the strength to get out from under the duvet.

Even as decaf this and half-caf that have become all the rage in our high-cortisol society, you can pry the double cappuccino out of my cold, dead hands! Sometimes you just need a 350-milligram latte to keep you chugging through the day (unless your doctor says otherwise, in which case, ignore me).

In the spirit of staying fueled, I analyzed the Starbucks menu and narrowed it down to the most caffeinated bevvies you can buy in a grande size. However, everything is customizable and may differ slightly in caffeine content—you can always ask for more or fewer espresso shots, or opt for a larger size.

There’s a reason you might feel extra jittery after a morning Cold Brew. It’s packed with caffeine because it uses almost twice as many coffee grounds as regular brewed coffee. It is diluted with water, though, to temper the buzz.

A Caffè Americano—hot or iced—packs a similar punch as a Cold Brew. It’s not all that different, TBH, except instead of brewed coffee, it combines water and espresso to give it a littleeeee more edge.

If you love a sweet latte but want more caffeine, the Iced Brown Sugar Shaken Espresso is your ticket at roughly 255 milligrams for a grande.

If you’re craving a Cold Brew with a kick, go Nitro. It uses a higher coffee-to-water ratio in the cold steeping and dispensing process, and it is served without ice (so, again, less dilution). The result: bold flavor and about 280 milligrams of caffeine.

There’s a reason I’m a drip-coffee loyalist, and it’s not just the flavor. It’s also about the 310 milligrams of caffeine that a grande Pike Place delivers, which earns the medium roast coffee a solid second spot on this list.

Drumroll, please! The most caffeinated beverage on Starbucks’ menu is the Blonde Roast Brewed Coffee at 360 milligrams. Why does it differ from the medium and even the dark roast? It’s pretty simple: Blonde usually tests higher because Starbucks brews it with a little more coffee, and lighter roasts can pack more grounds into the scoop. That means more caffeine makes its way into your cup.

#6705. Brûléed Kheer (Rice Pudding) With Mango Sorbet

This creamy, always comforting slow-cooked rice pudding is scented with cardamom and sometimes topped with nuts or dried fruit. Versions of it exist across South Asia and beyond, each one slightly different but speaking the same nostalgic language. I’m serving it with a little twist here—a brûléed top for that crackly crème brûlée moment, and a scoop of mango sorbet for brightness and as a nod to the classic sticky rice flavor.

Note: If you are not using a nonstick pot, be extra careful and stir every 4 to 5 minutes; otherwise the rice pudding will definitely stick to the bottom. In the event it does, quickly empty the contents into a new pot and start simmering from low heat again. If you do not have a kitchen blowtorch, you can skip the brûlée. Serve warm or chill for 2 to 4 hours and garnish with saffron threads.

To a medium heavy-bottomed nonstick pot, add 5 cups (1.2 L) whole milk, 1 cup (240 ml) water, one 14-oz. (415 ml) can sweetened condensed milk, 3 green cardamom pods, and one 1" (2.5-cm) cinnamon stick. Bring to a boil and add 1 cup (200 g) jasmine rice or any short‑grain rice, then turn the heat down to low and simmer, stirring often and scraping all the way down to the bottom of the pot to make sure the mixture doesn’t stick or scorch, until the rice is tender and the mixture is thickened, 50 to 60 minutes. Add ¼ tsp. fine sea salt and stir to combine. If the consistency feels too thick add, ¼ cup [60 ml] water and stir to reach your desired consistency.

Scoop the kheer into heatproof serving bowls and top each with 1 to 2 Tbsp. granulated sugar, ensuring the surface is covered completely. Then carefully brûlée using a kitchen blowtorch, moving in a circular pattern, until the sugar is melted and a deep amber color. (You want a thin, smooth crust that shatters when broken.) Top with a scoop of sorbet and serve immediately.

#6704. With Grand Theft Auto VI, Rockstar Bet $1.5B That Humans Could Make a Better Game Than AI

Somewhere in the far north of Leonida, Grand Theft Auto VI’s fictional take on Florida, a robbery is taking place. This much is unsurprising. Crime is baked into the title of the series, which began in 1997 and, despite its thick American accent, is still predominantly made in Scotland. But Jason and Lucia, the young, beautiful Bonnie and Clyde who together share the role of protagonists in GTA VI, are not the ones committing this crime. Rather, a grizzled biker hangs from the second-floor window of a locked bar, throwing valuables down to his accomplice on the street.

A thunderstorm has recently rolled through this area and the tarmac steams as sunlight knifes through the clouds. It’s Leonida-muggy. Patches of sweat swell beneath Jason’s arms as he takes in the scene. The bikers notice you watching and ask, politely, considering the circumstances, if you could fuck off. A choice presents itself: clamber back on your stolen motorbike and continue your road trip, or insert yourself into this micro-drama and take a principled stand on behalf of concerned citizens everywhere (then take the loot for yourself, of course).

Dozens of impromptu moments like this stud GTA6, a video game that makes you feel like one entity among many rather than the spoiled V.I.P. around whom everything has been artfully arranged. Most video games flatter the player by making them the centre of the virtual universe, the cog on which all the digital clockwork turns. What is extraordinary about Vice City and its surrounding area is how much of the world appears to trundle on without needing your presence or input.

These moments are made possible by dozens of interlocking systems built by dozens of discrete teams. An entire Los-Angeles-based team worked on the personalities and behaviors of the thousands of people who inhabit Vice City; the bar’s interior was meticulously set-dressed to suggest the lives and tastes of its proprietors and patrons, while the biker’s dialogue and reactions to your choices were written, cast and performed. The weather, too, is restless: cloud and rain move across the world, dynamically changing light and mood. This tableaux, which only a small percentage of players may ever encounter, has not been staged like a scene in a play but rather cultivated in what amounts to the world’s most expensive Petri dish.

Since 1984, when Elite first used procedural generation to conjure an entire galaxy on a computer screen, game designers have learned to produce universes from scant technological resources. But in the era of procedural generation, scale is almost routine; the harder, more costly work is making an expanse feel handmade and coherent. GTA VI manages both, with districts populated by distinct communities and social strata, each shaped by decades of imagined history. It reveals a kind of detail and thoughtfulness that A.I. has yet to reproduce convincingly—and perhaps never will.

The game’s lavishness is not only visible in the rain, the sweat and the individual strands of hair falling across Lucia’s shoulders, but also in the hundreds, perhaps thousands, of diversions away from the criminal grind. For example, Jason and Lucia can take time out from planning a heist to compete in illegal street races for prize money, enter dirt-bike scrambles or race stock cars in a stadium. You can become a hunter, choosing a rifle to suit your quarry so as not to ruin its pelt; start drone racing, streaking through abandoned buildings in an FPV headset; or dive for treasure among old shipwrecks, dodging both the circling sharks, and the authorities who want to check your license.

A compelling main story runs through the game, with missions that leave room for different approaches: you might play as a careful shooter, aiming to wound rather than kill, or as a less discerning one. The couple, so artfully written and performed, offer vivid points of entry into the world as you guide them from relative poverty towards financial security that, perhaps, might allow them to build a family life beyond crime. But much of the joy comes from straying from the main path.

To that end the game makes room for long, meandering drives across the country and peaceful early-morning flights over mountain ranges. You can spend a sleepy afternoon with a scenic view, peering through dollar-a-minute binoculars. Many of these diversions appeared in the studio’s earlier games, but here they feel newly vivid, rendered with extraordinary fidelity. The smaller encounters are just as memorable. Happen upon a possum at night and it might play dead on the tarmac. In a thrift store, you can while away assembling fits worthy of a Ralph Lauren vintage buyer; at a 24-hour nightclub, you can dance until sunrise, before emerging blinking into the dawn while wondering, as Kanye once put it, if they’ve got cabs still. (Of course they do: you can summon one in an instant from your virtual phone).

This is lavish game-making on an unprecedented scale. The irony, however, is that GTA VI arrives at a moment when other studios are bleeding staff (if they survive at all) and the cost of making games at anywhere close to this level continues to rise precipitously. In one industry survey published this month, a third of respondents said they had been laid off in the past two years; more than half of those laid off had yet to find new work—a stark picture of the ongoing insecurity across the industry. In the early 2010s, game budgets fell between $30 and $70 million. McKinsey puts the current cost of a blockbuster game production at $200 million to $500 million. At these prices, few can pay to play.

Even amid the union-busting allegations (which Rockstar denies; an employment tribunal is ongoing), the company has retained much of its long-serving talent between major releases. That continuity gives it a store of institutional knowledge that is hard to preserve when studios expand and contract around individual projects. Key members of GTA VI’s leadership have worked together for decades. The studio also has a clear commercial advantage over its closest creative peers: Naughty Dog, creator of the Uncharted and The Last of Us series, and CD Projekt Red, creator of The Witcher and Cyberpunk 2077. Their story-led games can dominate a moment, but their stories conclude when the credits roll.

After players finished Grand Theft Auto V’s story, by contrast, they could continue to inhabit its world through GTA Online, joining others in an ever-replenishing sandbox of play. A leak of Rockstar data suggested that GTA Online averaged about $9.6 million a week between September 2025 and April 2026—roughly $42 million a month. Rockstar is owned by Take-Two, and there is no evidence that GTA Online’s astonishing revenue was entirely funneled into GTA VI. But a game generating revenue year after year gives its parent a durable source of income, justifying artisanal-style work. These factors make Grand Theft Auto VI’s scale and fidelity exceptionally difficult for any rival to match.

That challenge is something even Rockstar’s former leaders have had to reckon with. Leslie Benzies—who for years was one of the three creative leaders on Rockstar projects alongside Sam and Dan Houser (of the trio, only Sam remains at the company)—founded Build a Rocket Boy, which raised more than $300 million before releasing MindsEye. The game’s troubled launch was followed by mass redundancies and, in September 2026, a slide into administration. The lesson seems clear. Money alone cannot buy the infrastructure, expertise and returning audience that have allowed Rockstar to build this world at extraordinary length.

In a game filled with enticing luxuries, from expensive shoes to glinting supercars, the visible human hand is, in this dawning age of AI abundance, the rarest luxury of all in Leonida. You can leave the bikers to their robbery: clouds drift on, the tarmac dries. The world keeps its own time. Making that unforced life cohere took years of work across many teams, and required a collective attention to every detail. With budgets rising and studios closing, perhaps no other company could afford such a wager. Leonida is a miracle of craft—and a monument to a form of game-making the wider industry can no longer sustain.

#6702. Bob's Chicken Salad and More Recipes We Made This Week

It’s no secret that Bon Appétit editors cook a lot for work. So it should come as no surprise that we cook a lot during our off hours too. Here are the recipes we’re whipping up this month to get dinner on the table, entertain our friends, satisfy a sweet tooth, use up leftovers, and everything in between. For even more staff favorites, click here.

Every fall I stuff my pantry with cans of pumpkin purée. And fortunately our annual Pumpkin Week tells me exactly what to do with the haul. I’m always in search of more savory uses for the ingredient, and my coworker Jesse Szewczyk’s Pumpkin Black Bean Enchiladas were right up my alley. I did a mix of black and kidney beans (what was around) and I toned down the spice for my toddler who—the universe laughs—refused to eat them anyway, instead opting to smear the sauce all over his clothes. C’est la vie! —Emma Laperruque, cooking director

There is a new vendor at my greenmarket who makes incredible buffalo milk yogurt. My favorite is flavored with tangy, floral passion fruit. It’s not too sweet and works beautifully with fruit and granola. To showcase its power as a sauce, I made David Tamarkin’s apple olive oil cake (baked in an 8" round instead of a loaf) and served the yogurt underneath. For the cake, you shred a couple of apples then squeeze out their juices, which you can then drink for a baker’s treat. —Joe Sevier, senior editor, SEO & cooking

One of my favorite things is to use a banana bread recipe to make muffins and call it meal prep. I pack this gluten-free batter into tins, sprinkle on some flaxseed and pecans, and reduce the bake time. They can easily pass for breakfast, an afternoon snack, or dessert, and the recipe yields enough muffins to be enjoyed throughout the week with a scoop of yogurt or swipe of nut butter. —Kate Kassin, editorial operations manager

With a recipe entitled Bob’s Chicken and Cabbage Salad, I’m sure you’re wondering: Who the heck is Bob? Author Melissa Broder’s late father, who happened to be fervently devoted to purple cabbage. Well, Bob, me too—especially after trying this one. Per the instructions, I chopped up half a head of cabbage, baby bok choy, scallions, bean sprouts, and water chestnuts, then tossed the roughage with pulled chicken. This time around, I did cheat with a store-bought rotisserie bird to jump-start cooking (it was 8 p.m. when I started, okay?). That also meant halving the heavenly soy-ginger dressing, since I no longer needed some of it for the marinade. —Nina Moskowitz, associate editor, cooking

My Test Kitchen buddy Rebecca Firkser has a wild little pasta up her sleeve that I can’t get enough of: Butternut Pasta With Sausage and Dates. When she first made it for the team, I was blown away. I would have never thought to throw dates into a pasta, but alas, it’s delicious. Each bite has something sweet, something creamy, a bit of crunch, and endless fun. —Jesse Szewczyk, senior Test Kitchen editor

Since my son started daycare in September, our household has been sick more than it has been healthy. So it makes sense that I have been on a chicken soup kick. I have it for lunch and dinner and drink mugfuls of stock between meals. My latest batch was my colleague Rebecca Firkser’s Toasted Orzo Chicken Soup, which has a beautifully spiced broth and lots of nourishing spinach. It may not be a cure-all, but close enough. —Emma Laperruque, cooking director

Delicata squash should be as popular as butternut. It’s far easier to cut (no hacks or hacking necessary), the thin skin is not only edible but tasty, and it’s got a sweet potato-like flesh that’s creamy and, well, sweet. I usually treat it simply (oil, salt and pepper, hot oven, done), but I treated myself to my former colleague Rachel Gurjar’s gussied-up Roasted Delicata Squash With Crispy Onions and I’m so glad I did. The squash itself takes no extra effort, but gathering up some toppings, like store-bought fried onions tossed with extra spices, juicy pomegranate seeds, and seasoned yogurt, transformed it into a dish I was proud to serve to guests. —Kelsey Jane Youngman, senior cooking editor

We have a new Friday tradition at our house: Pasta Alla Vodka and Caesar salad. Not rocket science, I know, but after a rough-and-tumble week of work and school, we are all reaching for some semblance of comfort. It is also easy enough to whip up that I can often beat the timing of our former Friday tradition, take-out pizza. The Caesar dressing is usually made in advance (I keep a batch in the fridge because my family lurves it) and the pasta of choice is usually rigatoni. It cooks in about the same amount of time as the sauce—I just love when that happens. —Hana Asbrink, deputy food editor

There’s nothing more perfect than a tuna melt. Buttery slices of sourdough or rye that leave a shine of grease on your fingers. Tuna salad mashed into tender flakes, speckled with crisp celery, onion, and herbs. A blanket of American cheese, so stretchy it’ll rival a mozzarella stick. And a mandatory pickle, to offer added tangy solace after each bite. I ate mine with salt-and-vinegar kettle chips stuffed inside because that’s how my favorite diner does it. —Nina Moskowitz, associate editor, cooking

#6701. Failproof Homestyle Chicken Kari

This recipe is inspired by the garam masala my mother used to make back in India. Just a pinch adds incredible depth and flavor to both meat and vegetable dishes—it’s the secret ingredient that often leaves my guests wondering why my Indian food tastes so exceptional. And now you can use it too! This version perfectly balances the warming notes of cloves and nutmeg, and it’s the one I return to time and time again. If you prefer a sweeter blend, add more cinnamon and fennel seeds; if you want a spicier kick, increase the black pepper and cloves.

In a small bowl, toss 12 dried bay leaves, 12 whole cloves, 5 black cardamom pods, 4 whole star anise pods, three 3" (7.5-cm) cinnamon sticks, 1 whole nutmeg, 3 Tbsp. coriander seeds, 3 Tbsp. whole mace fronds, 2 Tbsp. cumin seeds, 1 Tbsp. black peppercorns, and 2 tsp. white poppy seeds to combine.

Heat a medium dry skillet over low heat until hot. Add the spice mixture and toast until slightly fragrant, about 45 seconds. Transfer back to the bowl and let cool.

Transfer the whole nutmeg to a mortar and break into smaller pieces with a pestle (this is just to make it easier to grind, so don’t worry about being too precise!). Repeat with the cinnamon. Tear the bay leaves into smaller pieces. Transfer all of the spices to a spice grinder and grind into a fine powder (it will be slightly warm when you remove the lid). Transfer to an airtight container (do not cover yet) and let cool completely. Then close the lid.

Do Ahead: Garam masala can be made 1 month ahead. Cover and store in a cool, dark place.

To a small heatproof bowl, add 1 medium yellow onion (9 oz. [255 g]), halved, thinly sliced into half-moons, 2 Tbsp. lime juice, 2 Tbsp. Kashmiri red chili powder, 2 tsp. chaat masala, ½ tsp. cayenne powder, and ½ tsp. fine sea salt.

In a small pan over medium-high heat, warm 2 Tbsp. peanut oil or any vegetable oil or avocado oil until it shimmers. Add 1 tsp. cumin seeds and cook until fragrant and they start to turn brown, about 1 minute. Be careful not to let the seeds burn or they will taste bitter. Remove from the heat and pour the hot oil over the onions. Toss to coat the onions in the spice mixture.

Serve immediately or let cool and store in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 1 week.

To a mortar and pestle or food processor, add one 2" (5-cm) piece fresh ginger, chopped, 4 garlic cloves, peeled, and 3 green Thai chiles or 1 serrano, stemmed, chopped, and pound or pulse into a chunky paste. To a medium bowl, add 2 lb. (910 g) boneless, skinless chicken thighs, excess fat trimmed, cut into 2" [5-cm] pieces, along with the ginger-garlic paste and 1 tsp. fine sea salt and toss to combine. Cover and place in the refrigerator to marinate for a minimum of 1 hour or overnight.

In a medium heavy-bottomed pot or Dutch oven over medium-low heat, warm ⅓ cup (80 ml) vegetable oil or avocado oil, then add 1 tsp. cumin seeds, ½ tsp. black mustard seeds, ½ tsp. black peppercorns, 5 whole cloves, 1 fresh or dried bay leaf, 1 star anise, and one 3" [7.5-cm] cinnamon stick, broken into small pieces, and lightly toast and until fragrant, about 1 minute. Increase the heat to medium, add 1 medium red or yellow onion (9 oz. [255 g]), finely chopped, cover, and cook until the onion is translucent and beginning to turn brown around the edges, stirring halfway through, about 10 minutes.

Add 1 Tbsp. ground coriander, 1 Tbsp. Kashmiri red chili powder, 1 Tbsp. Garam Masala, 2 tsp. cayenne powder, and 1 tsp. powdered turmeric. Stir to combine and cook until fragrant, about 1 minute. Drain off the excess marinade from the chicken, then add it to the pot along with 3 Tbsp. tomato paste, 1 Tbsp. packed fresh fenugreek leaves, and remaining 1½ tsp. fine sea salt and stir until everything is mixed thoroughly and the chicken is covered with the spices and tomato paste. Add ½ cup (120 ml) water and mix again. Cover and cook until the chicken is cooked through and tender, 30 minutes, stirring halfway through.

Remove from the heat and sprinkle with the remaining 1 tsp. Garam Masala, remaining 1 tsp. fresh fenugreek leaves, and cilantro leaves. Serve immediately with lime wedges, Insanely Addictive Pickled Onions, and steamed rice or Indian flatbread.

Do Ahead: Store in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 5 days or in the freezer for up to 1 month.
