#6699. 15 Best Beard Oils for Taming Your Stubborn Facial Hair

So, you've realized. Growing a beard was only the beginning: Now you’ve gotta give it some love. Thankfully, the best beard oil will keep that sculpted scruff feeling great! And not just for you, either, because the right beard oil leaves your skin glowing—and stops your facial hair from annoying any partner who gets close to it. Great beard oils also keep coarse hair from getting brittle and wiry, and soften your beard so you’re not scratching your neck or dealing with beard dandruff (yes, it exists). All that from just a couple drops of wonder oil.

I’d go so far as to rank beard oil as the second most important beard care product, just after beard trimmers. And it’s a close second. Because whether you keep your facial hair at a precise 24-hour stubble, go full hermit-mode, or stay somewhere in between, a beard oil does irreplaceable work. (The kind you can’t replace with any old moisturizer or conditioner.) So, if you’re tired of flyaways, coarse beard hair, dandruff, or itchiness, these GQ-tested and approved beard oils will turn your scruff into fluff. Promise.

For the longest time, I thought of Honest Amish as “that Amazon beard oil,” given its enormous popularity on the e-tailer. (At the time of writing, we’re talking nearly 30,000 reviews and a 4.5-star rating.) And often, I'll intentionally seek out an alternative to these Amazon-beloved grooming products—if only because it feels like the equivalent of recommending Applebee's to an out-of-towner. But this time, well: Welcome to Applebee's.

Frankly, I couldn't find a better beard oil. Not only is Honest Amish cost-effective (at the second-best price-per-ounce on my list), but the brand nailed the blend. It's a pure oil recipe (no non-oil ingredients), with seven carrier oils and seven essential oils that give it an earthy scent and help tone the skin beneath your bushel. It absorbs fast, works great to smooth down flyaways, relax stiff bristles, and battle both dry skin and flake-prone beards. And did I mention the value? Six bucks per ounce is a steal.

To be clear, I only use Honest Amish on my beard, largely because I'm not a fan of using any essential oils on bare skin—I particularly find that pumpkin seed oil (a key ingredient here) can be slightly pore-clogging on acne-prone skin. That said: I count myself as acne-prone and haven't had issues with Honest Amish, so I'm probably being over-cautious. It's really just that good.

Despite the name Beardbrand, this company's product line covers the whole body, with a focus on multi-tasking grooming products like dual washes, all-purpose balms, and this utilitarian oil. You'll catch me using it on my hands, elbows, and hair—whether for a dash of shine (to offset a matte hair product) or a protective coat against frizz and humidity. But Beardbrand's Utility Oil is on this list because it works beautifully in the beard.

Sure, it's a touch heavier than some other oils—but that also makes it a terrific pre-shave lubricant. It's important to note that no matter which “flavor” you choose, this oil has a strong dash of fragrance. They're all good, but I'm partial to the brand's new Bold Fortune scent, which oozes a sexy combo of leather, oud, and tobacco. (And pro-tip: The brand's utility wash has the same come-hither scent, if you want to double down.)

Horace's beard oil will make a lot of you happy, though for different reasons. For those overwhelmed by scents—particularly artificial ones—you'll appreciate that its got the subtlest of aromas. I'm partial to the Lemon & Mint one, which reads as “fresh" and never overwhelms. For those looking to stay away from chemicals, Horace's formula is 99% natural—and the 1% is a synthetic grapeseed oil, not something with 12 syllables, which I'm fine with.

The brand gets bonus marks for its easily-understood ingredients labels, which break down these details and err on the side of much-appreciated transparency. Finally, if you're on the fence about beard oils, Horace will make you a believer. It absorbs fast, gives a pinch of shine, and makes just enough difference in terms of taming, moisturizing, and softening that you'll see why a beard oil makes sense.

In the grooming world, Jack Black (the brand) has earned its own fanbase, thanks in large part to one product—the signature Jack Black beard oil. Apply just a few drops and it immediately goes to work softening coarse and brittle facial hair, and without an overpowering fragrance that will distract you all day long. It's one of the most popular beard oils on Amazon, but don't hold that against it. While it softens and adds shine, this oil also delivers a dose of antioxidants and vitamins from natural ingredients such as brown algae and rosemary.

It also uses a unique blend of oils with the subtlest whiff of melon fragrance. Instead of the usual suspects (jojoba, avocado, sunflower seed), this product uses Kalahari melon oil, marula oil, and plum oil, which moisturize your facial hair and skin on contact. Because these oils are all fast-absorbing (my beard hair soaks it up like a sponge), they make this product much less greasy than other beard oils, giving it a more premium feel.

I test literally hundreds of grooming products a year, and I'm so intimately acquainted with brands new and old that I often think about them based on their best product. For Pura d'Or, it's this organic beard oil. It's the best value on this list, at $3 per fluid ounce. Given how quickly you can go through beard oil—just think, if you're applying up to twice a day, that dollar-per-ounce math becomes important. So, in addition to being my budget pick, this also gets my recommendation as the best beard oil for big beards.

Of course, it's only important if the formula measures up—and here, it does. There are two carrier oils, argan and jojoba, along with a bit of grapefruit peel and some sandalwood essential oil for both a natural scent and a touch of antimicrobial powers. It's a great beard oil for anyone, no matter how big your bank account, but if you're bearding on a budget, you'll appreciate how much goodness Pura d'Or packs into a few bucks.

Blind Barber's tonka-tinged oil is wonderful for the guy who wants to moisturize skin and whiskers with the same product. It wears extremely light—so much so that my oily skin barely registers its presence—and I find its carrier-oil-centric and shea-butter-forward formula especially comforting on the skin. It should be noted that this formula includes artificial perfume, so fragrance-averse individuals should steer clear. But for those of us who want a special scent, a flake-free beard, and soothed skin, Blind Barber's beard oil does it all.

While I'd use most of the above options as both a hair oil and a beard oil, Aesop's is one where I think of it as a hair oil first and foremost. It brings control without making things look greasy, while trapping moisture and stopping frizz. I'll use it in short hair to tame down the sides, in medium lengths (mixed with a cream or clay) for added shine, or in long hair to prevent split ends. Then, once my hair's settled up, I'll run my hands through my beard afterward. So, if you want a great hair oil with beard benefits, Aesop's is the winner.

Since the beginning of the men's grooming boom, I've been waiting for the big designer fashion houses to start catering to guys with facial hair. For now, Tom Ford's beard oil stands alone. So, how does the designer oil stack up against our everyday favorites? After trying it out, I can tell you it’s better as a fragrance than a beard oil, but the scent alone earns it a spot in this guide.

The tobacco vanilla fragrance is much more sophisticated than what we’re used to seeing, or smelling, in this space. The scent puts an emphasis on vanilla, and you just need one or two pumps for full coverage. Considering the price and the name on the bottle, we were shocked to find this oil comes in a plastic pump bottle, but that fragrance. If you want to buy beard oil as a gift, this premium conditioning oil is a step up over your average Amazon brand, even if it doesn't soften coarse hair as well as some of our other options.

If you have sensitive skin—or if you're afraid of your beard oil clashing with your daily cologne—then Marlowe makes one of the only fragrance-free beard oils. Most beard oil formulas contain both essential oils and some type of perfume oil (or, parfum, for my fellow ingredients detectives), which can be risky for your skin, follicles, and pores, so it’s nice to have an option for acne-prone and sensitive skin types.

This beard oil couldn’t be simpler—and I mean that in a good way. Marlowe No. 143 has an all-oil formula and just four ingredients: safflower seed oil, sunflower seed oil, corn oil, and soybean oil. That’s it. Considering the average beauty product contains up to a dozen ingredients we can’t even pronounce, it’s a refreshing change. Although, I have to say, if you want the softest possible beard, Honest Amish also makes a fragrance-free oil.

Most guys use beard oil to tame flyaways. But what if you want the thickest, fullest beard possible? In that case, I’d recommend reaching for Bevel’s oil. This brand makes skin and hair products for Black men, but anyone with coarse or curly hair can benefit from their products. GQ contributor Timothy Beck Werth reports that this oil gave him a thicker-looking beard without excess frizz, which isn't easy to do. (BTW, that’s as much as you can expect from any beard oil, so please don’t fall for the “beard growth serums” you’ll find all over the internet.)

For parched facial hair, the grapeseed, macadamia, jojoba, and argan oil will condition your beard and add a healthy shine. For beneath the scruff, the avocado and primrose oil with soothe your skin without clogging up your pores and follicles. Werth says this beard oil instantly softened his facial hair, and he also liked the sweeter smell of the macadamia-based formula. Most beard oils opt for woodsy fragrances, and the more subtle scent profile here is a nice surprise, he explains.

Anthony’s conditioning beard oil is a 2-in-1 product—a pre-shave oil and regular beard oil. That approach can be risky, since a lot of multipurpose products try to be everything and succeed at nothing, but luckily that’s not the case here. If you regularly cycle between clean-shaven, stubble, and full-on lighthouse keeper, then you can still use this product daily. As a pre-shave oil, it moisturizes the skin and softens facial hair for a smoother shave with fewer morning-after red bumps. As a beard oil, it hydrates, then locks in that moisture so your beard stays soft and shiny all day. I found the consistency to be somewhere in between a leave-in conditioner gel and a traditional oil, making it thicker and less messy to apply.

The oil also has an herbaceous, minty fragrance that’s so refreshing it feels like aromatherapy every time you run it through your hair. That’s because the formula is full of ingredients that smell good, feel good, and actually help your skin and hair. It uses carrier oils like soybean and castor to deliver skin-pleasing and antimicrobial botanicals such as rosemary, basil, tea tree oil, eucalyptus, and spearmint. And on the hydration front, calendula and olive oil nourish your whiskers into their softest, fluffiest selves. Because of the more complex fragrance and ingredients profile, this wouldn’t be my pick for guys with sensitive skin. Still, it’s a multi-functional serum you’ll look forward to using every day.

Lots of beard oils promise to get flyaways under control, yet those rogue hairs keep popping up. So, what do you do? Instead of trying yet another oil, try reaching for a product that’s more of a leave-in conditioner for your beard. We’ve previously praised Zeus’s beard brushes, but the California-based brand also makes a beard serum that deserves a spot in your bathroom cabinet. I love that this beard oil has the simplest possible ingredients list: sweet almond oil, soybean oil, argan seed oil, trihydroxystearin, avocado oil, and fragrance.

The only multi-syllable ingredient there is a simple thickening agent, which gives this refined beard oil a gel-like consistency. That thicker formula will also put flyaways in their place. The very subtle fragrance is more Mediterranean than winter pine forest (we tested the Verbena Lime version), but you can also opt for a fragrance-free formula if you have sensitive skin or allergies. The plastic bottle uses an air pump, letting you get every last drop of oil. For clean freaks who hate the messiness and spillage that always comes with beard oil application, this is another bonus.

Tired of being left out of couple’s skincare night just because those stupid sheet masks won’t hold onto your glorious beard? Cardon’s got you, boo. Actually, this bead oil hack is perfect for guys who only sparingly use beard oil on an as-needed basis.

The bamboo and charcoal sheet mask is specially shaped for guys with beards, and each mask comes with a dose of beard oil made with argan and jojoba oil. In the Venn diagram of Korean skincare and beard care products, this sheet mask-beard oil duo is the tiny, microscopic bit of overlap in the middle.

The best best oil should support your skin and your beard, and Baxter of California's sweet-smelling oil doesn't sacrifice one for the other. In his guide to the best beard care products, GQ's Timothy Beck Werth said that “In our testing, no beard oil absorbs as quickly or spreads as evenly as this one.” Squalane helps nourish the most overlooked skin on your face, while avocado oil, vitamin E, and botanical extracts smooth out your beard.

As with anything you put on your skin, the ingredients are what matter most. And with beard oils, there are two categories of ingredients: carrier oils and essential oils. No two ingredients will perform exactly the same. Some will help with very specific situations or conditions; some may actively aggravate your skin. Essential oils in particular can aggravate skin if used by themselves. So, consider the following carrier oils and essential oils, favored both by myself and Alyssa Bachowski, a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg, NYC barbershop.

Carrier oils—often vegetable and see oils—make up the bulk of these beard oil formulas, bringing beard nourishment and skin hydration. The most popular carrier oils are argan oil and jojoba oil (both of which Bachowski recommends), though there are dozens of varieties, from castor to marula to grape seed oils. They're usually unscented, though some—like coconut oil—may carry an aroma. Because carrier oils make up the “base” of the oil, they should be among the first to appear in a product's formula or list of ingredients.

“Jojoba oil is non-comedogenic, which means it will not clog your pores. It is also a lighter oil that is absorbent, so it won't just sit on top of the hair and give you a greasy appearance,” Bachowski notes. Along with argan oil, you’re going to see a lot of jojoba oil in most popular beard oil blends.

“Argan oil is filled with a ton of vitamins and minerals, as well as linoleic acid, sterolins, and squalene. All of these aid in protecting and keeping your skin and beard healthy,” Bachowski says.

“Castor oil is known for promoting beard hair growth,” says Bachowski. It’s hypothetically the best beard-growth oil of the crew, because it acts like a vitamin for your strands—though there's more on that below in the FAQs. But don't expect it to grow more hairs, or thicker hairs. Castor oil does have a smoke-and-mirrors benefit, at least. “It is a heavier oil and dark in color, so it can give the appearance of a denser beard on men with dark facial hair,” Bachowski says.

Grape seed oil can help tone down your skin's oil production, and is extremely moisturizing for scruff and skin alike.

Rich in fatty acids, this is a frontrunner for “most nourishing oil” for your skin and whiskers.

Yes, shea butter is in fact a carrier oil, and it has supremely moisturizing properties that make it great for detangling scraggly beards and softening stiff hairs.

Essential oils are distillations from more potent sources, and they tend to be additive—and active—ingredients in a beard oil. These oils are typically extracted through steaming methods, and their range of benefits can include antimicrobial properties, oil-toning abilities, and soothing superpowers. Typically, any natural fragrance in a beard oil will come from essential oils. But pure essential oils can be extremely damaging to the skin.

“You should not apply pure essential oil to a beard—it will do the opposite of what you are trying to achieve,” Bachowski says. “These [pure oils] are not good for your beard nor your skin. as they can be irritating and harmful. Essential oils can cause dryness, itchiness, and redness—and some people may have allergic reactions to them.” This is exactly why you want beard oils from trusted, tested brand.

Bachowski likes cedarwood oil for its naturally astringent properties. “It helps cleanse the pores and prevent ingrown hairs,” she adds. It also adds a natural fragrance.

“Sandalwood has anti-inflammatory properties that help reduce any skin irritation,” Bachowski says of this deliciously scented oil.

Lavender helps calm inflammation and redness, says Bachowski. Plus it brings lavender's soothing scent.

This antimicrobial, anti-inflammatory, oil-toning ingredient is terrific for acne-prone faces and oily skin types.

Along with its refreshing scent, grapefruit peel oil helps nourish and detangle.

As GQ's lead grooming product reviewer, I've tried just about every beard oil in the biz. The ones chosen for this roundup excel at protecting and moisturizing both skin and hair, with minimal risk for things like inverse reactions and blemishes. They absorb cleanly, unless they expressly choose not to (like those which expressly use silicone), and they smell terrific (with both natural and artificially scented options). I've tested beard oils for a decade, on a beard that's been long, short, and everything in between. I've also scoured online reviews to confirm that these products are backed by throngs of loyal fans, whose collective opinion echoes my own experiences.

What is the best beard oil according to GQ?

With more than a decade of testing beard oils under his belt, GQ’s expert tester chose Honest Amish as the best beard oil. And 29,000 Amazon shoppers have rated this same product 4.5 out of 5 stars.

What is the difference between beard balm vs. oil?

Both beard balm and beard oil are both terrific for facial hair—but they don't do the same job. Beard balm is optimal for taming and styling facial hair, especially at medium and longer lengths. Beard oil provides less control over the shape and style, but is excellent at softening and conditioning the hairs and the skin underneath (preventing beard itch, beard dandruff, and split ends).

After using a beard wash, most beard oils are applied the same way: “Start off with 2-3 drops in the palm of your hand,” says Bachowski. “Rub it into your hands so that it is evenly distributed from fingertips to the heel of your hand. Start by rubbing the oil with your fingertips onto the skin beneath the beard hair, then eventually working the oil from root to tip of the actual beard. Make sure to also apply oil to your neck and cheeks where you may have shaved or trimmed as well. If you feel like the 2-3 drops wasn't enough based on the length or density of your beard, apply another 2-3 drops.”

Preventing beard dandruff starts with changing your skincare regimen. Namely, introducing exfoliation and moisturizing products that can get past a thick nest of facial hair. For exfoliation, look for physical scrubs (even a beard brush will work). For the best moisturizing effects, be sure to apply beard oil both morning and night. The beard oil will also soften and condition the facial hair to prevent itchiness.

No, beard oil is not meant to help beard hair growth, whether that’s to make facial hair thicker or grow faster. To be honest, we don’t know of much that can really change your scruff's density besides a beard transplant and maybe minoxidil. Beard oil is here to make your existing beard hair softer and more cooperative, while also nourishing the skin beneath the beard—skin that’s often prone to dehydration and dandruff. It’s already doing a lot.

Absolutely. Beard oil is a great investment for anyone with facial hair, since it can help soften and tame your scruff while simultaneously moisturizing the skin underneath. This means no more itching, no flyaways, and no beard dandruff.

Alyssa Bachowski is a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg NYC barbershop location.

#6698. Cranberry Meringue Pie

A big, billowing cloud of meringue tops this crimson beauty and acts as a sweet reprieve from the tart cranberry curd. The vibrant filling is made even more sour with a generous amount of lime juice. Think lemon meringue pie but holiday-ified. Use our Perfect Pie Crust as the base to create a sturdy-flaky foundation, and be sure to start the pie the day before you plan to serve it, as the curd requires several hours of chilling to set into a sliceable texture. Cut into it too soon and it will run onto your plate, creating a messy—albeit delicious—slice.

We never make pie dough without a metal bench scraper. Our favorite is from Gestura. Use the scraper to cut butter, fold dough and nudge it into shape, transfer it from counter to plastic wrap, trim edges, cut strips, and, sure, scrape flour off your counter.

Welcome to Bon Appétit Bake Club, a community of curious bakers. Each month senior Test Kitchen editors Jesse Szewczyk and Shilpa Uskokovic share a must-make recipe and dive deep on why it works. Come bake and learn with us—ask questions, share pics, listen to our podcast, and lots more here. And don't forget to join the Bake Club Group chat over on Substack.

3 hours (plus 30 minutes for freezing and 5 hours for chilling)

Whisk 1⅔ cups (208 g) all-purpose flour, 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and ¼ tsp. baking powder in a large bowl to combine. Add 2 oz. chilled cream cheese, cut into 1" pieces, and rub into dry ingredients with your fingers until incorporated (mixture will look very shaggy). Add 10 Tbsp. chilled unsalted butter, sliced ½" thick, and toss to coat in flour, then smash with your fingers into smaller, flatter pieces (about chickpea-size).

Make a well in the center of mixture and pour in ¼ cup ice water. Toss with a fork or your hands to distribute, then knead in bowl a couple of times to bring dough together (some dry bits may remain).

Turn out dough onto a surface lightly dusted with rice flour or all-purpose flour and dust top of dough with flour. Roll out to a ½"-thick rectangle. Fold dough in half, rotate 90°, and fold in half again. Roll out again to a ½"-thick rectangle and repeat folding process, dusting with more rice flour as needed to prevent sticking. Pat into a ½"-thick disk and wrap tightly in plastic. Chill at least 2 hours and, preferably, up to 12 hours.

Let dough disk sit at room temperature 5 minutes. Roll out dough on a lightly floured surface to a 12" round, about ¼" thick. Brush excess flour from dough and wrap around rolling pin. Transfer to a 9"-diameter pie pan (such as East Fork Spun Iron Pie Dish). Unfurl into pan, then lift edges and allow dough to slump down into pan. Trim, leaving about a 1½" overhang. Fold overhang under so crust is flush with edges of pan and crimp as desired with lightly floured fingers. Set pan on a rimmed baking sheet and prick bottom and sides of crust with a fork in several places. Freeze, uncovered, at least 30 minutes and up to 2 hours.

Place a rack in lower third of oven; preheat to 400°. Line crust with a sheet of parchment paper and fill with pie weights or dried beans. Bake crust, tenting edges with foil if browning too quickly, until edges are set and look dry, 20–25 minutes. Carefully lift out parchment paper and weights and continue baking crust, gently pressing bottom and sides occasionally with a flat-bottomed measuring cup if puffing up, until bottom looks dry and is golden, 20–25 minutes more. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let crust cool.

Do Ahead: Dough can be made 1 month ahead. Wrap disk in 2 layers of plastic and freeze instead of chilling. Thaw in refrigerator 12 hours before using.

Bring 1 lb. fresh or frozen cranberries, 1½ cups (300 g) granulated sugar, and ¼ cup water to a boil in a large saucepan, then reduce heat to medium and simmer, stirring often, until cranberries soften and burst and most of the liquid has evaporated, 10–15 minutes. Let cranberry mixture cool.

Transfer cranberry mixture to a blender; reserve pan. Add ⅔ cup fresh lime juice and ¼ cup (32 g) cornstarch and blend on high speed until smooth. Scrape purée back into pan (not over heat) and add 3 large eggs, 2 large egg yolks, 1 Tbsp. vanilla extract, and 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt. Place over medium heat and cook, whisking constantly, until mixture bubbles and thickens to a pudding-like consistency, about 4 minutes. Remove pan from heat. Add ¾ cup (1½ sticks) unsalted butter, room temperature, cut into pieces, and whisk until melted and combined. Strain curd through a fine-mesh sieve, pushing with a rubber spatula, directly into crust. Smooth into an even layer and cover with plastic wrap, pressing directly onto surface. Chill pie at least 4 hours and up to 1 day.

Just before serving, beat 6 large egg whites, room temperature, ¼ tsp. cream of tartar, remaining 1¼ cups (250 g) granulated sugar, and remaining ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in the bowl of a stand mixer fitted with the whisk attachment on medium speed until foamy and sugar is dissolved, about 5 minutes. Increase speed to high and continue to beat until stiff peaks form, about 3 minutes more.

Spoon meringue over filling and swirl decoratively as desired. Using a kitchen torch, toast meringue if desired.

#6696. The Best Sparkling Water Maker, Based on Years of Testing (2026)

Dedicating some counter space to a soda maker comes with several benefits. For one thing, these appliances are a great way to cut down on single-use plastic and other waste by eliminating the recycling bags full of bottles, cans, and cardboard boxes you inevitably accumulate with a regular La Croix habit. But they will also save you some money in the long run, as carbonating your own water costs less than buying premade fizz from the store. One standard-size CO2 canister, which should cost roughly $15, can carbonate around 60 liters of water. Considering the $3 cost of a 2-L bottle of Canada Dry, that comes out to around three times as much soda water per dollar.

After years of testing soda makers, I’ve found models that make it quick and easy to make zingy, fizzy drinks (including juice, wine, and cocktails) with the push of a button. Find our top picks below and scroll further down to see what I thought of the machines that didn’t earn a top spot.

New in this update: For this update, I tested two sparkling water makers by Drinkmate: the previously discontinued (and now recontinued) Drinkmate Spritzer and the Drinkmate Lux, which in between testing and publishing was subsequently discontinued and replaced with the new Sparkleup, welp!

Drinkmate Omnifizz Sparkling Water and Soda Maker

Amazon (With 60-L CO2 Tank and Extra Bottle)

Height: 16.14"Machine material(s): PlasticBottle material: PlasticComes with starter CO2 tank: Yes (size varies by retailer)Requires proprietary CO2 tank: NoWarranty: 2 years

Over years of testing, nothing has surpassed the Drinkmate Omnifizz’s mix of versatility and affordability. It is, full stop, the most affordable sparkling water maker that can also do other “fun” carbonating, like wine, cocktails, tea, and (pulp-free) fruit juice.

What I love: The Omnifizz has a well-designed detachable siphon with fast and slow pressure release tabs. It carbonates fast, delivering maximum sharp fizz in around two seconds. This gives you more control when carbonating anything with a syrup or other sugars (wine or cocktails, for example), which fizz up a lot more than plain water. The siphon design also offers a much more secure connection than the machines that require you to pop and/or twist the bottle into the base, which diminishes the likelihood of an accidental soda explosion to the face (which happens to the best of us!)

What I’d leave: In addition to being kind of meh in the looks department (especially in a world where so many brands have placed a focus on the aesthetics of their soda makers), this carbonator is made of all plastic parts, so it doesn’t feel as durable as some others I’ve tested. It also screams like a banshee when its done. Depending on the retailer you order from, it may or may not ship with a CO2 cylinder, so be sure to read the product descriptions carefully.

Height: 16.9"Machine material(s): Stainless steelBottle material: PlasticComes with starter CO2 tank: Only if you buy the bundleRequires proprietary CO2 tank: NoWarranty: 2 years

Breville has cornered the market on high-end small kitchen appliances with its top-performing countertop oven, induction burner, toaster, and immersion blender. In 2024, the brand added a sparkling water maker to that list.

What I love: The Breville Infizz Fusion is a sleek, versatile carbonator, and like the Drinkmate Ominifizz, it will carbonate more than just water. The default setting on the carbonator cap is a slow pressure release that allows carbonation of fruit juice, wine, cocktails, and even creamy drinks if you’re feeling weird. You can manually release the pressure faster if you just need a bottle of fizzy water in a hurry. Unlike the soda makers I liked in the $100 to $150 range, which usually have all plastic parts, parts of the InFizz Fusion are made of durable, high-quality stainless steel, which is available in a variety of finishes, though options vary by retailer. It also comes with a handful of accessories (including a juice filter and silicone bottle brush) to enhance the experience of using and cleaning the machine and bottle.

What I’d leave: Compared to the Drinkmate, this machine is slightly more fussy to operate. Not by much, but I prefer the Drinkmate’s quick click to Breville’s twist-and-lock design. Countertop soda makers are simple gadgets, and this is one of the priciest ones you can buy. If it’s worth it to you to pay for quality materials, aesthetics, and the ability to wow your dinner guests by transforming a bottle of bad rosé into something bubbly and palatable, you’ll likely be very happy with this (FWIW the Drinkmate can also sparkle bad rosé, it just wont look as nice doing it). But do keep in mind that the base model, which is $249 at the time of publishing, doesn’t ship with a CO2 tank, so you’ll either need to buy one or spend $30 more on one that comes with the tank.

Drinkmate Spritzer Portable Sparkling Water

Height: 7"Machine material(s): PlasticBottle material: PlasticComes with starter CO2 tank: Only if you buy the bundleRequires proprietary CO2 tank: NoWarranty: 2 years

What I love: When I first tested the Spritzer several years ago, it was my top pick. But then Drinkmate discontinued it, which was sad, but now, it’s back. At this point I can’t give it the absolute top spot (I’ll get into that down below), but in a more niche position of portable carbonator, I still recommend it.

The Spritzer has all the versatility of Drinkmate’s countertop model in a smaller, portable format. Seeing as the earliest carbonators were portable, it’s almost a return to form. Almost, because the Spritzer looks more like one of those guns that shoots money and not like something you’d see in the hands of an old-timey soda jerk. But much like a gun that shoots money, the Spritzer is very fun to use. In terms of user experience, I prefer the Spritzer’s trigger over the standard, button-on-top-of-the-machine design. It just feels like I have more control over the thing when I use it.

Beyond that, the Spritzer takes up less space and is easy to stow away, and it is also cheaper than the countertop version.

What I’d leave: The Spritzer is designed to use smaller 10-L gas canisters rather than the standard 60-L size. Unless you source them directly from Drinkmate, the 10-L canisters are difficult to find, so you end up being reliant on its exchange program. The Spritzer will still work with a full size canister, but I wouldn’t recommend doing that because it doesn’t fit well in the carbonator and gets a bit unwieldy. Drinkmate also originally had a docking station for it the Spritzer, but unfortunately the brand got rid of it. Ultimately, the reason I don’t give this the top spot is that its design is such a departure from what most people expect in a soda maker it has a much narrower appeal.

With every update I look at the most popular and highly rated countertop sparkling water makers, taking into consideration availability, price, and customer ratings. Then, I judge them on the following:

I first assess how easy it is to use and assemble each machine. Looks matter too, but they aren’t everything. And if a machine can carbonate more than just water (what's up Drinkmate and Breville), that’s a huge plus as well.

I used each machine several times to get a general sense of how long the machine takes to carbonate and the fizz level of each batch. For the first few rounds I follow the manufacturer’s instructions. After that I try pushing the machine to its maximum capability (usually until I hear the hissing of excess gas escaping). If the machine can carbonate more than just water, I’ll do a few rounds with juice and wine.

This simple, stylish carbonator from Finnish brand Mysoda is made from renewable wood composite. It’s beautiful, sturdy, and, when it comes to the level of carbonation, performs as well as any SodaStream. And if you want an aesthetically pleasing, sustainably made, budget-friendly soda maker, you’ll be very happy with this purchase. But there’s a catch to that: Most soda makers ship with a starter CO2 tank, and this one doesn’t. New tanks, not tank trade-ins, cost $30 to $40. So when you factor that into the cost, the initial investment is more than the cost of our top pick, which did perform a little better anyway. If you want to stay under $100 all in, check out the Philips Sparkling Water Maker below.

This Mysoda distinguishes itself from the others with a glass carafe. Anytime I encounter a soda maker that carbonates in glass, I'm suspicious, because I haven’t seen them work well in the past. This might be because soda makers that carbonate in glass tend to be designed with additional safeguards to minimize the possibility of the bottle exploding, and those safeguards are usually fussy. Often they take the form of some sort of cover that goes over the entire bottle during the carbonation process. In the case of the Glassy, the bottle is covered by a curved sliding door that you shut by hand, reminiscent of a changing room curtain or a shower stall.

To use the machine, you place the bottle inside and twist it onto the gas nozzle to lock it in place. Then you shut the door and press the top of the machine toward the front to inject the gas (the top is mostly one large button). After a couple of pumps, you’ll hear a hissing sound, which signals that it’s ready. You then press the top toward the back of the machine, which causes the door to fly open, presenting your carbonated water like a magician’s assistant doing a quick change magic trick. It’s all very dramatic, which is kind of fun.

As far as soda makers that carbonate in glass, this is the best I’ve tried. That said, I'm not sure how important it is to be able to carbonate in glass, especially with the risk (however small) that it will explode into shards with you close by. You can always just carbonate in a plastic bottle and pour it into a glass vessel for serving.

Check out our full review of the Glassy here.

The Philips Sparkling Water Maker is a handsome machine with a comparatively accessible price tag, and it’s easy to set up and operate with one button. More importantly, during testing, it made pleasantly sharp carbonated water. Like the Mysoda Woody above, it doesn’t ship with CO2, so you’ll still need to shell out for a standard screw-in 60-L CO2 tank. However, at the current retail price, even with the addition of a tank, it still comes in at just under $100.

The Aerflo is even easier to tae on the go than the Drinkmate Spritzer. There is a carbonator mechanism inside the lid, which you activate by pressing a button on the top. Instead of full-size CO2 canisters used for most sparkling water makers, it utilizes smaller capsules typically used for airsoft or BB guns. Because of the capsules small size, you will need to swap them much more frequently; one capsule has enough CO2 for about one day’s worth of sparkling water. It’s cool and convenient if you really prefer carbonated water over still. But this is a product for a smaller subset of people than a countertop soda maker. If it appeals to you though, go for it.

Check out my full review of the Aerflo here.

This is the model that came after the now-discontinued Aarke Carbonator II, and the main upgrades are materials. The Aarke Carbonator III has more stainless steel and fewer plastic parts than the II. Both models are highly aesthetic machines that are easy enough to use and make perfectly fine sparkling water. If you like the look of it, you’ll probably be very happy with it, but the Breville is in the same general price range and is more versatile. Plus, there are lower-priced machines that perform just as well.

Awa appears to be selling itself entirely based on aesthetics. Once you move beyond the body design though, this is a standard sparkling water maker with a single button for injecting CO2. It lacks a tilting nozzle or a slide-in lock, which makes it more annoying to use than the winning models. The build is also almost entirely plastic, save for a few metal details, which doesn’t really justify the premium price point. If you like how it looks, it will get the job done, but it’s not innovating on the form.

A frequent complaint people have about sparkling water makers is their reliance on plastic bottles, as many consumers are looking to avoid plastic entirely. The Fizzit is one of the few soda makers that uses metal bottles instead. Apart from that, its design and performance are similar to the other soda makers sharing the same standard design on the market. Were this the only machine on the market to carbonate directly into stainless steel, maybe that would be something, but Sodastream also offers stainless-steel carbonator bottles, and I imagine more brands will follow suit. Also sorry to Spärklï, but the branding on this machine is a bit of an eyesore. If you’re looking for a machine that comes with metal bottles and aren’t a fan of Sodastream though, this might be for you.

The Fritaire carbonator flipped the conventional soda maker design on its head. It’s the only soda maker I’ve tested so far that carbonates from the bottom up, with a fold-up gas nozzle that makes for a slightly more compact machine. The bottle is also different, with mechanical components built in at the bottom, and a wide curved carafe-style lip. Unfortunately I could not get this to produce a satisfyingly sharp bottle of seltzer, no matter how much gas I pumped into the bottle. Where other machines make a clear sound when the pressure has been maxed out, the Fritaire’s was not obvious. The instructions recommend pressing the button for three seconds until you “hear the sound of pressure building.” Each time I tried, the water came out under-carbonated. On top of that, the lid for the bottle dribbled water even when tightly screwed on. I really tried to give this machine the benefit of the doubt, testing and retesting to try and get it not to leak on me and to get a proper fizz, but each time I encountered the same problems no matter what I did.

Smeg is known for its retro-futurist luxury kitchen products that really sell for their looks. This machine worked fine. You twist the metal dial clockwise to carbonate, a clever hidden feature that sets it apart from other carbonators, but it lacks the ability to tilt the nozzle, which makes screwing on the bottle more tedious. For a soda maker made almost entirely out of plastic, the price point is well beyond what it’s worth, but if you’re a Smeg collector, this is another piece to add to your collection.

One of the few new electric sparkling water systems, the Carbon8 is quite different from other machines on the market. Instead of directly carbonating into the bottle, the Carbon8 has a refillable reservoir (much like a drip coffee maker) that instead, dispenses carbonated water straight into your glass. It has added flourishes, like a sanitizing UV light and a filter that remineralizes water while removing chlorine.

The brand implies superior eco-friendliness in their website copy because they don’t carbonate directly into a reusable PET plastic bottle; however, the reservoir and other parts of the machine are made of plastic, so I’m not sure they have much plastic-free ground to stand on. You could argue that since the plastic bottles of typical soda makers have to be replaced every couple of years, this system produces less plastic waste, but it’s a marginal difference in my opinion.

As a premium machine this Carbon8 worked fine, but had one shortcoming: There seems to be no way to control the carbonation level of the machine, and when I tasted the finished product, I found the level of carbonation to be far less than what you’d expect from store-bought seltzer, or what you can produce with the winning machines. If the Carbon8 gave you control over how fizzy you wanted your water, it would make this a much better machine.

Ninja is among the most prolific small appliance manufacturers with coffee makers, countertop ovens, blenders, ice cream makers, juicers, toasters, air fryers—the list goes on and on and on. So it should come as no surprise that the brand is now in the soda maker business too. Like many of Ninja’s other offerings, the Ninja Thirsti is a bit gimmicky while still being kind of cool. Out of the box, this electric soda maker (yes, you have to plug it in) reminded us of a Nespresso machine. The water tank sits off to the side and the carbonation happens inside the machine before coming out of a little spout. There’s an opening on the front to add up to two proprietary flavoring cartridges, which you can put in your bubbly water individually or combine them both. Currently there are 30 flavors available and the machine shipped with four of them. The control panel on the front of the machine allows you to choose between still water or three levels of carbonation, and four drink sizes (6, 12, 18, or 24 oz.). After testing, I have mixed feelings about this machine. Overall, it seems like a bit much for something to have at home. It could maybe be a great addition to a teachers’ lounge or a small office. But keep in mind that the water tank does require chilled, cold water so you’d also need a fridge or ice nearby whenever you want to use it.

If you’ve ever dreamed of having a club soda tap in your kitchen, this is probably as close as you’re going to get without cutting into your countertops. Like the Ninja above, the Glacier Fresh Sparkin Cold Soda Maker carbonates water in the machine, not in a bottle. Unlike the Ninja, the machine chills the water for you (though the manual still recommends filling the reservoir on the back of the machine with cold water). Once it’s filled, getting your bubbly water from the “tap” is a three-step process. First, you push a button to send water from the reservoir to the bubbling (and presumably chilling) chamber, then you push down on a lever to carbonate it. Once it’s sufficiently bubbly, you press another little lever on the front to dispense the water into a glass. It’s less complicated than it sounds, but with an 88-square-inch footprint, most people will be better off with a bottle-style carbonator. I will also note that when moved from the counter to a nearby table with the CO2 canister in place and some water still in the chamber (ill-advised, perhaps), it began to make a weird squealing noise.

The SodaStream Art is marketed as the company’s premium carbonator, because of its retro-inspired body. Though it produces the exact same carbonated water as other SodaStream models, it operates a little differently: Instead of pushing a button to carbonate, you pull the lever and you can pull it up to six times for the bubbliest water. But it’s made of lightweight plastic that feels cheap. If you want something that looks good on the countertop, there are prettier options that are also sturdier.

Aarke carbonators are really easy on the eyes, and this “pro” version doesn’t skimp on materials. Instead of the typical plastic bottles, this one uses glass, and the top of the machine (which is available in a matte black, matte sand, or shiny stainless-steel finish) covers the top of the bottle in case you were to over-carbonate and cause the glass to explode. Unfortunately, it’s not very user-friendly. I had issues getting everything to lock into place and ended up with an explosion—though, luckily, it was only water and not glass. I eventually got it to work, but for the price, it should be easier to operate.

I loved the sleek design of this soda maker in pictures, but in person, the materials felt cheap and flimsy. Also, because it’s so sleek and slim, the water bottles are really tall and didn’t fit into any shelves in a standard sized fridge.

Instead of using a CO2 tank to produce carbon dioxide, this machine uses citric acid and baking soda. The mechanism is just way more high maintenance compared to the machines that utilize a gas canister, and I struggle to see why this method is in any way better than the industry standard.

Currently, the Terra is SodaStream’s most basic offering, and functionally, it’s a lot like the original SodaStream, the Fizzi. However, instead of a screw-in CO2 cartridge, this machine uses the brand’s Quick Connect cartridges, which click in. Because the Quick Connect feature is proprietary, using this machine means being married to Soda Stream’s cylinder exchange program. With products like the Mysoda Woody or the Philips Sparkling Water Maker, you can and should get something with a universal connection.

This style of carbonator is a relic of the 19th century, and while it has an abundance of charm, it’s more tedious, fussy, and impractical than its modern counterparts. It relies on small, single-use CO2 cartridges—which negates the whole eco-friendly argument for carbonating at home rather buying seltzer from the store— and you have to use two cartridges per liter if you like your seltzer extra sharp. Frankly, the only people I’d recommend this to would be that husband and wife on TLC who pretend they’re living in the 1950s.

There is nothing inherently wrong with the E-Duo. Of the carbonators that use glass bottles, it is the easiest to use. It does require a few additional steps to lock the bottle safely into place (just in case that glass bottle explodes!), but I didn’t find the safety mechanisms to be overly fussy. However, as with the Ninja, this soda maker requires electricity. With so many excellent soda makers that function effectively without electricity, I don’t really see the need to use one that does.

#6695. Bourbon Caramel Apple Pie

The one thing that can make classic apple pie better? Caramel! That it’s laced with a generous dose of bourbon vaults it straight into hall-of-fame territory. Made using a double batch of our never-fail Perfect Pie Crust, this pretty lattice-topped pie is a crowd-pleaser, familiar enough to appeal to the traditionalists with just enough of a surprise from the booze and the smoky burnt sugar to make the thrill-seekers come back for more. Like all good things, apple pie can’t be rushed. It needs time to chill, bake, and rest—so plan ahead, especially if it’s for Thanksgiving.

Welcome to Bon Appétit Bake Club, a community of curious bakers. Each month senior Test Kitchen editors Jesse Szewczyk and Shilpa Uskokovic share a must-make recipe and dive deep on why it works. Come bake and learn with us—ask questions, share pics, listen to our podcast, and lots more here. And don't forget to join the Bake Club Group chat over on Substack.

2 hours 15 minutes (plus 7 hours for chilling and cooling)

Whisk 3⅓ cups (417 g) all-purpose flour, 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and ½ tsp. baking powder in a large bowl to combine. Add 4 oz. chilled cream cheese, cut into 1" pieces; rub into dry ingredients with your fingers until incorporated (mixture will look very shaggy). Add 1¼ cups (2½ sticks) chilled unsalted butter, sliced ½" thick, and toss to coat in flour, then smash with your fingers into smaller, flatter pieces (about chickpea-size).

Make a well in the center of mixture and pour in ½ cup ice water. Toss with a fork or your hands to distribute, then knead in bowl a couple of times to bring dough together (some dry bits may remain).

Turn out dough onto a surface lightly dusted with rice flour or all-purpose flour; dust top of dough with flour. Roll out to a ½"-thick rectangle. Fold dough in half, rotate 90°, and fold in half again. Roll out again to a ½"-thick rectangle and repeat folding process, dusting with more flour as needed to prevent sticking. Divide dough in half and pat each piece into a ½"-thick disk. Wrap each disk tightly in plastic. Chill at least 2 hours and, preferably, up to 12 hours.

Do Ahead: Dough can be made 1 month ahead. Wrap each disk in 2 layers of plastic and freeze instead of chilling. Thaw in refrigerator 12 hours before using.

Place 1 cup (200 g) granulated sugar and ½ cup water in a medium Dutch oven or other heavy pot and cover. Bring to a boil over medium heat (no need to stir); cook until sugar is dissolved, 4–7 minutes total. Uncover and continue to cook, stirring or swirling pot occasionally, until caramel turns a deep golden brown, 10–12 minutes.

Add zest of 1 lemon, 3 lb. Honeycrisp apples (about 6 large), peeled, cored, quartered, thinly sliced crosswise, ½ cup bourbon, 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and 1 tsp. ground cinnamon to caramel (it may become stiff). Cover and cook, stirring occasionally, until caramel dissolves and apples have released a generous amount of liquid and are tender but still retain their shape (they should look mostly translucent), 15–18 minutes.

Stir ⅓ cup heavy cream, 3 Tbsp. cornstarch, and 2 Tbsp. cool water in a small bowl until cornstarch is dissolved. Pour into apple mixture and cook (it should be bubbling), stirring often, until mixture is thick, about 5 minutes. Transfer filling to a large bowl; let cool. Cover and chill until cold, at least 45 minutes.

Meanwhile, let dough sit at room temperature 5 minutes. Unwrap 1 disk and roll out on a surface lightly dusted with rice flour or all-purpose flour to a 12" round, about ¼" thick. Brush excess flour from dough and wrap around rolling pin. Transfer to a 9"-diameter pie pan (such as East Fork Spun Iron Pie Dish). Unfurl into pan, then lift edges and allow dough to slump down into pan. Trim, leaving about a 1" overhang. Chill 15 minutes.

Unwrap remaining disk; roll out on surface lightly dusted with more rice flour to a 18x12" rectangle, about ⅛" thick. Brush excess flour from dough and transfer to a large parchment-lined baking sheet. Brush dough with a thin film of cold water; sprinkle remaining ¼ cup (50 g) granulated sugar over and press gently with your hands to cover dough completely in an even layer (coating dough before cutting into strips will give you the most even coverage). Chill 15 minutes.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 375°. Spoon chilled filling into pie pan and smooth surface to level. Using a ruler, cut dough rectangle into 1"-wide strips. Lay 5–8 strips over filling, spacing evenly across surface to cover. Weave another 5–8 strips over, then under, alternating to create a lattice crust (you may not use all of the strips). Trim overhang to match bottom crust. Press edges of top and bottom crusts firmly together to seal, then trim again, leaving about ¼" overhang. Fold overhang up and over lattice so crust is flush with edges of pan and crimp as desired with lightly floured fingers.

Place pan on a rimmed baking sheet (to catch drips) and bake pie until crust is set and golden, 45–50 minutes. Reduce oven temperature to 350°; continue to bake until crust is deep golden brown and filling is bubbling up through lattice, 40–50 minutes more. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let pie cool in pan at least 4 hours before serving.

Do Ahead: Filling can be made 2 days ahead; keep chilled. Pie can be baked 1 day ahead; store, loosely covered, at room temperature.

#6694. Your Thanksgiving Turkey Might Be Free If You Shop At These Stores

A free turkey could be just one shopping trip away.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Turkey Day is coming, and as the name implies, you’re gonna need a turkey. You’ll want to try our classic oven-roasted turkey recipe for the ultimate Thanksgiving centerpiece. But while getting a hulking 15-pound bird onto the dinner table is one thing, getting it into your home in the first place can be another challenge these days. Luckily, a few companies are trying to make it easier this fall to feed your family the turkey dinner they deserve. I scoured the internet to find you the three best deals on the bird.

Instacart is giving away 50,000 turkeys this November, dropping 10,000 birds at noon each day from Wednesday, November 18, through Sunday, November 22. And the best part? They're basically free.

Instacart is offering $40 off a whole turkey (fresh or frozen), which should cover the cost of the bird. All you have to do is spend $50 on your Thanksgiving basket, turkey included. So, essentially, you’re getting a turkey with your weekly grocery haul.

All you have to do is redeem the promotion on Instacart’s Thanksgiving landing page and get to shopping!

ShopRite has been giving away free turkeys since the ’90s, so they’re pros at making sure you have a bird to put at the center of your Thanksgiving table. And they’re back this year with a Free Holiday Favorite promotion!

If you’re a Price Plus Club card member, you can get a free turkey weighing up to 21 pounds. All you have to do is spend $400 on qualifying purchases between October 25 and November 26, 2026. Just shop for your usual groceries, rack up your savings, and redeem your free bird! And if turkey isn’t your thing, you can also redeem your rewards for other holiday favorites, including ham or chicken.

If you stopped by BJ’s Wholesale Club to shop in the past few weeks, you might already qualify for a free turkey. Check your receipts to see if you spent $150 in a single transaction between October 31 and November 9, either in-store, online, or through the app. If you did, you can head to your local BJ’s and redeem a free Butterball turkey valued at up to $25. You can pick up your bird between November 14 and November 25.

#6693. How Will Robert Pattinson’s James Bond Dress?

When Terence Young, the director of Dr. No, needed a suit for Sean Connery to wear for his debut as James Bond, he took him to see his personal Savile Row tailor, Anthony Sinclair.

“Connery’s suits were considered relatively contemporary, because Anthony always had a very soft shoulder on his suits,” explains Elliot Mason, the director of business at Anthony Sinclair, which is no longer located on Savile Row, but still makes classic British suiting. “Anthony liked to nip in the waist, so it gave that real hourglass silhouette to the suit, which really is quite timeless. And as was the trend at the time, the Connery suits all had a relatively high rise, double-pleated with English forward pleats, and most of his trousers had Daks button-tab adjusters on the side.”

Opting to dress James Bond in a Savile Row suit might seem like a no-brainer in hindsight, but the decision—and the cut of the suits themselves—still resonates more than 60 years later. Sinclair would make all of Connery’s suits during his nine-year run as James Bond, and his blend of classic British tailoring and of-the-moment influences would create a template for every Bond to come. With Robert Pattinson—a Dior ambassador and a guy with a reputation for adventurous style—chosen to take up the mantle in Bond 26, the question now becomes: what will he wear?

Tailor Anthony Sinclair fitting Sean Connery for one of the suits he wore in 1963’s From Russia With Love.

Ian Fleming, the author who created James Bond, spent a lot of time talking about what Agent 007 drank (Wolfschmidt Vodka) what he drove (Bentley convertibles, for the most part) and how he made his coffee (he was a dedicated pour-over guy), but, surprisingly, he never specified where the spy bought his clothes. As a result, for the last six decades, it’s been up to the directors and costume designers of the 25 previous films to create a new look for every successive Bond. In the Connery and Moore eras it was Savile Row suits. Timothy Dalton’s Bond wore Paul Smith. Brosnan was a bespoke Brioni guy, as was Daniel Craig, who also brought Tom Ford into the mix. At 6’1” to Craig’s 5’10”, with a leaner build, and a long track record of fashion-forward dressing, Pattinson has everything needed to reinvent Bond once again.

“I’m pretty sure we’re going to see something quite different from Pattinson's Bond,” says Matt Spaiser, the tailoring obsessive behind BondSuits.com and the author of From Tailors with Love: An Evolution of Menswear Through the Bond Films. “Pattinson is a different kind of actor, and he’s going to wear something that moves differently. Plus, he’s 18 years younger than Daniel Craig.”

The actor playing Bond has a major influence on how he dresses—Daniel Craig, an adventurous dresser himself, was very hands-on with the wardrobe in his films—but Pattinson’s ongoing contract with Dior could also factor in. “Some of his Dior looks look somewhat like James Bond—he wears a great tuxedo—but I don’t think it’s going to tell us what he’s going to wear as James Bond,” Spaiser says. “He’s worn suits from other brands in other roles—like in Tenet, he wasn’t wearing a Dior suit—so I don’t think he's necessarily obligated to be wearing Dior as James Bond.”

Daniel Craig, seen here in 2019 with then-Prince Charles on the set of No Time to Die, mainly sported sharp Tom Ford suiting in his later Bond films.

Another important factor that contributes to a new Bond’s look is what’s happening in fashion whenever the film eventually gets made. “One thing that we've seen throughout most of the Bond eras is that he dresses in suits that are fairly traditional, but there's always some nod to the era that they’re in,” says Spaiser. “Sometimes they lean a little more into fashion, like when Roger Moore was wearing flares. That was something that I think they couldn't avoid without making Bond look too old-fashioned at the time.”

Tone also plays an important role, and historically has ping-ponged between serious (Connery, Dalton, Craig) and more campy (Moore, Brosnan), but with Denis Villeneuve, the auteur behind Sicario, Blade Runner: 2049, and Dune in the director’s chair, the smart money isn’t on hovercraft gondolas and punny Bond girl names. A darker, more atmospheric milieu could make for an interesting complement to Pattinson’s taste for high-fashion.

If Mason were dressing Pattinson for the role, he says, he’d take a lead from his clientele, many of whom, like Bond, are well-heeled Londoners with a taste for expensive sports cars and well mixed cocktails. “The majority of our customers are reasonably conservative, and when it comes to fit, they’re sensitive to current trends,” Mason notes. As such, he’d opt for longer jackets, both single- and double-breasted, that are fuller through the body, and straight-leg pleated trousers. “It’s not that very, very slim fit that we'd seen for so many years, but it still needs to be refined,” he says. “I don’t think it would be appropriate to put him in wide-leg or baggy trousers. I just don't think it’s right for Bond.”

Robert Pattinson, the latest 007, has been the face of Dior’s menswear for over a decade.

Given the intense scrutiny on every relaunch of the Bond franchise, and the tendency of fans to get worked up online over perceived mishandling of a beloved character, Pattinson, Villeneuve, and the film’s yet-to-be-announced costume designers have little room for error. Go too conservative and they’ll be accused of playing it safe; make Bond into a fashion guy, and they’ll incur the wrath of the traditionalists.

“He needs to look current, but also timeless,” Spaiser says. “It’s a hard balance to find.”

REI and Beams Revived Your Dad’s Built-Tough Parka From 1986

Brooklyn Beckham’s Shirt Wants to Know: Is It Clocking to You Yet?

Josh Hartnett Just Declared Open Season on Overcoats

#6691. A Beautiful Cabbage Galette

Cut this festive galette into little squares for a hearty appetizer, or serve sizeable wedges as a vegetarian main or standout side. It’s worth seeking out savoy cabbage, the ruffly sort that looks like lace. Ordinary green cabbage, which has a higher moisture content and flatter texture, doesn’t work here (we tried).

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Whisk 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt, ¼ tsp. baking powder, and 1⅔ cups (208 g) all-purpose flour in a large bowl. Add 2 oz. chilled cream cheese, cut into 1" pieces, and rub in with your fingers until incorporated (mixture will look very shaggy). Add 10 Tbsp. chilled unsalted butter, sliced ½" thick, and toss to coat in flour, then smash into smaller, flatter pieces (about chickpea size). Make a well in the center and pour in ¼ cup ice water. Toss with a fork or your hands to distribute. Knead dough in bowl just to bring together (some dry bits may remain).

Turn dough out onto a lightly dusted surface and sprinkle with all-purpose flour. Roll out to a ½"-thick rectangle. Fold in half, rotate 90°, and fold in half again. Roll out again to a ½"-thick rectangle and repeat folding process, dusting dough with more flour as needed to prevent sticking. Pat into a ½"-thick disk and wrap tightly in plastic. Chill at least 2 hours and, preferably, up to 12 hours.

Do Ahead: Dough can be made 1 month ahead. Wrap disk in 2 layers of plastic and freeze instead of chilling. Thaw in refrigerator 12 hours before using.

When dough is almost done chilling, cut 1 medium head of savoy cabbage (1¾–2¼ lb.) in half and cut out triangular core at bottom; discard. Separate leaves and tear into pieces about the size of your palm. Transfer about half to a large bowl and set aside.

Melt 1 Tbsp. unsalted butter in a large skillet over medium heat. Add 1 medium white or yellow onion, thinly sliced, and season generously with kosher salt. Cook, stirring occasionally, until softened and starting to turn translucent, about 5 minutes. Add remaining 1 Tbsp. unsalted butter and ¾ tsp. cumin seeds (if using) and cook until butter is melted. Add remaining cabbage leaves and cook, stirring occasionally, until onion is translucent and lightly browned and cabbage is wilted, 18–22 minutes. Transfer to a large plate or a small baking sheet; chill in fridge or freezer until cool.

Place a rack in bottom third of oven; preheat to 400°. Whisk 2 garlic cloves, finely grated, ⅓ cup heavy cream, 2 Tbsp. Dijon mustard, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a measuring glass to combine. Pour cream mixture over reserved raw cabbage leaves and toss with your hands to coat. Add 5 oz. Gruyère or sharp cheddar, coarsely grated, and toss again to combine.

Unwrap dough and roll out on a lightly floured sheet of parchment paper to a 13–14" round (some dough will extend beyond the parchment). Transfer on parchment paper to a baking sheet.

Spread cooked cabbage mixture over dough, leaving about a 1½" border around edges. Fold dough up and over filling, folding and tucking as needed to make a rustic border. Pile raw cabbage mixture on top of cooked cabbage mixture to make a dramatic heap. (It will look ridiculously high at first but will cook down when it bakes.)

Bake galette 25 minutes. Rotate baking sheet front to back, reduce heat to 375°, and bake until cabbage and crust are deep golden brown, 25–35 minutes. (If cabbage seems to be browning too much, reduce heat to 350° toward the end.) Let galette cool on baking sheet at least 1 hour and up to 4 hours before serving.

Do Ahead: Sautéed cabbage can be made 1 day ahead. Transfer to an airtight container and chill. Tightly wrap undressed raw cabbage in plastic and chill.

#6690. On the Front Lines of the Advent Calendar Wars

Welcome to Open Tab, a weekly column covering the news, gossip, and stories that have stayed open in my tabs all week. Last time we covered the mysterious hackers hitting refrigeration systems.

I’m starting today’s Open Tab with my favorite thing in the world: a morsel of exclusive news. In chatting with BA alum Alison Roman about First Bloom, her soon-to-open NYC storefront (more on that from me very soon), she told me she’s releasing a new podcast that will be officially announced early next week.

I’m mostly sworn to secrecy until Roman drops the trailer, but I can tell you there will be cooking, there will be guests, and there will be banter. But, she said, this project will be distinctly different from her earlier work. “I’m not going back to Home Movies. I’m just not,” she told me over the phone earlier this week.

Also in today’s Open Tab: How much more ridiculous can advent calendars get? Was this NYC gay bar actually designed by AI? And how is Andy Baraghani thinking about his first restaurant?

Gone are the days when advent calendars were essentially manufactured cardboard inside of which you could find some of the most unfortunate tasting chocolate ever produced. These days we’ve moved beyond chocolate. Some advent calendars open to reveal tinned fish, others jewelry, and still others skin-care products. Google Trends data shows search interest for advent calendars has grown steadily for the past five years, and as writer Emily Bloch reported for Bon Appétit, the global advent calendar market is expected to grow to $2.47 billion by 2033.

Part of the appeal, as Bloch mentions, is the little-treat-culture of it all. It’s nice to open up a little surprise each day. But another element, surely, is the content that’s made around advent calendars. There are the unboxings—tailor-made for the short-form vertical video format which rewards reveals—and there are the scandals. Who could forget (everyone except me, realistically) the backlash against Chanel’s $825 advent calendar in 2021 when customers declared the included products decidedly lackluster? This year Skims faces a similar backlash for its $148 advent calendar for which it is now offering refunds.

But drama and outrage gets attention, which in turn fuels more content. And as the Kardashian clan knows better than most, no matter what the public is saying, at the end of the day they’re still talking about you. Thus, the advent calendar cycle continues for another year, growing ever greater. Will we ever summit the peak of the advent calendar craze? So far, the data say no.

If you’ve read—or even skimmed—this column in the past, you’ve likely gleaned that I often point out the many fumblings of AI—one could argue I make a sport of it. That’s why, when I saw grumblings online that a new gay bar in New York City’s Hell’s Kitchen looked like it had been designed by Chat-GPT, I felt a morbid curiosity begin to bubble. The bar, commenters said, was so deeply on-the-nose gay-bar-coded, it simply couldn’t have been made by a human brain. It was named Pride Bar x Lounge (a bit first thought, right?), and its decor is essentially just rainbow flags. It kind of looks like how they’d design a gay bar in a Netflix show, which is to say: almost impressively bland.

In a report from The New York Times, the two co-owners say that they did not in fact use any AI in designing the bar, which has cost about $5 million so far to come to fruition. On the other hand, the posters released to promote the bar, which had also received some heat, were in fact made using AI.

The saga is an interesting data point in the public’s perception of what is an appropriate way to use this tech: making a grocery list? Sure. Using it to help you figure out what exactly you like about your own child? Absolutely not. Asking AI to help you plan a trip? I guess. Using it to design a gay bar? Hard no.

Former Bon Appétit food editor Andy Baraghani is likely familiar to readers of this publication. But now, after years of cooking for others—on the line at Chez Panisse, here in the Test Kitchen—he’s opening his own restaurant, Ditto, in Union Square. “There’s an explicit confidence you’ll see,” he told Ella Quittner, who profiled him for BA. Read on to see how Baraghani’s vision comes to life at his first ever restaurant.

#6689. How To Roast Butternut Squash

Roasting is my favorite way to prepare butternut squash, hands down. The high, fast heat of the oven brings out the squash’s natural sweetness and eliminates excess moisture, concentrating all that flavor into a smaller package. Once roasted, the squash is ready to be pureed into soups, tossed into grain bowls—or simply eaten by the forkful. While many roasted butternut squash recipes restrict seasonings to just salt and pepper, I’m going a little extra here. Yes, there is a basic recipe for simply seasoned squash if you want to puree it into soup or use it as the base for a creamy orzo skillet, but I’ve also given you a few other options to try if you want to give the squash more personality. The garlic-herb variation is just begging to be tossed into a salad as “squash croutons,” and the sweet ’n’ spicy is so good I almost ate it all, straight off the baking sheet.

For the basic roasted butternut squash recipe, preheat the oven to 425° with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil to make for easier cleanup.

Spread the cubed squash out onto the foil-lined sheet, drizzle with olive oil, and sprinkle with salt, then use a flexible rubber spatula to toss the squash until everything is evenly incorporated. Spread the squash back out into an even layer, making sure the pieces are not crowding each other. (If they’re too close together, they’ll steam and you’ll lose some of that great caramelization.) Season the squash with freshly ground black pepper.

Roast the squash, undisturbed, until tender and lightly caramelized on the bottom, 25 to 30 minutes. Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

For the sweet ’n’ spicy variation, preheat the oven to 425°, with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil.

In a large bowl, toss together the squash and olive oil. (I know it might seem weird to use a bowl here when we’re just tossing on the baking sheet for the basic version, but this is because the spices tend to stick to the baking sheet and burn while the squash is roasting.) Sprinkle the garam masala, salt, sweet paprika, cumin, and cayenne over the squash, and toss everything until evenly coated. Finally, drizzle the spiced squash with the honey and toss to combine.

Spread the seasoned squash out on the foil-lined baking sheet, making sure the pieces don’t crowd each other too much. Roast, undisturbed, until tender and caramelized on the bottom, 25 to 30 minutes. (I recommend checking at the 20-minute mark to make sure the pieces of squash on the outside of the baking sheet are not caramelizing too fast on the bottom. If they are, just flip the pieces that are getting dark on the bottom.) Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

For the garlic-herb variation, preheat the oven to 425°, with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil.

Spread the cubed squash out onto the foil-lined sheet, drizzle with olive oil, and sprinkle with salt, then use a flexible rubber spatula to toss the squash until everything is evenly incorporated. Spread the squash back out into an even layer, making sure the pieces are not crowding each other.

Roast the squash, undisturbed, until it’s just beginning to be tender (about 20 minutes), then remove the baking sheet from the oven and sprinkle with the garlic, thyme, and sage, then return the baking sheet to the oven and roast until the garlic is golden and the squash is tender and lightly caramelized on the bottom, about 10 minutes more. Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Roasted butternut squash is wonderful fresh, but it also makes good meal prep! If you’d like to make it ahead of time, store in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 3 days.

Nutrition (per serving): 135 calories, 2 g protein, 19 g carbohydrates, 6 g fiber, 4 g sugar, 7 g fat, 1 g saturated fat, 487 mg sodium

Bakeware and cookware courtesy of Crate & Barrel.

#6688. Who Is Sara Boustany, the Breakout Star of Netflix’s ‘The Body’?

What’s that? A TV show that isn’t a prequel, a spin-off, or a remake and that contains a cast of relatively unknown yet talented actors, none of whom are nepo babies?

It sounds too good to be true, but The Body is one of this year’s big hitters. The Netflix series—about a dance troupe at a Catholic high school in 2004 who accidentally send one of their friends into a “minimally conscious state” after a Satanic ritual at a Halloween party—is the teen drama you shouldn’t sleep on. Think Jennifer’s Body meets The Crucible, and you’ll come close. “It’s not just about being gay,” explains Gabby Windey, one of the show’s supporting players. “It’s about being hot and gay.”

But who are the show’s big breakout stars? Lebanese-American actor Sara Boustany, who plays Elise—the mean girl with a heart of gold—appears to be emerging as one to watch already, with well over half a million Instagram followers and counting (every time I check, it goes up), unusual for someone with such a sparse IMDb page. It’s easy to see why viewers have fallen in love with her character, though. As Elise, she’s fierce, independent, emotionally complicated, and kind of an outcast despite being one of the “popular” girls. Oh, and she’s a killer dancer. (An improvised dance to Jeff Buckley’s “Lover, You Should’ve Come Over” is a standout moment from the show.)

But who is Sara Boustany, the actor, and what do we know about her?

While the characters in the show don’t reveal their ages, we can estimate that they’re between 16 and 18 years old. (The sneaky weird girl Lisbeth, played by Geena Meszaros, another standout, at one point refers to herself as a “senior,” which would make her 17 or 18.) In reality, Sara Boustany is 23. Considering that Rachel McAdams was 25 years old when she played Regina George in Mean Girls, this discrepancy in age is in keeping with the show’s very Noughties spirit.

As mentioned earlier, Boustany has a relatively sparse list of credits under her belt, with The Body being her breakout starring role. However, she did appear in Dolly Parton’s Heartstrings, an eight-episode anthology series on Netflix that tells the story behind some of Dolly’s most famous songs. Before then, in 2019, she made her professional debut on Maya Erskine and Anna Konkle’s cult-favorite teen comedy PEN15, also set in the 2000s.

If you’ve never heard of Rommulas, all you need to know is that he’s a 22-year-old Mexican-American musician with around 430,000 monthly listeners on Spotify. And, while neither has confirmed their relationship in any official or public capacity, both he and Boustany have been pictured at events together, including at Gucci’s 2027 resort show.

Fun fact: Boustany originally auditioned to play Elise’s co-dance captain—and complicated love interest—Anya (Kristina Bogic). The casting directors, however, immediately saw her as Elise instead. “I weirdly didn’t get why,” Boustany told Teen Vogue earlier this month. “Elise is so quippy, and her defence mechanism is to be bitchy, which isn’t really me. But I’d only read the pilot (“Slutty God”), and I was so focused on Anya and trying to get on the show. If I’d stopped to think about it for even 30 seconds, I would’ve been like, Oh, yeah. This is a much better direction for me.”
