#6687. 15 Best Zip-Up Hoodies for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Surveying the best zip-up hoodies for men takes me back to my younger days as a hopelessly romantic high school kid. My formative years were marred by angst, soundtracked by pop punk, emo, and indie rock. The piece of clothing that got me through it all? A zip-up hoodie. It was core to the indie aesthetic, with every forlorn frontman sporting one over a thrashed band tee, skinny jeans, and shredded Chucks. But nostalgia never seems to stay in the past, especially when fashion is involved.

Gen-Z has revived every minute piece of 2000s pop culture, which means zip-up hoodies are back and have all but usurped the pullover hoodie as the sweat du jour. The zip-up is inherently versatile in both practical wear and sheer styling dexterity. With the flick of the zip, you can put your beloved Death Cab for Cutie tee on full display while letting your pits breathe, or keep it zipped up when it's just a bit too nippy out. To find the best zip-up hoodies on the market, I dusted off Transatlanticism and went on a deep dive. The good news is that the old classics are still good. The better news is that there's a lot of great competition to choose from.

Uniqlo’s no-nonsense clean aesthetic and low price point has made it a go-to for any menswear enjoyer looking for good style on a budget. While the brand excels at cashmere sweaters, T-shirts, and a slew of homerun collabs, the Japanese label’s sweats are criminally underrated. We’d go so far as to say that they should be talked in the same breath as brands like Champion and Sunspel. This zip up hoodie took us by surprise thanks to its comfy midweight, all-cotton fabric and solid construction. But the main selling point is the impeccable fit.

So many other brands cut their hoodies too long in the body and leave you looking like Jack Skellington’s pubescent brother. Uniqlo managed to cut its French terry hoodie to sit right at the hips which makes for a more classic and natural silhouette that’s neither lanky nor sloppy. Because of the fabric, the feel, and the overall fit, this is my favorite zip-up hoodie by a sizable margin. It’s my most-worn hoodie and has yet to show signs of any fraying.

Hoodies were originally meant for sportswear, as warm up gear before a grueling game of football. These days, they’re more often worn for lounging on the couch to watch the kickoff. And if you want to kick it in comfort while saving your pennies for booze and amuse bouche for the big game with the boys, the best budget zip up hoodie to buy is Hanes’ EcoSmart zip hoodie. The fit is spot on—relaxed, but not sloppy, comfy in the body and the sleeves without swallowing you like a sand pit.

The fit is impressive for the price, and the overall quality means you’re getting a little more than what you pay for. It’s not a hoodie that’ll last you for too long, but it is a good quality piece for just 15 bucks. And when other hoodies offer lesser quality and worse-fitting options for about the same price? The answer is clear.

It’s hard not to sing the praises of Reigning Champ, a brand that built its fleece empire on sweatpants and hoodies. The Canadian label’s flagship midweight terry fabric has drawn the attention of streetwear royalty and menswear buffs alike and its dedication to quality has elevated the humble hoodie to oxygen-deprived heights. We named Reigning Champ’s crewneck sweatshirt and pullover hoodie as among our favorites in their respective categories, and the same goes for its zip-up hooded sweatshirt. It’s made of the same 100% cotton French terry fabric as its superlative-earning brethren and is cut with high-quality flatlock stitching for a smooth, un-bulky feel.

I had high expectations for Reigning Champ, given my previous experience, and I’m not surprised at all with how nice this zip-up is. And while I'm partial to the standard fit, it’s also available in a slim fit version, too.

Hoodies live in a universe well beyond the sportswear world from which they originated. And while we still enjoy the OGs and the classics that made the hoodie a bona fide menswear must-have, we also love seeing all the ways the style has been pushed, tweaked, and freaked. Our Legacy's Level Hood has been a successful avenue for the Stockholm-based brand to explore the limits of the hoodie and this particular version strikes a delicate balance between the freaky and the fashion-curious. It's made of an English wool knit into an all-over ribbed texture for a throwback '90s feel. The cropped, boxy silhouette and elongated sleeves harken back to the grunge-y era, while the sultry dual-tone nightshade colorway throws you off in the best way possible.

Menswear influencers will be happy to regale you with their TedTalk on the golden ratio of clothing proportions. Essentially, that means your outfit should be divided into three equal sections where the top takes up one third of the outfit. Basically, your tops should be cropped and sit closer to your natural waist. Follow it up with some high-waisted pants with a wide silhouette and you’ve instantly garnered 300K views on TikTok (RIP). With so many indie brands and influencers scrambling to make their own versions of “the perfect cropped hoodie” you’re probably tired of wading through all the choices. Do yourself a favor and go to Abercrombie.

The Moose’s version is backed by actual trend forecasters, actual designers, and actual fit models. What does that mean, exactly? It means that its cropped zip hoodie fits really well and uses surprisingly beefy fabric. It was a revelation for me, too—Abercrombie wasn’t a name I associated with hoodies, but after I saw this in person, I had to readjust my menswear word cloud.

Some of the best zip-up hoodies for men are made by a brand you never see in brick and mortar stores. Camber has been crafting the most durable sweats for decades, right here in America and have been the shining example of the difference between good quality and great quality. Sure, you have to order through an old-school web 1.0 website and wait for several weeks to actually get your hoodie. But trust us when we say that the relatively minor inconvenience is completely worth it.

The heavyweight fleece is almost as thick as a leather jacket and can stand in as outerwear if you wanted it to. While other hoodies are washed to hell in giant industrial washing machines to reach a super soft texture by the time it gets to you, Camber’s actually require you to do the work yourself. It sort of feels like you’re being swaddled by an anaconda, which means if you’re a proponent of hard clothes like raw denim, you’ll like Camber’s hoodie a lot.

Cashmere zip hoodies, once the exclusive province of Silicon Valley unicorn founders and PJ-flying nepo babies, have been democratized, none better than Naadam's take. You know the brand for bringing thoughtful, sustainable cashmere pieces at “really, only that much?” prices. Its zip hoodie is as soft as it gets, with extra credit points awarded for coming in a classic fit (too many cashmere hoodies err on the side of slim) and six colors, including this decidedly handsome olive. Wear it on weekends, work-from-home Fridays, and long flights—even if it's just Economy+.

Shoutout to brother in arms Louis Cheslaw, GQ Recs contributor, whose writing for the newsletter Magasin put us on to young brand Cease. From seed to sewing, it's beefy 20-ounce cotton is fully American-made. Assembling the fabric vertically means the hoodie will shorten up to a slightly cropped, oversized fit. Act fast if you want some of the less-boring colors—they move fast.

We can’t deny Russell Athletic’s contributions to the hoodie space. And while its formula has changed over the years, the new hoodies are still pretty good for the price. The fabric is soft and lightweight, and it’s available in a rainbow of colorways. Plus, if you’re looking for a hoodie with a little more length, this is a great option.

Bold graphics aren’t usually deemed classics, but Gap’s retro-leaning collegiate-style block logo has staying power almost as much as the Levi’s red tab. Add to that a wonderfully fluffy midweight fleece material and a tailored straight fit and you’ve got something you’ll see on moodboards for generations to come.

Champion’s Reverse Weave fabric is iconic for its nearly shrink-proof quality and almost unsettlingly rough exterior. Flip it to the inside and you’ve got a luxuriously brushed interior that’ll have you feeling like you’re melting into a cloud. Heads-up that pricing varies kinda wildly by size on Amazon.

The secondhand market is chock full of navy-blue hoodies that have been sun-faded and beaten down with time. They'll cost you just as much as this Mfpen version, but will also fall apart faster than you can zip 'em up.

Les Tien's dedication to all things sweats results in a catalog of luxurious fleece and terry cloth with a modern approach. Featuring high-end Cobrax hardware, RiRi zippers, raw-edge hems and sleeves, and a heavyweight brushed fleece, no detail is phoned in.

The Real McCoy's is considered by many to be Americana repro wizards. This revival of an early 20th-Century hoodie is just one small piece of the brand's preservation prowess. It's made using beefy sinker-knit fleece, old-school Talon-style zippers, and vertical patch pockets.

A.Presse's chokehold on the menswear illuminati refuses to let up and it's pieces like this vintage-inspired hoodie that keep the pressure on. Based on very rare “after-hood parkas", this made-in-Japan hoodie features an idiosyncratic attached hood, super dense and lofty fleece, with artfully distressed details throughout.

Think of the zip-up as the pullover hoodie's elder brother, but with way more layering potential. In its most natural state, it can cuddle up to a matching set of sweatpants and a baggy tee with flair. (Top it off with a swanky necklace, crisp sneakers, and some stellar shades and you’re left with a fit on par with Shai Gilgeous-Alexander at his tunnel-trotting best.)

Unzipped and left open, it can also take on the role of a picture frame ready to display your sickest graphic tee—much like the cardigan, another treasure of 2000s fashion. Or make like a mid-Aughts garage band ironically donning corporate attire and wear your zip-up over a dress shirt and baggy tailored trousers, a high-minded fashion move with a jolt of kooky Scandinavian cool.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6686. 26 Thanksgiving Desserts That'll Have Guests Fighting For The Last Bite

There's always room for one more dessert.

#6685. The Best Nontoxic Cookware Trusted by BA Editors (2026)

If you’ve shopped for nonstick pots and pans in the past decade, you’ve had the term nontoxic cookware thrown at you with increasing velocity. As you've thought about getting new pans you may have even searched for the best nontoxic cookware without being totally sure what “toxins” they supposedly do not have in them. So what exactly makes cookware “toxic,” and why do these “safe” or “healthy” pans claim to be better?

The answer begins with a class of synthetic chemicals known as PFAS, short for per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances. These chemicals, which repel liquids, have been used in everything from waterproof jackets to nonstick cookware coatings, and after decades of use, the health concerns raised by scientists started to break through to the public more broadly in more recent years.

Whether you’ve never heard of PFAS or you’re already familiar with their nearly 100-year-old history, you’re in the right place if you want to avoid them. We’ve tested dozens of nonstick and “nontoxic” pans to find the best options to help you keep PFAS out of your cookware. Read on for the lowdown on all things nontoxic cookware.

When companies label their cookware “nontoxic,” it’s a marketing move as much as anything, designed to position it as a safer alternative to traditional nonstick, which is typically coated in a chemical called polytetrafluoroethylene, or PTFE. And to understand why people began to colloquially refer to old nonstick pans as “toxic,” we need a little history of PTFE.

Discovered accidentally in 1938 and trademarked by chemical company DuPont as Teflon in the early 1960s, PTFE was an earthquake in the home cookware market during the second half of the 20th century because it provided consumers with a completely nonstick, no-oil-needed cooking surface. By the turn of the century, Teflon had become ubiquitous, finding its way into a staggering number of American kitchens in the form of PTFE-coated skillets, muffin tins, baking dishes, and more.

PTFE, though, is just one of thousands of a class of chemicals in PFAS family mentioned earlier. Around 1951, DuPont started using another PFAS called PFOA (short for perfluorooctanoic acid) in its Teflon manufacturing process. Over time, it became clear that PFOA could enter the human body by way of gaseous fumes or chipped coatings and have detrimental effects on human health and the environment, including increased risk of certain cancers, birth defects, ulcerative colitis, and thyroid disease. Under mounting pressure from public health and environmental advocacy groups, manufacturers phased out PFOA by 2013.

These days traditional nonstick cookware is made using PFOA-free PTFE (which is also typically free of PFOS, another harmful PFAS). You’ll see it marketed under names like T-fal, Dura-Slide, or hard-anodized aluminum. While PTFE itself is considered less harmful than other PFAS, there is still a broader concern. Thousands of PFAS compounds remain unregulated, and there is ongoing research into the class of chemicals as a whole. A label that says “PFOA- and PFOS-free” does not necessarily mean the product is free of all potentially risky chemicals in the PFAS family.

While not necessarily the first recorded use of the term, the idea of “nontoxic” cookware was largely introduced to consumers when GreenPan released the first PFAS-free ceramic nonstick frying pans in 2007. Since then, “nontoxic cookware” has come to mean “PFAS-free nonstick cookware.” While there’s nothing inherently incorrect about this characterization, it’s important to recognize the degree to which it has become a marketing buzzword.

While “nontoxic cookware” typically refers to ceramic nonstick, which is made from silica using a method called the sol-gel process, any cookware that’s made without the use of harmful chemicals like PFAS can be considered “nontoxic.” That includes some tried-and-true options you probably already know and love: stainless steel, cast iron, and carbon steel.

Ceramic nonstick comes closest to mimicking the ease of traditional Teflon, but like any coating, it will degrade over time and can chip (more on that below). That’s why we recommend getting comfortable with uncoated stainless, cast iron, and carbon steel. With the right technique, your food won’t stick to them either, and they’ll last a lot longer.

Available sizes: 8", 10", 12"Induction compatible: YesDishwasher-safe: Yes, but hand-washing recommendedMetal-utensil-safe: Theoretically, but wood or silicone recommendedOven-safe: Up to 600ºFWarranty: 1 year

About ceramic nonstick: I have to be honest here, I've personally sworn off coated nonstick pans for good, because no matter which one you buy, it will only last you a few years tops before the coating starts to degrade and you need to replace it. That said, I understand why people gravitate towards them—and if you're dead-set on buying one, ceramic nonstick is the way to go. These pans are typically made of stainless steel or aluminum and lined with a silica-based coating that makes them nice and slick—for a few years, at least. The exact makeup of that coating varies brand to brand, which is why we’ve spent years testing lots of ceramic nonstick pans to find the ones that best deliver on their nonstick promise.

Our top pick: GreenPan’s ceramic nonstick pans were the first to hit the market in 2007, and the brand’s products have consistently come out on top in our best nonstick pan testing. Its Valencia Pro line is our favorite for a few reasons: It’s hefty enough to feel durable while remaining lightweight enough to be easily maneuverable. It’s also affordable enough that you won’t have to break the bank when you inevitably need to replace it in a few years, and more scratch-resistant than some earlier GreenPan iterations we've tried. Oh, and it's induction compatible too.

A budget option: If you don’t cook on an induction stovetop and are looking for a cheaper option, you can go with GreenPan’s Paris Pro line, which performed similarly to the Valencia in our testing.

Deeper Carbon Nonstick Frying Pan & Glass Lid

Available sizes: 8", 10", 12"Induction compatible: YesDishwasher-safe: NoMetal-utensil-safe: YesOven-safe: Up to 1,100ºFWarranty: Lifetime

About carbon nonstick (or nitrided carbon steel): At the outset, these pans act like traditional nonstick but can hold up to high heat, metal utensils, and years of use because they aren’t coated. Instead the carbon steel is nitrided, a process that infuses nitrogen into the metal to make it tougher, slicker, and both corrosion- and rust-resistant.

Our top pick: When we first got a hold of Misen’s Carbon Nonstick pan it seemed like the holy grail for anyone trying to quit Teflon without giving up easy-release eggs. Unlike traditional carbon steel pans, Misen’s version has an aluminum core that heats quickly and evenly, and its surface repels oil straight out of the box. In testing, fried eggs, chicken thighs, and sautéed onions all slid right off with minimal oil (food director Chris Morocco said it was “practically allergic” to eggs). The pan is lightweight and durable and will develop a terrific nonstick seasoning. However, as our lead cookware tester Wilder Davies continued cooking with the Misen, he noticed the initial corn oil seasoning layer started to wear, and the miraculous oil repelling characteristics decreased. Once that happened we assigned four more people, myself included, to work with these pans and we all noticed a reduction in the nonstickiness as time went on. However, the nonstick curve on these has proven to be more U-shaped, as we've continued to cook on and season them, food release started to become easier again.

Read our full review of the Misen Carbon Nonstick Pan.

D3 Stainless Steel 14-in Skillet with Lid

Available sizes: 10", 12", 14"Induction compatible: YesDishwasher-safe: NoMetal-utensil-safe: YesOven-safe: Up to 600°FWarranty: Limited lifetime

About stainless steel: Stainless-steel cookware is a staple in home and professional kitchens because of its versatility, durability, and low maintenance needs. Use it on the stovetop, in the oven, or with metal tools—unlike shorter-lived ceramic pans, a stainless-steel skillet can serve you well for a lifetime. And here’s the thing: With the proper technique, stainless steel can release almost as easily as ceramic nonstick. The key in guaranteeing your food slides right off instead of becoming a stuck-on mess is using sufficient fat and heat. Make sure to wait until your oil is shimmering before adding whatever you’re cooking, give it enough time in the pan to let the maillard reaction do its thing, and you should have no issues with stickage.

Our top pick: We’ve tested many types of stainless-steel cookware throughout the years here at BA, and one brand has repeatedly stood out time and time again for its consistently well-made, durable, high-performing products. That brand is All-Clad.

In our testing of stainless-steel frying pans and sauté pans, product testers and Test Kitchen editors alike lauded All-Clad’s D3 Everyday line as the best mix of value and performance for home cooks. The “3” refers to these pans’ 3-ply construction, which features an aluminum core sandwiched between two layers of stainless-steel cladding. That aluminum allows the pan to conduct heat quickly and evenly, and it makes the pan more lightweight than solid stainless-steel cookware. In our testing, we found that those promises held true, and that the D3 pans were easy to clean (just hand-wash with a little soapy water, plus some Bar Keepers Friend if you need a hand getting any remaining bits off) and have continued to perform well over the many years we’ve been using them.

About cast iron: If you’re willing to put just a bit of work into caring for your cookware, a cast-iron skillet will easily fill the nonstick pan-size hole in your kitchen. Cast-iron cookware will never quite reach the level of nonstick that a Teflon-coated piece offers, but in our testing, we’ve found that a properly seasoned cast-iron pan is on par with ceramic nonstick—plus it only gets more slick over time as you continue to use it.

Our top pick: After testing more than 20 cast-iron skillets over several years, our current top pick is the Lancaster No. 8 because it’s lightweight, it has a smooth pre-seasoned surface that’s nice and slick right out of the box, and it provides even heat distribution. The one downside is that it’s quite pricey compared to something like Lodge’s iconic cast iron, which we recommend as a great budget alternative. The Lodge is probably the pan most people think of when they hear “cast-iron skillet,” and at around $20, depending on where and when you buy it, it’s one of the best values in the cookware world—particularly because you can keep it forever rather than replacing it every few years. In our testing, it performed just as well as many more expensive pans.

Read more about our favorite cast-iron pans.

About enameled cast iron: You may have also heard of enameled cast iron, which is frequently seen in products like our favorite Le Creuset and Staub Dutch ovens. Many people expect enameled cast-iron pots and pans to be nonstick because the ones with light-colored interiors sort of resemble the kinds of ceramic nonstick coatings we wrote about above, but in reality, that’s not the case. That doesn’t mean you can’t cook food in these things without it sticking, but you’ll need to use sufficient fat and heat to make that happen, as you do with a stainless-steel pan. The benefit of enameled cast iron is that it provides superior heat retention and distribution, and protects the cast-iron core from rust and acidity. It's become one of my favorite types of cookware to use at home over the past few years.

Our top pick: The Staub Round Cocotte is my personal top pick when it comes to Dutch ovens, and tied with the Le Creuset in our official product testing. I prefer the Staub’s dark textured enamel interior, which is less prone to showing discoloration. In addition to the round cocotte, I also own the braiser and a deep skillet, both of which I find myself pulling out constantly.

Available sizes: 8", 10.25", 11", 12.5"Induction compatible: YesDishwasher-safe: NoMetal-utensil-safe: YesOven-safe: Up to 500ºFWarranty: Limited lifetime

About carbon steel: Carbon steel is sort of the Goldilocks cookware because it affords you the nonstick qualities and lightweight feel of a coated nonstick pan and the durability of a cast-iron or stainless-steel skillet. The one caveat is that in order to achieve those nonstick qualities, you’ll need to spend some time properly seasoning your new pan, just as you would with cast iron. But with a bit of work at the outset and occasional maintenance, you’ll be rewarded with an heirloom-worthy piece of cookware that has a well-earned nonstick patina.

Our top pick: De Buyer is one of those famed, old European cookware brands that lots of American home cooks may not be that familiar with. Its “blue” frying pan comes pre-seasoned and will just get better and better as the seasoning improves. As senior writer Wilder Davies notes, you can take specific steps to season it—use a thin layer of oil (as thin as you can possibly get) and put it in a hot oven for an hour—but you can also just keep cooking with it and that seasoning will develop on its own. Another nice feature of this pan in particular, and one that has nothing to do with its seasoning, is its welded-on handle. That means there are no rivets to clean around when you’re done.

Read more about our favorite carbon steel pans.

What types of kitchen gear is PTFE found in?

A skillet may be the first thing you think of when you think of nonstick cookware, but PTFE coatings show up in a host of other items in the kitchen and beyond. On top of skillets, pots, saucepans, and Dutch ovens, you'll also find PTFE in rice cookers, waffle irons, baking sheets, and grill baskets.

You may recognize ceramic nonstick as the material of choice of many of the aesthetically-pleasing, Instagram-viral cookware companies that started popping up in the late 2010s (I’m looking at you, Our Place and Caraway). Ceramic nonstick pans are made without any PFAS. Instead, a gel made from silica is applied and then dried onto the cookware’s metal body to create a nonstick surface.

Sounds great, right? Well, there are a few caveats, the main one being that ceramic nonstick coatings are not built to last. That means you’ll want to be sure to avoid using metal utensils when cooking with them, opting instead for wood or silicone. You’ll also want to avoid exposing them to extremely high temperatures: Even though ceramic cookware is technically more heat tolerant than cookware made with PTFE, high heat will wear down the pan’s ceramic coating. Many manufacturers claim their pans are oven-safe up to certain (often high) temperatures, but we’d recommend using one of our other recommendations above for any stove-to-oven cooking, as those trips to the oven will only further degrade that ceramic coating over time. Similarly, despite any of being dishwasher-safe, hand-washing your pans will prolong their lifespans.

Even if you treat your ceramic nonstick cookware with the utmost care and attention, you still shouldn’t expect it to last you more than one to three years before it starts to lose its nonstick properties. That’s why the best nontoxic nonstick cookware is not necessarily a coated piece of PFOA- or PFAS-free gear—in fact, we’d argue the best nontoxic “nonstick” pans money can buy are made of cast iron, or carbon steel. While these materials are not actually manufactured or marketed as nonstick, each has nonstick properties that can be achieved with proper use and care. .

The bottom line here is that no one needs a coated pan in their life. There is no recipe on this website (or in this world) that requires you to use one, and we tend to think you’re better off investing some time in learning to cook with a more durable type of cookware than spending you money on a full nonstick cookware set. That said, we’re recommending a range of cookware options here for those who are looking for alternatives to traditional PTFE-base nonstick, including a ceramic option for people who aren’t quite ready to kiss coated nonstick goodbye.

Dean and Dan Caten set out to design a rebel aristocrat’s wardrobe for pre-fall. Rigorous military uniforms and tailored pieces clashed with punk and glam rock references. Often the designers used “callbacks from previous Dsquared2 collections” as a starting point.

For menswear that meant a leather-effect coated biker jacket and ripped denim pants with rocker vibes. A “Kate Moss attitude” characterized the women’s looks, with outerwear taking center stage. Distressed denim jackets were reworked with couture-like balloon-shaped backs, while a red leather jacket became more sculptural thanks to a metal wire strategically placed inside the hems and the collar. Napoleon jackets were turned out in denim, giving them an everyday aura that was undercut by the Victorian lingerie-inspired garments paired with them. #6681. What You Need to Know About Japanese Knives

The popularity of Japanese knives in home and commercial kitchens has exploded in the last few years, and for good reason: A lot of them outperform their Western counterparts in kitchen tests. Western-style knives are bulkier, all-purpose kitchen tools—years ago, it would be common for many chefs to use the heavy bolster near the heel of the blade to crack open a can of tomatoes during service. Japanese knives (and Japanese-style knives produced by manufacturers elsewhere) are designed with precision cuts in mind and usually have a specific purpose. However, because they’re designed with that specificity in mind, it can be tricky to know what you need and what you’ll use.

Thankfully, we’ve reviewed a wide variety of Japanese-style knives for Bon Appétit and have assembled a roundup to help you identify exactly what your kitchen needs. While there are more styles of Japanese knives than those listed here, these options have all been thoroughly tested, giving us the confidence to speak to each knife’s utility. Before we jump into specific styles, however, it’s useful to give a quick rundown on some of the broad strokes of what makes a Japanese-style knife unique.

Many Japanese knives are designed for push cuts or pull-through slicing rather than the rocking chop of a heavy Western chef’s knife. They tend to have a straighter edge than their Western counterparts, making them slightly less adept at speeding through a mince of herbs, but more precise in their cuts. Most Japanese-style knives also feature a sheep’s-foot tip that curves down to the point, giving the user more control over fine-point work.

Japanese knives are often made with a harder steel than their Western counterparts. Harder steel helps a knife’s edge retain sharpness, which lets manufacturers use steeper sharpening angles for a finer edge. This helps most Japanese knives slice more delicately than Western knives, though harder steel can chip on material like ice or bone.

Traditional Japanese-style knives are only sharpened on one side, giving them a much finer edge than the 50/50 bevel of Western-style knives that are sharpened on both sides. Some modern Japanese knife makers have pivoted to a 50/50 style bevel for ease of sharpening; however, when you’re shopping, you’ll encounter plenty of knives with 70/30 bevel with the inside edge (the left edge for a right-handed knife) sharpened at a steeper angle. A 70/30 bevel is easier to maintain than a single-bevel but offers a sharper edge than a 50/50 bevel.

Many traditional Japanese kitchen knives feature a wa-style handle—an octagonal wood handle that envelops a hidden knife tang (the metal part of the knife that connects to the handle). Wa-style handles help keep knives lighter and nimbler, while many Western-style knives use full-tang construction, adding weight and heft to the blade. Many modern Japanese knife makers, though, combine full-tang knife handles with Japanese blade shapes to create a hybrid-style knife that’s both durable and adept at slicing.

The gyuto, or Japanese chef’s knife, originated in Japan in the early 1900s, when French cooking began to gain popularity in a rapidly modernizing country. This knife style took cues from the traditional French chef’s knife and combined them with elements of traditional Japanese knife making. The resulting Japanese take on a chef’s knife was primarily designed for slicing meat, though it also features a curved edge that makes it easy to chop herbs. Compared to a Western chef’s knife, the gyuto has a straighter blade and a drop point tip with a lower overall profile. This is primarily designed so that the user can place the knife’s tip on the cutting board and draw it through food, taking advantage of the gyuto’s sharp edge and thin blade for precision slicing. But while the gyuto excels at slicing meat, its general shape makes it an overall great pick to anchor your knife collection, and many home cooks opt for a gyuto as their daily driver over a traditional chef’s knife.

We’ve long been fans of the gyuto fan here at Bon Appétit, with MAC’s Professional Hollow Ground Chef’s Knife topping our chef’s knife review for years. For a wicked sharp, more traditional option, we really like the Kazan Arare by Shibata gyuto, which impressed us so much we had to give it its own review.

Increasingly common as a part of brands’ knife sets, the santoku knife is a relatively modern knife shape, first appearing in the 1940s or so. Nicknamed the three virtues knife, the santoku draws on the gyuto, nakiri, and deba to create a well-rounded utility knife that’s great for cutting meat, fish, and vegetables. Typically, these knives feature a pretty steep sheep’s-foot tip for precision point work, a tall heel for push cuts, and just enough curve for pull-through slicing and rock-chopping. A santoku knife is great for well-rounded utility work—think cutting cheese and an apple for a snack, slicing leftover roasted chicken to make a sandwich, or dicing an onion for a curry.

Typically, we like a santoku knife that’s between six and seven inches, so it contrasts with the longer gyuto. Our favorite overall model is the MAC Professional Santoku Knife, but if you’d like a razor-sharp version with a traditional wa-style handle, we love the Kazan Ginsan Nashiji Santoku.

Nakiri knives have a straight edge and a sturdy spine, specifically designed for chopping vegetables. Sometimes referred to as a vegetable cleaver, the nakiri excels at push cuts, making it great for both matchsticking carrots and breaking down tough-skinned squash. Since most gyutos feature thin blades and fine edges, the nakiri also holds a spot in the Japanese knife lineup as a tougher, heavier knife for more demanding tasks. Traditional nakiri knives may also feature a blunt, rounded tip, which can be rotated against the cutting board to create a smooth, chopping motion.

The Tojiro DP Damascus Nakiri is by far our overall favorite tested nakiri knife for its extremely sharp edge and overall balance, but it’s also pricey. We also really like the Tojiro Classic nakiri, though it can have stock issues due to its popularity and price point.

The deba knife is a traditional Japanese knife designed for fish butchery. Unlike a Western fillet knife, which is designed to be flexible and nimble, the deba is a heavy, sturdy blade with a thick spine that gives the heel of the blade a cleaver-like feel. But a deba knife is also designed for precision slicing, and it usually features a traditional single-bevel edge. That single-bevel edge allows the knife to come to a finer point than a double-bevel edge, and the sheep’s-foot tip adds to the deba knife’s ability to portion out delicate fish fillets as well.

We haven’t tested deba knives because they require a bit of knowledge and skill to use them for breaking down whole fish, but if you’re intrigued by the shape and want a cleaver-like knife for your kitchen, the Seki Mago Roku Deba from Kai is an affordable, solid option. Otherwise, we think most people would be better served looking to a nakiri or santoku knife.

Named for the French word petite, which means small, the petty knife is often a replica of the gyuto, just in a much, much smaller form. Most petty knives are around five inches long and feature a thin, lightly curved blade with a drop point tip and heel just tall enough to keep your fingers from bashing into the cutting board. Petty knives are great utility knives for small tasks, like dicing a shallot, slicing garlic, or slicing herbs, but they’re also long and nimble enough to be great slicers. Unless you do a lot of handheld turning and peeling, a petty knife is a great replacement for a paring knife in your lineup.

We found that both the MAC Professional Petty Knife and the Tojiro Classic Petty Knife were sharp enough to slice a steak while still being the perfect size to slice strawberries for your morning yogurt bowl.

Traditionally a fisherman’s knife used for all sorts of purposes on the boat, the funayuki is an old-school knife shape that predates the gyuto and santoku, and it likely helped inspire both shapes. The traditional funayuki is a shorter knife, around seven inches long, with a narrow, pointed tip and a tall heel. They are santoku-sized but gyuto-shaped. Unlike the deba knife, which was a heavy, single-bevel knife used for fish butchery, the funayuki was designed to be light and nimble so that fishermen could fillet their lunch while at sea. These days, most home cooks might pick up a funayuki as a secondary, shorter gyuto, or opt for this shape over a santoku as an all-purpose utility knife with a narrower tip.

Though it’s not as common in modern kitchens, one of our favorite Japanese chef’s knives is the Akiru-Saku Funayuki Gyuto, which is more traditionally shaped like a funayuki but is advertised as a gyuto since the two shapes are so similar.

The kiritsuke is a Japanese slicing knife with an angular “sword tip” that allows the user to make precision cuts. The initial design combined a long slicing shape and tall heel, making the kiritsuke a hybrid knife, similar to the santoku. For most people, the kiritsuke will be a version of an all-purpose chef’s knife that could take the place of a gyuto as a daily driver, with the big difference coming in the shape of the tip.

When reviewing chef’s knives, we found that the Shun Classic Kiritsuke was a great all-purpose chef’s knife with a unique tip that some people might prefer over a gyuto, since it curves upward more like a Western chef’s knife and allows for more of a rock chop motion.

Boning and fillet knives are often unitaskers that don’t see much use unless you’re a fan of home butchery, but the Japanese honesuki is an even more specialized boning knife shape. Designed specifically for poultry, the honesuki is a steep, wedge-shaped knife with a very fine point and a tall heel. The goal is to have a knife that’s delicate enough to slip between a chicken joint while being sturdy enough to cut through cartilage. While the honesuki was designed for a specific purpose, we find that this shape is more versatile and can be swapped in for a petty knife for other small chopping and dicing tasks in ways that a curved fillet knife can’t.

We like the MAC Professional Honesuki as a great option that could tackle most boning-knife tasks while remaining sharp and versatile enough for utility work. If you find yourself breaking down a whole chicken while also wanting a strawberry-slicing knife, the honesuki could be a nonconventional multitasker.

#6680. Dsquared2 Pre-Fall 2027

#6679. Best Corn Casserole

In my opinion, corn casserole is the most underrated Thanksgiving side. Don’t get me wrong, I love classic stuffing and mashed potatoes, but for me, this is the dish that the holiday isn’t complete without. This tried-and-true recipe is my go-to; it's packed with sweet corn, balanced by a pinch of paprika and herby chives, and has an insanely tender and custardy texture that will have you on your third helping before you even know what hit you. Let this classic side be the star of your Thanksgiving dinner if you’re ever in the mood for endless praise and compliments.

What People Are Saying:“Very delicious, made it exactly like written, came out perfectly.” - mgnpanossia4246

After greasing your baking dish (very well) with softened butter and preheating the oven, break out that box of Jiffy mix. Whisk the mix with granulated sugar in a bowl until very few lumps remain.

Then mix—in a medium bowl or large measuring cup—your eggs, milk, cream, and melted butter until combined. Add that bowl into your Jiffy mix bowl, and stir until just combined. You don’t have to overmix here. Stir in your (thawed!) corn, creamed corn, salt, paprika, and pepper.

Pour your mixture into your prepared baking dish, using a spatula to smooth out the top. Bake until the casserole has fully set and the top is golden brown. This should take anywhere from 40 to 50 minutes.

Once done, let it cool slightly before serving, and top with fresh chives.

The full ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Since only 10 minutes of prep go into this corn casserole, you’ll have plenty of time to pull together the rest of dinner as it bakes in the oven. Try this with simple air-fryer chicken thighs or a hearty harvest chicken skillet for a comforting weeknight dinner, or add it to your holiday sides of mashed potato casserole, sweet potato casserole, and butternut squash mac & cheese alongside your roast turkey.

If you want to make your holiday easier, then this corn casserole is a perfect make-ahead Thanksgiving recipe. Simply prepare the casserole, then cover it tightly and store it in the fridge for up to 48 hours. Remove and bake once you're ready to serve, keeping in mind that it may take slightly longer to bake coming straight from the fridge.

If you somehow manage to have any leftovers, tightly cover your casserole dish or store it in an airtight container in the fridge for 4-5 days.

#6678. Latin Grammy Winner Urias Stars in CARRANCA, a Film by ODE

CARRANCA draws on the eponymous sculptures, anthropomorphic and zoomorphic figures traditionally believed to ward off evil spirits, also associated with Afro-Brazilian religious traditions such as Candomblé. The project references Exu, the orisha who mediates between the material and spiritual worlds and is associated with movement and the creation of possibilities. Inspired by ancient Egyptian and Yoruba cultures, the carrancas’ physiognomy blends human and animal traits, evoking pre-dynastic Egyptian deities.

The palette of black, red, indigo, and white references these traditions and Brazilian popular representations of Exu. Using Afro-surrealism as a conceptual framework, the series visually narrates Urias’ eponymous album, with each photograph corresponding to a track. The project explores transcorporeality and how reclaiming divine and ancestral figures can become a means of liberation from Eurocentric identity norms.

Creative Director, Art Director and Stylist: OdePhotography: MaltoniSet Design: Jean LabancaHair and Makeup: Ivan BarriaUrias’ Hair: Rafaella SchlinUrias’ Makeup: Camila AnacNail Artist: CyshimiProduction: BELTRAME+Post-Production: Studio Bruno RezendeCast: Fina Cadjancu, Akin Henrias and Marcinha do Corintho

Ode (b. 1998) is a Brazilian multidisciplinary artist working across filmmaking, creative direction, styling, writing, and curatorial practice. Her work explores self-representation as a means of challenging Eurocentric notions of identity through transcorporeality, Afro-Brazilian spirituality, memory, and transformation. Her photobooks and films, including A Rose and A Prayer (2022), Divina (2022), and CARRANCA (2025), bring human figures, deities, and Afro-surrealist narratives into dialogue. Her work has been exhibited at MEP, Instituto Moreira Salles, Lafayette Anticipations, ICA London, Photo London, and SP-Arte. She founded the platform Dúdús (2016–2019) and has curated exhibitions dedicated to Afro-diasporic art. Her work has been featured in Vogue, Dazed, ArtReview, and Harper’s Bazaar, and she has collaborated with Comme des Garçons, Jean Paul Gaultier, and COLORSxSTUDIOS.

#6676. What a 43-Year-Old Server and Drag Queen Eats on $63K CAD in Toronto, Ontario

Welcome to The Receipt, a series documenting how Bon Appétit readers eat and what they spend doing it. Each food diary follows one anonymous reader’s week of expenses related to groceries, restaurant meals, coffee runs, and every bite in between. In this time of rising food costs, The Receipt reveals how folks—from different cities, with different incomes, on different schedules—are figuring out their food budgets.

In today's Receipt, we follow a 43-year-old server and drag queen in Toronto, Ontario. Keep reading for his receipts.

The financesThe dietThe expensesThe diary

What is your occupation? Server, Drag Queen

What city and state do you live in? Toronto, ON

What is your annual salary, if you have one? Girl, I have no idea. Waiting tables brings in around $60K CAN, and performing in bars, nightclubs and drag brunches probably makes me another $3K, but all those sweat-and-glitter-covered bills get stuffed under a mattress, so who knows?

How much is one paycheck, after taxes? Let’s call it an even thousand dollars.

How often are you paid? (e.g., weekly) Weekly or biweekly

What are your approximate fixed monthly expenses beyond food? (i.e., rent, subscriptions, bills)

Do you follow a certain diet or have dietary restrictions? I avoid salt and gluten as much as possible

What are the grocery staples you always buy, if any? Eggs, gluten-free pasta, bananas, bone broth, greens, yogurt. I almost always have boiled eggs ready in the fridge, a tub of yogurt, some dark berries, and bananas to keep me fueled up. If I’m going to cook, it’s pasta or meat and veggies.

How often in a week do you dine out versus cook at home? I work in an upscale Italian restaurant and the food is exceptional. I eat there three or four times a week and have the cholesterol levels to prove it. At 50% off any menu items (minus the $128 porterhouse steak) it’s a bargain to eat delicious, mostly healthy cooked meals. I love going out socially or alone and do so almost weekly.

How often in a week did you dine out while growing up? Once a month? Rural Alberta didn’t have a ton of dining-out choices

How often in a week did your parents or guardians cook at home? Daily. My mom’s (and occasionally my dad’s) cooking was excellent. Nothing fancy, but a real meat-and-potatoes type menu that kept us going

10 a.m. My annual sinus infection came early, and I’ve been sick since Saturday. I’m trying to break my morning caffeine habit, but today I’m too lazy and run down. I hydrate with lemon water while sipping an Americano with brown sugar. Between the coffee and the illness I don’t have much of an appetite. I forage on peanut butter and pumpkin seeds while shuffling around my apartment.

4 p.m. A thought occurs to me: Let’s heal this body with some miso and wasabi. I order sushi from Kibo down the street. It’s takeout, so I am forced to don clothing and walk down the street. I pick up 2-for-1 dynamite rolls, chicken gyoza dumplings, miso soup, the obligatory green salad that comes with it all. and a spicy salmon combo. $44

As I’m feeling sorry for myself on my walk back home, I stop at the corner store for a bottle of Blake Boulevard Sauvignon Blanc. $21

10 a.m. I know that I have a shift at the restaurant today, so I’m holding my head up and pretending that I'm not at death's door. A banana-and-blueberry yogurt provide the gas I need to leave the house to get provisions.

We’re out of coffee beans! I have two espresso machines because I am a complete coffee snob. Machine number one, my favorite, is a De’Longhi manual espresso maker, a tall, silver tower with a handle on the side. I grind my own beans for this. Machine number two is a Nespresso Virtuo. It requires aluminum pods stuffed with coffee. It’s sleek and makes a lovely crema, but it’s never hot enough for me. The pods vary between $1.12 and $1.50 each.

On the way home I stop in at St. Clair Avenue Fruit Market for some of my usuals: bok choy, celery, dates, rapini, bananas, and a container of feta, for fun. $31.75

3 p.m. Before work I load up on cold meds even though they make me feel like a bobblehead. Somewhere between 4 p.m. and 7 p.m. I ask my chef to sneak me some arancini to avoid passing out while bringing some nice folks their wine and dinner. Our arancini are filled with mozzarella, rolled in a saffron risotto, and plated with tomato sauce. Retail $24, but for me it’s $12.

10 a.m. Americano, brown sugar, lemon water. I have a friend coming in from out of town and she is fully aware how ill I am, and I feel as though she may need a snack after her two-plus-hour drive, so I pop down to another cute hot spot on my street, Le Bleu. I may or may not also have a huge crush on the owner, Iman. I pick up vegan baklava, vegan banana bread, a caprese sandwich for her and prosciutto for me. I try to make small talk with Iman while he packs up my order, but he’s so handsome I end up muttering and mumbling my thank-yous while I stumble out the door. $25.76

6 p.m. Reservations for two at a vegetarian restaurant, Planta Yorkville. I am not a vegetarian. I had mid to low expectations. Guys, this place was incredible.

Delicious regular and zero-proof cocktails. A Cosmic Colada for me: coconut water, lemon, blueberry, and pineapple. For my friend: the Tokyo Blossom made with sake, gin, lychee liquor, and grapefruit tonic.

The unagi eggplant was so good I almost ordered a second one; we also ordered spicy tuna roll (some kind of juiced-up watermelon substitution), miso soup, and bang-bang broccoli. I ordered a chocolate brownie and a sticky toffee pudding for dessert—the latter was so big it was almost comical. They also stuffed an entire ice cream cone full of vanilla ice cream upside down onto my pudding. I finished about a quarter of it.

After she leaves and the day progresses I realize I am in no shape to work. Thankfully someone takes my serving shift so I don’t have to go and cough all over our lovely patrons.

6 p.m. My appetite has been suffering, but eventually I whip up one of my favorites: a bean medley, chickpeas, celery, olive oil, and feta salad.

8 p.m. As the sun sets and the self pity sinks in deeper, I throw a trench coat over my pajamas and scurry down to the store to get another favorite, a bottle of Te Henga Sauvignon Blanc. $19.45

10 a.m. Another Americano and lemon water.

1 p.m. At this point in the week, I am so sick of tea I never want to see it again. I’ve been trying to stave off whatever I have with honey/lemon/ginger tea, and I’m over it. That was lunch, I guess.

8 p.m. Guilty pleasure time! I call in an order for Little Caesars Chicago Deep Dish Meat Treat pizza, an order of hot wings, and a ginger ale. Pure class and total nutrition. The good news is that while attempting to remain unseen, I happen to run into someone I’ve been chatting with on a dating app. Oh, did I say good? I meant horrific. $39.77

10 a.m. Americano. Lemon Water. I’m a creature of habit, okay?

12 p.m. Bananas, blueberries, leftover bean salad, ginger tea.

3 p.m. I’m at work early, because we have new menu items. At pre-shift, the chef makes one of each and we discuss and sample it. A prime rib–chuck burger with scamorza and tomato bomba, lamb pizza with basil pesto, steak tartare, Atlantic cod and potato risotto, and mushroom-and-pea-stuffed ravioli. Some call this “product knowledge.” I call it a free meal(s).

8:45 p.m. Despite all my newfound product knowledge, I still get hungry halfway through my shift. Chef makes me a bulgur, kale, and roasted beet salad with a 7-oz. flatiron steak. $22

10:45 p.m. Shift done, head feeling like a block of ice, I sneak off to the market before heading home to grab a bottle of Squealing Pig Sauvignon Blanc. $22

10 a.m. Pretty much right after I open my eyes, I get a lovely text from a friend; “Wanna get breakfast?” She knows I’ve been sick and we haven’t caught up in a while. She is informed that I will be wearing my now-uniform of pajamas and trench coat.

11 a.m. Three eggs, toast, and bacon from a pub by my house. No culinary awards will be given out here, but it did what it was supposed to do. The bill for both meals with coffee and a couple of spicy Bloody Caesars came to $50.

7 p.m. Another go-to when I’m feeling down or simply have the craving is to simmer some rice noodles in a broth and add miso paste. I stir in fresh chopped bok choy, green onions, and a couple of cut-up hard-boiled eggs. I think I’m gonna make it.

#6672. Vogue First! Dagmar’s Pre-Spring Collection Lands Early on Net-a-Porter

Opening a boutique is a delicate exercise in materials and mood to bring its world to life in a physical space. So it makes sense that for Karin Soderlind, Kristina Tjader, and Sofia Wallenstam, the sisters and founders behind Stockholm-based Dagmar, their Alyssa Kapito-designed flagship became a strong point of reference. When Wallenstam, the brand’s creative director, went looking for inspiration for pre-spring ‘27, the debut collection from new design director Rachael Proud, formerly creative director of Raey, the two didn’t need to look further than the walls of its new shop.

“When Rachel and I started to look at what the collection should be, we looked at our store,” Wallenstam shares with Vogue. In the flagship, a metal Jean Prouvé piece hangs on the wall alongside a handcrafted André Sornay sofa, and the tension between the two became the collection’s throughline. “The Prouvé piece on the wall is metal, quite hard, almost brutalist, and then we have this beautiful soft boucle sofa,” she says. “That was the meeting point, the hard versus the soft.”

Launching November 19 alongside a new brand identity, the 130-piece pre-spring collection spans relaxed denim (a favorite of Wallenstam’s), sharp-shouldered tailoring, a croc pencil skirt, open-cut tanks, suede outerwear, and boots, much of it drawing on the textures and tones of the store. “We wanted to reuse the bouclé, not in the same way obviously, but that feeling of it,” Wallenstam says. The colors, too—the blacks, whites, and browns—feel representative of the Dagmar woman, Wallenstam adds.

The collection also marks a new chapter for the Dagmar woman, who Wallenstam describes as confident and living a busy, bustling life with a packed calendar. “She might go and have a school drop-off and go straight to a meeting, and then meet friends, go to a dinner,” she shares. “She wants to look smart and she wants to look cool at the same time.” With Proud on board, that approach is getting a little more edge, with roomier silhouettes and sharper shoulders at the forefront. It’s a bit more oversized than Dagmar has done in the past, with warmer tones integrated throughout. Wallenstam also pushes back on the notion of minimalism, so often pinned on Scandinavian fashion. “Of course, we have neutral tones, but it has heritage. It has a depth to it and a warmth to it.”

As for how she and Proud approached the collection together, “When Rachel and I first sat down to discuss what the collection should be and how it should feel, we started immediately with the Dagmar woman and what’s important to her,” Wallenstam says. And the pencil skirt is where Proud’s influence really shows. “You can really see the meeting point between Rachel and Dagmar in the pencil skirt,” she says. “It’s a very feminine product, but it has an elastic waist to make it more relaxed, and it’s in croco, which is a little bit harder in the material.” It’s the piece in the collection that will feel as comfortable at your desk as it does at dinner—a full expression of the new Dagmar approach. It’s the Prouvé and the bouclé sofa all over again, translated into a single skirt. And of course, the same push and pull runs through the rest of the collection.

Prices range from $140 to $3,300, and Vogue readers can exclusively shop the collection on Dagmar for 24 hours, before its official launch on November 20; retailer drops on Net-a-Porter and Nordstrom to follow.

#6670. The Key To The Perfect Pie Crust Is In These 5 Simple Rules

Admit it, a pie is only as good as its crust. No matter how good the filling is, a flaky, buttery crust is what separates the good from the great. But how to achieve the pie crust of your (and your guests’) dreams? The master of all things baking, Senior Food Editor Makinze Gore, is here to explain her five golden rules of pie crust—just in time for the holidays.

A buttery, flaky pie crust is the stuff of dreams; to achieve this, Gore says it’s essential to use cold butter. The same goes for many flaky pastries and baked goods, as the steam that cold butter releases as it melts creates those layers inside the crust. If you use butter that isn’t cold, it will melt too quickly—it can even melt just during the process of rolling the dough out if it's warm enough, which won’t produce any steam. Warm butter will leave you with a dense, mealy crust.

The size of your butter chunks will affect the end product. Gore says larger and smaller chunks can both work, but larger chunks leave a flakier crust, while smaller chunks let the crust hold more intricate designs. Typically, you want your chunks to be at least pea-sized to form the steam pockets required to create flaky layers.

Sensing a theme here? Gore says it’s vitally important to chill your dough at several points throughout the process. This is because you need to keep the butter cold, as it will warm up as you work with it. Gore recommends chilling your crust after you first make it, after rolling it out, before crimping it, and once more before baking it. This will help keep the butter cold and prevent the crust from shrinking as it bakes.

One great trick to ensure an even bake is to use a metal pie tin. Metal conducts heat better than glass, so it bakes the crust more evenly, though glass does have some benefits. Baking pie crust in a glass pie dish lets you see the bottom of the crust and know exactly when it’s perfectly cooked.

Another tip Gore says will help bake your crust more evenly is to set a baking sheet in the oven as it preheats, then to place the pie tin on top of the hot baking sheet as it cooks. This extra heat helps the crust cook slightly faster, giving it more time to set before the liquid filling seeps in.

#6669. 5 Cozy Meals Our Editors Make With Boxed Stuffing

Each month in Well Stocked, our staff shares unfussy recipe ideas that you can call upon any day of the week. Because yes, our editors love to cook, but sometimes we need to get dinner on the table 12 minutes ago. That’s when we look to pantry and fridge staples that can quickly morph into delicious meals. This month, it’s all about boxed stuffing.

Few Thanksgiving dishes inspire as many strong opinions as stuffing-slash-dressing. In a baking dish or the bird itself? Sourdough or white bread? Fresh herbs or dried? Sausage or nah? There are the purists, who insist on drying their own bread, chopping aromatics, and seasoning everything from scratch. Then there are the Stove Top loyalists, who grew up on that unmistakable red box and remain fiercely faithful. Site director Rachel Tepper Paley is firmly in the latter camp. “My late father considered boxed Stove Top the Platonic ideal of stuffing, so I grew up with the uber-savory stuff on every Thanksgiving table,” she says. “We continue to serve it every year in his honor.”

And then there’s a particularly resourceful subset of boxed-stuffing devotees: the maximalists who see the box as a pantry staple, not just a holiday shortcut. Our editors happen to be among them. Read on for all the ways they’re thinking outside the box.

The first time I tried my colleague Jesse Szewczyk’s Schmaltzy Chicken and Stuffing Skillet in the Test Kitchen, I immediately started plotting all the occasions I could make it: Friendsgiving, a random Tuesday, football Sunday, date night. Its deliciousness caught me off guard. Jesse sears chicken thighs until golden and crisp, then uses the schmaltzy drippings (a.k.a. liquid gold) to season the boxed stuffing. Dotted with sweet-tart cranberry gems, then baked with the chicken nestled back in, it’s basically Thanksgiving in one pan—and a recipe I plan to make well beyond November. —Nina Moskowitz, associate editor, cooking

When I find myself with instant stuffing, I like to make a baked eggs situation. I’ll make the stuffing according to the box’s directions in a shallow oven-proof skillet. With the heat on (so the stuffing continues to crisp), I’ll toss in some greens like kale or spinach until they’re just barely wilted. Then I’ll use a spatula to make a couple divots throughout and crack an egg into each. From there, I’ll pop the skillet into a 375° oven and bake until the whites are set, about 15 minutes. A drizzle of green hot sauce and some salty feta crumble or sliced avocado, and I’m ready to eat. —Rebecca Firkser, Test Kitchen editor

The way I see it, boxed stuffing mix is really close to a box of well-seasoned breadcrumbs. So why not use it as a savory panade that transforms lean ground turkey into tender meatballs? Senior Test Kitchen editor Jesse Szewczyk called this sheet-pan dinner Turkey and Stuffing Meatballs, but it honestly delivers all the flavors of Thanksgiving in one simple meal. You’ve got creamy baby Yukon gold potatoes and snappy green beans, plus a dipping sauce made from another autumn pantry staple, canned cranberry sauce, with lemon and honey to serve alongside. —Kelsey Jane Youngman, senior cooking editor

As a person who works in food media, I end up with my fair share of “what can I do with this?” ingredients. So while I firmly believe that no Thanksgiving dressing matters as much as my classic cornbread-and-biscuit version, I am no stranger to boxed stuffing. Frequently, it becomes a crust for baked fish. I’ve used Anna Stockwell’s easy no-recipe-required crispy fish method dozens of times since it first published, and it never disappoints. Best of all, you can use whatever bits and bobs you have lying around to build out the meal, and it’s relatively hands-off. Wins all around. —Joe Sevier, senior editor, SEO & cooking

For cozy season, there is nothing more comforting than carbs on carbs. I’ve always loved pasta with breadcrumbs (every which way), but leave it to senior Test Kitchen editor Jesse Szewczyk to make that classic combo even better: In this recipe, instead of using plain breadcrumbs or Italian seasoned ones, he swaps in stuffing mix blitzed in the food processor. Brilliant. It’s a highly flavorful topping for pasta with sausage and kale, a total delight for a cold night. —Emma Laperruque, cooking director

#6666. At MoMA PS1, a New Exhibition on Latin American Fashion Asks: Who Gets to Define Style?

Willy Chavarria and Raul Lopez of Luar are among the designers participating in “Malvestir,” a major new exhibition at MoMA PS1 that examines the relationship between Latin American fashion and contemporary art. Announced today and opening April 22, 2027, the exhibition will feature work by 20 artists and designers from across the Americas, bringing together clothing, photography, sculpture, and textiles to explore how dress reflects questions of cultural identity, class, and power.

For Chavarria and Lopez, whose respective designs draw on their Chicano and Dominican American backgrounds, these conversations have long been part of their work. Both have built internationally recognized labels that incorporate the aesthetics and experiences of communities historically underrepresented within luxury fashion.

“Being part of this exhibition at MoMA PS1 is deeply meaningful to me,” Chavarria tells Vogue. “It’s an opportunity to share a piece of who I am, where I come from, and the culture that has shaped me. I’m proud to be part of a moment that gives space to Latino voices and celebrates the beauty, creativity, and richness of our culture.”

Josh Tafoya. Bad Hombre. 2026. Hand-woven Serape-style cape in Churro wool. Multi panels with Rio Grande, Chimayo, and Vallero styles. Hand-sewn with natural-dyed wool warp. With spotted black and white hair on hide chaps. Hand-cut fringe. Courtesy of the artist.

Lopez, meanwhile, has built Luar around his experiences growing up in New York, drawing on Dominican culture, queer nightlife, and the city’s ever-shifting approach to getting dressed. “I grew up in New York in a Dominican family, around people who could make a whole look out of whatever they had,” Lopez tells Vogue. “The fashion industry has always pulled from communities like mine. With Luar, I’ve always wanted us to be the ones telling those stories. Being part of “Malvestir” at MoMA PS1 feels personal because I get to bring that point of view into the room alongside artists and designers with their own stories about what it means to get dressed across Latin America and the diaspora.”

The exhibition’s title offers some insight into its broader themes. Combining the Spanish words mal (“poorly”) and vestir (“to dress”), “Malvestir” references the colonial-era designation malvestidos, applied to those whose clothing did not conform to prescribed boundaries of race, caste, or gender. The term remains pejorative but can also express a rejection of established conventions of taste and social class. The exhibition considers these histories alongside the ways artists and designers use clothing to express identity today.

Leonardo Choxom. Performance, from the series Piezas Experimentales, 2023–2024. Textile. Courtesy of the artist. © Leonardo Choxom

José Ballivian. La virgen viste de Balenciaga. 2024. Pencil on paper. 100 x 70 cm. Courtesy of the artist

Organized by MoMA PS1 director Connie Butler and associate curator Elena Ketelsen González, “Malvestir” will bring together artists working across a wide range of disciplines. Colombian photographer Camila Falquez’s ongoing series Syncretism, for example, features portraits of queer and trans people from Latin America and the Caribbean, drawing on Indigenous and African spiritual traditions. Mexican designer Carla Fernández, known for her collaborations with Indigenous artisans, will also participate, alongside Bolivian artist José Ballivián, whose drawings place familiar luxury and sportswear logos within traditional Andean imagery.

Other works address the global movement of clothing and its environmental and economic consequences. Guatemalan artist David Ramírez Cotón draws on his family’s experience running a secondhand clothing business in Guatemala City, while Venezuelan artist Cassandra Mayela uses garments collected from Venezuelan migrants in her large-scale woven works. The Afro-Colombian collective Las Tejedoras de Mampuján will contribute a newly commissioned tapestry depicting the reuse and recycling of textiles within its workshop. The influence of Western luxury and beauty advertising is also examined: Costa Rican artist La Cholla Jackson, for instance, reimagines a Dior campaign from the 2000s, posing with accessories constructed from discarded materials.

Barbara Sánchez-Kane. Lessons in Draping. 2023, rawhide, metal, wood. 325 × 92 × 84 cm. Image courtesy of the artist and Kurimanzutto, Mexico City / New York. © Bárbara Sánchez-Kane. Photo: Da Brandica

Patrica Belli. Mamá (detail). 2021. Clothing, rope, buckles. 194 x 335 x 172 cm. Courtesy of the artist and Parallel Oaxaca.

“‘Malvestir’ reimagines how fashion can be displayed in museums, reflecting the very alive possibilities and contradictions inherent to the form,” Ketelsen González tells Vogue. “The show offers a provocative, materially rich look at 20 artists and designers across the Americas who are in dialogue with one another yet have never been brought together in an exhibition until now.”

“Malvestir” arrives amid a wider cultural interest in fashion as a means of exploring identity, heritage, and power. Following the Metropolitan Museum of Art’s 2025 exhibition “Superfine: Tailoring Black Style,” which examined the history of Black dandyism and its relationship to identity across the Atlantic diaspora, the MoMA PS1 show continues a broader conversation about the cultural and political significance of dress. It also builds on MoMA PS1’s own history of presenting fashion alongside contemporary art, dating back to its experimental fashion programming in the early 1980s.

Installation View: Se abrió paca, David Ramírez Cotón. All pieces acrylic on canvas, variable measurements, 2024. Curated by Josseline Pinto at La Galería Rebelde, Guatemala City. Photo: courtesy of La Galería Rebelde.

Willy Chavarria. Safe from Harm (still), 2024. Courtesy of the artist and MATTE. © Willy Chavarria

“‘Malvestir’ addresses urgent, shared concerns across fashion, contemporary art, and visual culture, engaging daring artists and designers both in New York and across the Americas,” Butler says. “MoMA PS1 has a history of presenting innovative designers alongside contemporary artists. We’re thrilled that audiences will be able to experience the formal experimentation, political imagination, and cultural contradictions at the heart of ‘Malvestir.’”

“Malvestir” will be on view at MoMA PS1 from April 22 through September 6, 2027.

#6665. Beer Cheese Stuffing

Thanksgiving is equally about food and football in my house, so this tailgate-inspired stuffing was a no-brainer creation for me. Filled with beer cheese sauce and topped with crispy bacon, this twist on the classic holiday side is so irresistible, it’ll be gone long before halftime of that first Thanksgiving Day game.

How we’re making the beer cheese:A high-quality cheddar and flavorful beer are both key to making beer cheese sing. Make sure to choose a beer you love, since you’ll definitely taste it in the finished stuffing. I recommend a lager or an amber ale, since hoppier beers like IPAs can be too assertive in the larger context of a stuffing. When it comes to cheese, I love extra-sharp cheddar here, but you could also try a milder cheddar if you like.

To achieve a glossy, silky texture, this beer cheese harnesses the dual powers of cornstarch and evaporated milk. Together with the lager, they form a smooth, stable base that helps keep the sauce smooth and emulsified, even when it’s heated to bubbling in the oven.

Make ahead & storage:I don’t recommend assembling this stuffing in advance, but if you want to work ahead, you can make the beer cheese up to 2 days before. Let it cool completely, then store it in an airtight container in the refrigerator. When you’re ready to assemble, reheat it slowly on low heat before layering it with the bread.

#6663. Herby Sheet-Pan Meatballs With Feta

These aren’t your slow-braised, leisurely Sunday-sauce meatballs. They’re let’s-get-this-crew-fed-at-the-end-of-a-busy-workday meatballs. The large amount of breadcrumbs isn’t just a filler; combined with the water, they make the meatballs impossibly tender and springy. You can use any ground meat you’d like; pork, lamb, turkey, or chicken (only dark meat for the poultry, though) all work well. Roasting the meatballs in the oven instead of frying them on the stovetop reduces the active cook time and makes cleanup simple.

More crowd-pleasing meatballs recipes here.

Place a rack in bottom third of oven; preheat to 425°. Using a sturdy wooden spoon, vigorously mix 2 large eggs, zest of 1 lemon, 2 garlic cloves, finely grated, 1 cup panko, ½ cup finely chopped dill and/or parsley, 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 2 tsp. dried oregano, 1 tsp. freshly ground pepper, 1 tsp. ground cumin, and ½ cup water in a large bowl until nearly a smooth paste. Add 1 lb. ground beef and mix vigorously until well combined and mixture is slightly tacky. Oil your hands; divide mixture into 16 portions and roll into balls. Arrange on a large rimmed baking sheet.

Toss 1 large red onion, cut through root end into 2"-thick wedges, 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil, a large pinch of salt, and a large pinch of pepper in a small bowl. Arrange in a single layer around meatballs. Bake meatballs and onions until meatballs are deeply browned underneath and an instant-read thermometer inserted into centers registers 160°, 25–30 minutes. Let cool slightly.

To serve, generously top with sliced cucumbers, crumbled feta, and more dill and/or parsley if desired. Serve with country-style bread and lemon wedges for squeezing over.

#6659. Cranberry Paloma

A paloma is a classic summer cocktail in my rotation, but I still want an excuse to enjoy it throughout the colder-weather months. Enter: the cranberry paloma. Just like a classic paloma, this version is made with a base of tequila and grapefruit. But it gets a boost of sweetness and tartness with the addition of cranberry juice cocktail. This sweetened cranberry-based beverage replaces the need for sugary sodas or simple syrup. Bonus: It adds a gorgeous red hue to your glass, perfect for the holiday season.

Reposado tequila:The type of tequila you use is ultimately up to you, but I recommend either reposado or blanco tequila. Reposado tequila is aged for up to a year, typically in oak barrels, and takes on a warm sweetness with notes of vanilla and caramel. It’s a great choice for cocktails (especially when grapefruit is involved) because the smoothness balances out the tart and bright citrus.

Blanco tequila:Blanco tequila, on the other hand, is for people who want to double down on the brightness. It’s barely aged, if aged at all, so it retains the sharp, vegetal flavor of the agave plant. This recipe works well with either option, so it all comes down to personal preference and what you already have on your bar cart.
